# Changelog

All notable changes to `precision-soft/melody/v3` will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/), and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- container: `WithReplacesContainerService()` admits a scoped registration whose name — or whose registered type — the container already claims, so a scoped service can deliberately shadow a container singleton inside a scope instead of being refused at boot. It admits substitution, not decoration: a scoped provider that resolves the name it replaces re-enters itself and is reported as the circular dependency it is. The wiring generator emits it for exactly the scoped-shadows-container registrations its scan finds
- cli: melody owns the flag and context layer a command is written against, and the flag-parsing engine is confined to one adapter source. `cli/contract.Context` is what a command's `Run` receives — `String`, `Bool`, `Int`, `StringSlice`, `IsSet`, `Arguments`, `Writer`, the seven readers the tree actually uses; `cli/contract.Flag` describes itself through `Definition() FlagDefinition`, with `FlagKind` naming the four kinds melody parses and `StringFlag`/`BoolFlag`/`IntFlag`/`StringSliceFlag` keeping their typed `Validator`; `cli.Root` is the command tree, built by `cli.NewRoot`, with `SetWriter`, `SetErrorWriter`, `Run` and `CommandNames`. Until now `clicontract.CommandContext` and the four flag types were **type aliases** of `github.com/urfave/cli/v3`, which put every field that engine's command struct has, mutable, into melody's public surface and made the engine's own API part of this major's compatibility promise — an engine release renaming a method would have reached every consumer without melody doing anything, and without melody being able to absorb it. See the `Changed` entry for what an upgrader does, and `UPGRADE.md` for the mechanical rewrite
- cli: `cli/contract.StaticContext` is a `Context` whose answers are given rather than parsed, for testing a command's body without a command line. It exists because taking the engine's command struct out of the contract took away the only way a caller had to build one; hand it the values a run would have produced and call the command's `Run` directly. A zero value answers the zero of every flag, no arguments, and a writer that discards
- cli: `cli.DispatchCommand(ctx, command, runtime, arguments, writer)` parses one command line against a command's own flags and runs it, with none of what the registration path adds around a command: no banner, no scope close, no exit handling. It is for a caller that runs a melody command inside a process it owns — the cron runner drives one per due entry, each on its own goroutine into its own captured output, where the banners of concurrent runs would interleave and a scope closed under the caller would end one it is still using. One engine behaviour travels with its `ctx` and is documented at the site: a command dispatched with a context descending from ANOTHER command's action inherits that command's flag set
- cli: `cli/contract.ErrFlagValueTypeMismatch` is the cause of a validation refusal when the value handed to a flag's neutral validator is not of the type its kind names — a wiring mistake in an adapter or a hand-written flag type, reported rather than swallowed by a validator that quietly passed everything it could not read

- container: `container.WithTeardownDependency(serviceNames ...string)` declares that a registration must be CLOSED BEFORE the services it names, without resolving them. The teardown graph is otherwise recorded from resolutions — a provider that resolves another service while it is being built has that dependency recorded at the moment of the resolution — so a provider that CAPTURES an already-built collaborator and simply hands it back declares nothing, and the order of the two closes is decided by creation order, which is not an ordering at all. Resolving stays the better door wherever it is possible, because an edge derived from the resolution cannot fall out of step with what the provider actually uses; this one is for the pre-built instance that has nothing to resolve. It orders teardown alone: it does not build the named service, does not make it exist, and takes no part in cycle detection, and an edge naming a service that was never created is dropped by the walk. An empty name and a service naming itself are refused where the registration is made, with `ErrTeardownDependencyNameIsRequired` and `ErrTeardownDependencyIsSelf`; `RegisterScoped` refuses it outright with `ErrScopedTeardownDependencyUnsupported`, because a scope keeps its own teardown graph, built per scope from the resolutions that scope actually made
- event: `event/contract.SubscriberRegistration` identifies one installation of a subscriber, and `AddSubscriber` answers it. It exists because the subscriber value cannot identify itself: a subscriber that carries no fields occupies no memory, and every zero-size allocation in Go answers one address, so two instances of such a type are one pointer and nothing in the value can ever tell them apart. See the `Changed` entry for what it replaces
- event: `event.RequiredListenerSkippedError` is the distinct type a dispatch returns when a listener ended it — by stopping propagation or by failing — while a listener marked required through `RequiredListenerRegistrar` was still behind it. The type is what lets a caller separate this class from an ordinary listener failure and refuse the dispatch outright, which is what the http kernel now does for `kernel.request` and `kernel.controller`. Assert it on the error a dispatch returns directly rather than reaching through the cause chain: a listener is free to dispatch further events, and one of those nested dispatches skipping a required listener of its own travels up as the cause of an ordinary listener error
- event: `event/contract.RegisteredListener` carries `Required` and `MaySkipRequiredListeners`, so an inspection can tell an armed fail-closed guarantee from an unarmed one — a listener that is supposed to be required looked exactly like one that is not, at the call site and at the audit alike. `debug:events --verbose` renders them as a column, and the json document carries them too
- logging: `logging/contract.LevelReporter` is the optional capability a `Logger` implements to say, before being handed anything, whether a record at a given level would survive its threshold, and `logging.LevelEnabled` is the single door onto it — a logger that does not implement it is reported enabled, so the reporting behaviour is the floor. It exists for the callers that BUILD what they log: the event dispatcher assembles a context map per dispatch and resolves a listener's name through reflection per listener per dispatch, and every one of those was discarded unread by a journal at error level, on the hottest path the framework has. The three loggers melody ships implement it
- debug: `debug.NewEventCommand` takes a declaration channel — `debug.DeferredListener` entries naming what the SERVING process wires and this process does not — so `debug:events` run from a console dispatcher answers "is access control wired?" with a declaration instead of with an absence that means "not in this process". The application feeds it the security pair, which stays http-only by design

- session: `NewInMemoryStorageWithClock`, `NewFileStorageFromPathWithClock` and `NewFileStorageFromFileWithClock` read every expiry instant — stored, compared, and the sweep's tick — from an injected clock, and the framework-wired default storage runs on the kernel's. Both storages read `time.Now()` directly, so session time was the one framework-supplied lifetime a frozen-clock test could not move; the monotonic-against-wall divergence between the two storages — the in-memory expiry survives an NTP step and never a restart, the file expiry survives a restart and follows every wall step — is now named in `SESSION.md` as the trade it is, instead of living unstated in the two comparison sites. The in-memory sweep also enumerates its ids under the read lock, the repair its cache twin received
- logging: `NewReopenableFileWriter` opens the journal behind a writer that can be told to open its path again, and the container-built logger arms it on `SIGHUP`. The file journal was one descriptor for the life of the process with no reopen door anywhere: a logrotate in its default rename-and-create mode moved the journal out from under the process, every later record landed in the rotated file — or, once that was compressed or deleted, in an unlinked inode only `lsof` could still see — and nothing errored, because writes into a renamed or deleted-but-open inode keep succeeding. The conventional `postrotate` `SIGHUP` now points the journal at the fresh file; a reopen that fails keeps the current descriptor writing and reports once on stderr, and `Close` disarms the watcher before surrendering the descriptor, so a torn-down application leaves no watcher armed. The frozen majors keep the single-descriptor shape and prescribe `copytruncate`, which remains valid here too
- logging: `NewJsonLoggerWithClock` stamps every record from an injected clock, and the container-built logger reads the kernel's. The journal was the one framework-supplied timestamp no test could freeze and no deployment could redirect: every record read `time.Now()` directly while the rest of the process answered the injected clock, so the journal's instants and the application's instants came from two sources that a frozen-clock test showed disagreeing. The two constructors without a clock keep the process clock, because they also build the last-resort loggers — stderr, and the exit path of a dying process — where no injected clock exists to be wrong
- internal: `CopyAnyValue` copies one value at the depth `CopyAnyMap` copies a whole map, so a caller holding a single `any` no longer has to wrap it in a map to get the same documented boundary — maps and slices descended into, everything else returned as-is.
- http: `ServerSentEventHub.SetLogger` gives the hub the journal it never had. A backplane whose publish failed and a subscriber whose buffer overflowed were both counted into a private atomic nothing reads, so a redis outage silenced cross-node delivery on every node while each node kept serving its own subscribers and no record above nothing at all was filed anywhere. A publish failure is recorded at error; a subscriber's FIRST drop is recorded at warning and its later ones are not, because a consumer that has stopped reading drops every event from then on and a record per drop would bury the journal under one fault
- http: `ServerSentEventHub.Close` is `Shutdown` under the one name the framework's teardown recognises. The container closes a service by asserting `Close() error` on it, so a hub named only `Shutdown` was the single component in the framework its own ordered teardown could not see — it was skipped in silence, and the only thing that stopped it was a composition root remembering an http shutdown hook by hand, which an application running as a worker or a cli command never reaches
- http: `ServerSentEventHub.IsClosed` answers the difference a caller's range cannot see. `Subscribe` on a shut-down hub hands back a subscriber whose channel is already closed, which reads exactly like an ordinary end of stream: during the drain window a fresh connection was answered with a successful, instantly empty event stream and neither the client nor the journal learned why. `ServerSentEventSubscriber.Topic` reports the topic a subscriber holds
- http: `FilterRouteManifestByZone` narrows a manifest to one zone. The gate existed only inside the cli command, so an application projecting the manifest in-process — into a page, into a bundle — had no way to apply it and the zone travelled as a label on an artifact that carried every zone to every consumer, the anonymous ones included
- http: `RouteZones` and `IsRouteZone` name the declared zones, so a command that takes a zone can list the accepted spellings in its refusal instead of restating them
- http: `RouteManifestEntry` carries `host`, `schemes`, `locales` and `priority`. Three of the seven fields the matcher discriminates on were absent from the artifact, and each absence produced the same outcome: the frontend minted a relative path against the current origin, the current scheme and no locale, and the router refused what it had just been told to build. A route bound to another host was advertised to a page served from this one; a route restricted to https was advertised to a page served over http; and a route restricted to a locale set was advertised with nothing saying which locales it accepts — or, when it declared locales and carried no locale segment at all, advertised while unreachable by any url whatsoever

- validation: `IsRuleWiringErrorCode` reports whether a validation code names a mistake in the DECLARATION rather than in the value — a rule the registry does not know, a tag the parser cannot read, a pattern that does not compile — none of which any client input can produce; `ValidationErrors.HasRuleWiringError` asks it over a collection, and `ValidationErrors.WithoutRuleWiringContext` projects the collection onto what the client may see, stripping the internal context of the entries that blame the declaration while every other entry keeps the bounds and lengths a client needs to correct its request
- validation: `ValidationErrors` implements `json.Marshaler`, rendering the collection as the array it is so a log record carrying it says the same thing the http response body says — the flattened `Error()` string was the only form the log ever saw, and the per-field structure survived in one place and not the other
- exception: `PanicCause` reads a recovered panic value as the cause of the error a recovery boundary fabricates in its place; a typed nil answers no cause, because its `Error()` would dereference a nil receiver at the first render
- example: two opt-in wirings join the example's switches, each unwired on the empty value like every other door. `APP_CORS_ALLOW_ORIGINS` arms the cors listeners — the preflight answered ahead of token resolution, which the middleware form cannot do — and `APP_REQUEST_BUDGET_PER_HOUR` arms the kernel.request rate limit listener, which charges every request of one client address against a shared hourly budget before authentication runs, the refused ones included

- tooling: `.dev/validate/documentation.sh` checks the package documents against the code of every major, and now runs in the staged mode as well as the full one; it reads both bullet characters the documents use, every symbol a list entry names rather than only the first, methods as well as top-level declarations, the members of grouped `const`/`var` blocks, and packages at any depth, so a symbol documented for one major and shipped here can no longer go undocumented here

- security: `JwtConfig.RevocationEpochSkew` widens a revocation boundary by the worst clock skew between the node that mints and the node that revokes, and covers the imprecision of `iat` itself. `iat` is a whole-second numeric date, and nothing in the token says whether its issuer truncated or rounded up, so the instant a token claims can sit up to a second either side of when it was really minted: a token minted just before a boundary can read as minted after it. The allowance is where that is paid for, rather than in a hidden default — guessing the issuer's rounding would refuse tokens legitimately minted after a revocation. It defaults to zero; set it to at least a second on the json web token path, plus whatever the fleet's clock skew is
- security: a revocation stops being an enumeration. `security/contract.RevocationEpochStore` declares `RevokeBefore(userIdentifier, deviceIdentifier, instant)` and `RevocationEpoch(...)`, and `EpochRevocableTokenStore` composes it with `RevocableTokenStore`; both stores melody ships satisfy it. `DeleteByUser` walks an index with `SSCAN`, which does not promise to return a member added while the walk is in progress, so a token issued to a user after the walk passed the bucket it lands in survived the revocation — the window an account compromise is most likely to be exploited in. A boundary is one write instead: every later lookup refuses a token stamped before it, so a token issued during the revocation is refused by the same comparison as one issued an hour earlier, without anything having to find it first. A boundary can be published for a user or for one device of a user, and the one applied is the later of the two, so a user-wide revocation cannot be undone by a device-scoped one.
  `DeleteByUser` keeps its place as cleanup, and its documentation now says so rather than promising a guarantee it cannot give. **Breaking** is not needed: the epoch methods live on a new interface rather than on `RevocableTokenStore`, so a store written against an earlier release still compiles
- security: `security/contract.Claims` carries `IssuedAt` and `DeviceIdentifier`. The issue instant is what a revocation boundary is compared against and is stamped by the store on every write, ignoring any value the caller passed — claims read back through `Lookup` and put again would otherwise carry the original instant forward, so a token refreshed after a revocation would be born already refused. **Behavioural change**: a token written by an earlier release carries no issue instant, and the zero instant precedes every boundary, so such a token is refused as soon as a boundary exists for its user. Nothing else changes for it, and the cost falls only on the accounts somebody actually revoked
- security: `NewJwtTokenValidatorWithRevocationEpoch` makes a JSON web token revocable, which it has never been: the validator already parsed and verified `iat` and then discarded it, because `Claims` had nowhere to put it, so a signed and unexpired token could not be withdrawn by anything. It now carries the instant, reads a device from the claim named by `JwtConfig.DeviceClaim` when one is configured, and refuses a token issued before the boundary published for its subject. The wiring is opt-in because it makes verification stateful — one store read per request, no cache, and every token on that firewall refused while the store cannot answer; a deployment that cannot pay that keeps `NewJwtTokenValidator` and gives up revocation. The constructor forces `RejectFutureIssuedAt` on regardless of the configuration, since a token stamped ahead of the verifying process's clock would otherwise sit after every boundary ever published and outlive every revocation, silently

- example: the development stack serves all three example applications at once, each under a name that says which it is — `v1-example.`, `v2-example.` and `example.melody.localhost.precision-soft.com`. They are the same image with a different example directory, so nothing about the supervision is per-major, and none of them publishes a host port: they are reached through the load balancer, which keeps the per-developer host-port map out of it entirely. An end-to-end section tells them apart where a status code cannot — all three answer the same routes, and v1 and v2 are the same application on different majors — by writing through each vhost and reading which catalogue table the row landed in
- example: the example application is a working nomenclature rather than a set of routes that exist to be driven. Products, categories, currencies and users are kept through bunorm when the environment configured a database and in memory when it did not, so the application still boots on a machine with no containers; the listing is served from redis and dropped when a product changes; every write leaves a journal entry naming whoever made it; the write endpoints sit behind a per-address budget while the reads stay open; and the catalogue reading is refreshed on a schedule instead of being computed inside the request that finds a cold cache. The routes that only demonstrated a backend — `/integration/cache/`, `/integration/database/`, `/integration/ratelimit/` — are gone, and what they proved is now proven against the nomenclature itself, half of it read out of band by the end-to-end harness straight from redis and mysql
- example: every redis key and every table the example writes carries its major. The three example applications share one redis and one mysql in development, the cache holds gob-encoded entities whose wire format is keyed on the fully qualified type name — which carries the major — and the rate-limit counter is asserted on exactly by the end-to-end harness. Both example applications had been writing under the same prefix, so with the two of them running at once one would read entries it cannot decode and spend the other's budget; the comment above the constants had said so while the code did the opposite
- example: the schema is owned by one migration set in `v3/.example/migration/` — six mysql migrations, one per table: the four catalogue tables, the journal, and the two-factor enrollment table neither frozen major carries. Each repository used to create the table it read, and the enrollment table was created inline by the composition root, so the schema was spelled once per reader and nothing could roll it back, inspect it or report what had been applied. Two doors now run the same set: `migration.EnsureMigrated`, called at boot and again by every repository constructor before it seeds, and the `db:*` command family (`db:init`, `db:migrate`, `db:rollback`, `db:status`, `db:unlock`, `db:create`) the `bunorm/migrate` module registers. The set is applied under bun's migration lock with a bounded retry that names `db:unlock` when it gives up, and every statement is tolerant, because several processes of the example reach an empty volume at once. The framework's own tables are not in it: the outbox store and the audit registry each open their schema through the module that owns it
- example: the packages melody:wiring:generate scans expose exactly one constructor per service — the one that picks the implementation the environment can support. The concrete implementations are unexported so the generator neither registers them beside the chooser, which the container refuses as a duplicate type registration, nor reports them as skipped; the database handle they choose on lives outside the scanned packages, because a constructor asking for a `*bun.DB` by type could only have been generated for an application that has one
- config: `MELODY_STATIC_EXCLUDED_PATHS` (`kernel.static.excluded_paths`) names the path prefixes the built-in file server declines without touching the disk, comma separated. A declined request continues down the rest of the middleware chain, which is how an application serves part of the url space behind its own middleware — authentication on a directory, a stricter policy, a file server of its own. An entry is compared against the raw request path exactly as `security.NewPathPrefixMatcher` reads one, so a prefix written for a firewall rule and one written here select the same requests. An empty entry, or one that does not start with a slash, fails the boot. **Breaking**: the accessor is added to `config/contract.HttpConfiguration`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface
- http: `SimpleRateLimitWithResolver`, `IpRateLimitWithResolver` and `UserRateLimitWithResolver` take the client-ip resolver the convenience helpers could not reach. The three original helpers build their configuration internally and return only the middleware, so `SetClientIpResolver` — the remedy the documentation prescribes for a deployment behind a reverse proxy — was unreachable through them, and `IpRateLimit(n)` therefore gave every client one shared budget behind any proxy. The original three are unchanged and remain correct where the direct peer is the client
- http: `static.FileServerConfig.SetAllowedDotPrefixList` names the dot-prefixed first path elements the file server may retrieve. The default allows `.well-known` alone, so an ACME http-01 challenge, `security.txt` and `assetlinks.json` published under the public directory stay retrievable while `.env`, `.git` and `.htpasswd` do not. The allowance never reaches past the first element, so `.well-known/.env` is refused exactly like `/.env`, and an empty list refuses every dot-prefixed path
- http: `RegenerateRequestSession` rotates the session id of a request and republishes the rotated session in one call, which is the whole session-fixation defence a login handler needs; write the authenticated identity to the session it returns. `session.Manager.RegenerateSession` remains the storage-level primitive: it mints a fresh id, carries the values over, removes the entry the previous id pointed at, and latches the session passed in out of use, so a caller that does not publish the rotated one logs the client out cleanly instead of leaving it presenting a deleted id. The latch matters because `Session.Set` lifts the cleared flag: a caller that rotated and then kept writing to the original object would otherwise have the response path re-create the just-deleted id and re-issue it as the cookie, silently undoing the rotation and letting a pre-login, attacker-plantable id carry the authenticated identity. A rotated-away session can no longer be resurrected. **Breaking**: the
  added contract method breaks an out-of-tree implementation of `session/contract.Manager`
- config: `MELODY_HTTP_SESSION_TTL` (`kernel.http.session_ttl`) sets how long a stored session stays valid, reaching the session manager through `Configuration.Http().SessionTtl()`. The default is unchanged at `0`, which stores sessions with no expiry; a negative value fails the boot. A deployment that leaves it at `0` should know the default in-memory store then grows without bound — an http application running on both the framework-supplied storage and a ttl of zero is told so once at boot
- http: `SessionCookiePolicy.Secure` takes a `SessionCookieSecurePolicy`. The default `SessionCookieSecureFromScheme` derives the attribute from the resolved scheme as before, `SessionCookieSecureAlways` forces it on for a deployment whose proxy terminates TLS but is not trusted for `X-Forwarded-Proto`, and `SessionCookieSecureNever` is the explicit opt-out — the only value that can weaken the cookie, so it is discouraged — previously such a deployment emitted every session cookie without `Secure` and had no way to override it

- cache: `Manager.GetCounter` reads a key written by `Increment` or `Decrement`. Those operations are backend-native so a distributed backend keeps them atomic, which means they store the count as decimal text rather than through the serializer — `Get` handed that raw payload to a deserializer that does not expect it, and with any serializer other than the default the two were mutually unreadable in both directions. The contract is now stated on `Increment` as well
- application: two boot warnings name a resource melody supplied that nothing will ever reclaim, so the deployment learns it from a log line rather than from a memory graph. One reports that the framework-supplied in-memory cache backend carries no item ceiling, so a key cached without a ttl is kept until the process exits. The other reports the framework-supplied in-memory session storage paired with a session ttl of zero: melody mints a session for every request that arrives without a session cookie, so one write on a public path turns every such request into a permanent entry, and an unauthenticated caller decides how many arrive. Either half alone is silent — a storage the application registered is the operator's to prune, and a lifetime the deployment set reclaims on its own. Both are raised from the http path only: a command builds its map, runs, and takes it away with it
- config: a dollar in an `.env` value opens a reference only where godotenv says it does — upper case, digits and underscore. Admitting lower case and the dot turned literals that had always been read as data into references to keys no file defines, and an unresolvable reference fails the boot: `DB_PASSWORD=pa$sword` became a lookup of a key named `sword`, and `PRICE=$1.50` a lookup of `1.50`, in applications whose configuration had not changed
- http: a middleware reference is satisfiable when the registrations sharing the referenced name cover the referrer between them. Each registration was weighed alone, so the ordinary way of writing an environment-specific middleware — an `auth` for development beside an `auth` for production, both under the one name the pipeline orders against — was refused in development AND in production for a configuration that boots correctly in both. The environment is not free-form (`config.validateEnvironment` admits `dev` and `prod` and refuses to boot on anything else), so a name registered for both is registered everywhere
- http: the reference-gating check weighs only what the group being built assembles. It was handed every definition the builder holds, so building the `web` group reported an unsatisfiable reference between two definitions confined to `api` — a pair `web` never assembles, over a gating no request to it could reach. The check still fires for the group that does carry the pair
- application: the comment on `MiddlewarePriorityStatic` describes where the static file server actually sits. It said a priority above the default keeps the server innermost; the constant is below the default and the server is outermost, which is what makes a request for a file that exists skip the rate limiter, the compressor and the access log
- http: `PrefersHtml` is covered again for an html type that arrives on its own in mixed case. The entry was rewritten to carry a second type, which reaches the parser differently — one takes the whole header as the type, the other has to split it first — and the single-type case was left uncovered everywhere
- openapi: a field the validator rejects for every value is advertised as `not: {}` rather than as an object-level contradiction. `minProperties: 1` beside `maxProperties: 0` reads as impossible and is — for an object; JSON Schema draft-04 §5.4.1 and §5.4.2, the dialect the document declares, apply both to object instances only and ignore them otherwise. Once a named collection is promoted to a component, the same pair beside its `$ref` excluded nothing and `["a"]` satisfied a conjunction the server refuses, while the inline path for the same verdict emitted `minItems`/`maxItems` and did bind — so the two paths contradicted each other. The tests asserted on the keywords rather than on the exclusion, which is why none of this was visible
- bunorm: the encrypted-width measurement no longer allocates the widest row in the table. It sealed a plaintext of `MAX(LENGTH(col))` bytes to measure the result — some 320 MB resident for a 64 MiB row, and `LONGTEXT` reaches 4 GiB, so measuring cost more memory than the batched migration it was measuring for and an out-of-memory kill is not recoverable. Raw-standard base64 encodes every three input bytes as exactly four characters, so the width is now a probe of at most two bytes plus arithmetic; the marker, key id, nonce, tag and alphabet are still read off a real seal rather than assumed
- amqp: a re-publish the broker refuses returns the delivery to the queue when no dead-letter queue is configured, instead of refusing it outright. With a dead-letter exchange bound, refusing without requeue routes the message there — kept, visible, recoverable. Without one the same refusal discards it, so the transport turned at-least-once into at-most-once under exactly the conditions that produce a refused re-publish: a `max-length` policy with `overflow=reject-publish`, an unroutable return, a queue that filled. The requeued delivery carries the counts it arrived with, which is the accounting given up to keep the message
- container: a service the container owns stays one instance for the whole process even when it is resolved through a request scope. A provider builds from the container alone now; the scope layers over the container for the code running inside a request and no longer reaches underneath into the container's own wiring. The kernel installs a request logger into every scope under the name the container registers, so a provider that did nothing request-specific beyond asking for a logger was assembled from a scope entry, kept per request, and closed when that request ended — the bunorm providers read the logger while opening, so the `*bun.DB` pool was closed at the end of the request that first resolved it, taking it away from every other one. A provider that genuinely needs something only a request carries is now told the service does not exist, at the point the mistake is made, instead of quietly becoming a per-request object a request ending destroys
- debug: the error-context walk stops at a depth bound instead of descending until the goroutine stack is gone. The cycle guard answers a context that holds itself and says nothing about one that is merely very deep, so a deep enough acyclic context — a producer-supplied map, which nothing validates — ended the process with `fatal error: stack overflow` while rendering a debug page: not a panic, so no recover in the command layer turns it into a reported failure. Measured with the stack capped at 16 MiB it took some five hundred thousand levels, which the production cap of one gigabyte scales up rather than removes. The bound is the same one `internal/copy.go` puts on the same shape of walk, far above anything a real error context reaches; a subtree past it renders as `<depth limit>`, told apart from `<cycle>` because the two say different things

- container: a scope is a registrar in its own right, layered over the container it came from. `ScopeManager.RegisterScoped` — reached through the container, which is a scope manager — declares a service whose lifetime is one scope — one http request, one message delivery, one cron tick — built lazily on the first resolution through a scope, held for as long as that scope lives, and closed when it closes; `container.RegisterScoped[T]`, `MustRegisterScoped[T]`, `RegisterScopedType[T]` and `MustRegisterScopedType[T]` are the typed front doors, and `Scope.RegisterScoped` adds one to a single live scope. The root container never sees a scoped service and two scopes never share one: each holds its own creation guard, so neither waits on the other's build. A scoped service reads both levels — the scope's overrides and the container's singletons — while a container provider stays request-agnostic exactly as before, which is the direction that had to stay closed. Until now the only way to put
  anything request-specific in front of userland was the override the framework installs for its own logger and request context, so an application had no way to declare a request-lifetime service at all. The declaration sits on `ScopeManager` rather than beside the container's own registrations, because a scope does not exist until a request arrives: the services a scope owns have to be declared by whatever will be making the scopes. **Breaking**: `container/contract.ScopeManager` and `container/contract.Scope` gain `RegisterScoped` and `MustRegisterScoped`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of either — including an implementation of `Container`, which embeds `ScopeManager`
- container: `Replacing()` admits a registration whose name — or whose registered type — the other lifetime already claims. Without it the collision is refused where it is made, in both directions and whichever order the two registrations arrive in, because a name that answers with a process singleton outside a scope and with a per-request service inside one is an ambiguity whose symptom appears one lifetime away from its cause. It admits substitution, not decoration: a scoped provider that resolves the name it replaces re-enters itself and is reported as the circular dependency it is
- container: `ClosedWithScope()` hands an installed override to the scope's teardown, through the new `OverrideInstanceWithOptions` and `OverrideProtectedInstanceWithOptions`. The default is unchanged and stays the right one — an override belongs to whoever installed it and outlives the scope, which is what lets the http kernel report a scope-close failure through the request logger it installed — so the option is for the caller that built the value for one scope and holds no other reference to it. The four `OverrideService` signatures are untouched
- container: a scope closes what it built in dependency order, dependents before their dependencies, falling back to the descending node key for anything the graph says nothing about and reporting a cycle the way the container's own teardown reports one. The previous order was the node name alone, which was defensible while the only scope-built values were incidental and is a coin flip now that a scoped repository holding a scoped transaction is the ordinary shape
- container: a collection gathered on a scope holds the scope's registrations as well as the container's, and one gathered by a container provider holds only the container's. `AllImplementing` on a scope previously delegated straight through, so a scope-registered handler was silently absent from every collection — a handler nothing dispatches to, with no error anywhere to say a member was missing
- application: `ScopedServiceModule` is the module hook for request-lifetime services, with `RegisterScopedServices(registrar ScopedServiceRegistrar)` running at boot beside `RegisterServices`. It is a separate interface, so no existing module changes. `ScopedServiceRegistrar` and `ServiceRegistrar` deliberately share no method with each other's container counterpart: a container provider handed to the scoped hook, or a scoped provider handed to `RegisterServices`, is a compile error at the call site rather than a service quietly rebuilt and closed once per request. A name claimed at both lifetimes joins the aggregated boot collision report
- wiring: `//melody:scoped` on a constructor emits it into a second generated function, `<function>Scoped(registrar containercontract.ScopedRegistrar)`, through `MustRegisterScoped`/`MustRegisterScopedType`. The two registrars arrive at two different module hooks and neither satisfies the other, so two functions is the only shape that fits both; the function is emitted only when something carries the directive, and a project that declares nothing scoped regenerates a byte-identical file. `melody:wiring:generate` takes `--scoped-function` to name it

- exception: `Logged` answers an error that reports itself already logged, and is what a writer returns after filing its record. An error whose chain carries an `AlreadyLogged` implementer is marked in place and handed back unchanged, so its identity — and every `errors.Is` and `errors.As` its readers perform on it — survives; an error whose chain carries none is wrapped in a marked melody error keeping it as its cause, because `errors.New`, `fmt.Errorf` and every runtime error have nowhere for the mark to live and the next reader would otherwise file the same failure a second time. The wrap cannot change how a status is resolved: it happens exactly when no `HttpException` is in the chain, which is exactly when the status was already going to be the generic one
- exception: `IsAlreadyLogged` reads the already-logged mark at the depth `MarkLogged` writes it. It is the single reader: reading the mark off a concrete `*Error` instead misses a marked `HttpException` and anything wrapping a marked error, which are then rendered a second time
- logging: `NewStandardErrorLogger` adapts a melody logger to the `*log.Logger` the standard library asks for, so a component that can only speak to one — net/http's `Server.ErrorLog`, bun's diagnostic channel — reaches the application's journal instead of printing unstructured text to stderr, where it is invisible to a deployment whose journal is a json file. Each line the standard logger emits becomes one warning record with the line in the context, so the message stays the one groupable string an operator can query on; a nil logger leaves the destination inert

- cli: `--format=json-pretty` renders the same envelope indented for a person reading it by hand. It exists because json is the machine format and a machine reads a stream: one document per line, so a consumer can follow a long-running command live with a line reader and hand each line to a parser whole
- cli: `output.IsJsonFormat` answers for both json spellings, and every place that used to compare against `FormatJson` asks through it — the two differ in whitespace alone, so a site that recognised one and not the other would colour a banner into a document, or print a table where a document was asked for
- container: `IsClosed` reports whether a `Close` already began tearing the container down. A repeated `Close` returns the first teardown's memoized error, so a caller that closes defensively could not tell a failure it had just caused from one somebody else had already discovered and reported; asking before closing is what keeps one failure from being presented as two incidents
- security: `InMemoryTokenStore.WithRevocationEpochRetention` bounds how long `PurgeExpired` keeps a published revocation boundary after its instant, mirroring the rueidis store's option of the same name. The default keeps boundaries forever, the safe cell for a per-process store whose growth is bounded by its user count; a negative retention is refused rather than silently ignored, because a boundary dropped early is a revocation bypass
- security: every time-verifying component takes an injected clock — `JwtConfig.Clock`, `HmacTokenSourceConfig.Clock`, `HmacEnvelopeSignerConfig.Clock`, `TotpSecondFactorAuthenticatorConfig.Clock` and `NewMemoryNonceGuardWithClock` — defaulting to the system clock, the seam the token store and the rate limiter have always had. Until now the JWT expiry, the envelope window, the nonce expiry and the TOTP step all read `time.Now()` directly, so none of them could be tested deterministically and none obeyed the frozen clock the rest of a test harness runs under; the HMAC source also hands its own clock to the in-process nonce guard it defaults, so the two measure one window on one clock
- application: `ProcessContext` — `ServiceProcessContext`, `NewProcessContext`, `ProcessContextMustFromResolver`, `ProcessContextFromResolver` — is the console counterpart of the http request context: the generated process id every log record of the run is correlated under, and the moment the run started.
- logging: `NewProcessLogger` is the console sibling of `NewRequestLogger`, and the cli entry point now decorates with it.
- logging: `RunShieldedStep` runs one step under the exit handler's own budget and answers whether it finished, so the normal return of `Run` tears down under the same shield the panic path has had since the exit-step budget was installed
- logging: `Closed()` on the json logger reports whether `Close` really closed the underlying writer, and `NewRequestLogger`'s decorator forwards it to the logger it wraps — the question the process-boundary exit handler asks before trusting a logger with the final record, because a file-backed logger a teardown already closed silently drops every write

- messagebus: `RegisterTransports` registers a `TransportsCloser` beside the transports map, under `ServiceTransportsCloser`, resolved as a dependency of the map itself — so whichever run resolves the transports guarantees their broker connections join the container's ordered teardown, closed after every consumer that depends on them, in deterministic name order, with a failing transport's error carrying its name and not stranding its siblings unclosed

- http: `Kernel.SetMethodPolicy` installs the method policy the kernel reads on every request — whether HEAD falls back to the GET route, whether an unrouted OPTIONS is answered with the computed Allow header — and `MethodPolicy` moves onto the http contract beside its two sibling policies. Without the door the policy was a documented type with nowhere to hand it: `DefaultKernelOptions` built one, the kernel read one on every request, and no application could reach the field between them, so an api that had to answer 405 to OPTIONS wrote the configuration and watched it change nothing. **Breaking**: the contract interface gains a method, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of `http/contract.Kernel`
- http: `Kernel.HasErrorHandler` reports whether the application installed an error handler, the question a composition root asks before deciding to register the framework exception listener — that listener answers every `kernel.exception` dispatch first, so a handler behind it can never run
- http: `Kernel.OpenRequestScopes` counts the request scopes opened and not yet closed — one per request being served, hijacked connections included — and the http shutdown drains on it within the shutdown budget. `http.Server.Shutdown` returns nil for a hijacked connection the moment the handler takes it, so a websocket still being served left the process reporting a clean stop it had not obtained, with the container closing under the running handler; a drain that does not finish inside the budget is an error naming how many scopes stayed open, and a replacement kernel without the counter is not waited on at all
- http: `RequestContextMustFromResolver` and `RequestContextFromResolver` resolve the current request's context — its id and start moment — from a request scope's resolver, the counterpart of `application.ProcessContextFromResolver` for the http process shape; the tolerant form answers nil on a console process or the root container, where the service does not exist
- http: `RouteRegistry.SetBootCollisionRecorder` arms the aggregated boot-collision channel with `BootCollisionKindHttpRoute` and `BootCollisionKindHttpRouteName`: with a recorder set, a duplicate registration is recorded — the first registration wins — instead of panicking over each one in isolation, so a boot can collect every collision into the one report that names them all
- bag: `ParameterBag.AppendString` adds one string under a name inside a single critical section, and `All` copies a stored string slice or string map instead of handing back the live value. Both had been released on the frozen majors and were absent here: the contract-level `AppendString` helper reads and writes under two separate locks, so two concurrent appends kept only one of the two values — a lost update no race detector reports, because each individual access is locked
- container: `ServiceDescriptions` on the built container answers what it can say WITHOUT running a provider — every name either lifetime knows, with the type read from the built instance when one exists and from the provider's declared return type otherwise, which the container now records at registration for both lifetimes. It is the door an introspection listing answers through, so listing a production wiring stops meaning dialing every pool and opening every connection as the price of a listing
- config: `MELODY_HTTP_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT` (`kernel.http.shutdown_timeout`) sets how long a stopping http server waits for the requests it has already admitted before cutting them, reaching the server through `Configuration.Http().ShutdownTimeout()`. The same budget bounds the drain of the request scopes the kernel still holds and the join of the `OnHttpShutdown` hooks. It defaults to five seconds, deliberately far below the thirty the write timeout promises each request; a deployment whose supervisor grants a longer termination grace raises it to match. Only a positive value can describe a wait, so zero and negative fail the boot instead of silently becoming the default. **Breaking**: the accessor is added to `config/contract.HttpConfiguration`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface
- application: what `net/http` reports on its own reaches the application's journal. A connection that fails before a request exists, a request the server rejects before any handler, a listener degrading while the process stays up — none of it is ever seen by the http kernel, and with `Server.ErrorLog` unset `net/http` prints all of it to stderr as unstructured text while every other line the process writes is structured, so a deployment whose journal is a json file never saw any of it
- httpclient: `HttpClient.Close` releases the idle connections the client's own transport is holding. Every client builds its own pool — a hundred connections per host by default, kept for ninety seconds — and dropping the last reference to the client releases none of them, because each parked connection has a read loop of its own keeping the transport reachable. Close does not abort requests in flight, and the client stays usable afterwards: it dials again
- httpclient: `RequestStreamWithContext` is `RequestStream` bound to a context: cancelling it ends the request and the body read, which is the only remedy for a stream a server never ends. The close obligation is unchanged — cancelling releases the connection, it does not close the `StreamResponse` for the caller — and a nil context is refused by name
- httpclient: `TransportDuration` and `TransportCount` build the pointer fields of the redesigned `TransportConfig` in place, so a configuration literal stays a literal — Go offers no address of a constant inline

- debug: `debug:container --build` and `debug:middleware --build` are the doors that opt into running providers and factories. The listing itself no longer does: see the `Changed` entries for what the bare command now reports
- debug: `debug.MiddlewareDescriptionProvider` and `debug.MiddlewareBuildProvider` are the two channels `debug:middleware` reads — the pipeline as the selection and ordering see it with no factory run, and the built chain behind `--build`. They replace the single provider that could only be satisfied by building
- debug: `debug:router` reports `priority` and `order`, and under `--verbose` also `requirements`, `defaults` and `attributes`. Priority and registration order are the two discriminators the dispatch itself breaks ties on, so the command that exists to say WHICH route answers could not say it: two routes overlapping on pattern and methods rendered identically

- session: `session.ErrSessionDeleted` is the cause the save refusal carries for a session deleted while the request holding it was still running. It exists so the response path can tell the session ending apart from a storage outage: the first expires the browser cookie and serves the handler's own response, the second suppresses the cookie and answers 500. Assert it with `errors.Is`
- session: `(*Session).Snapshot` reads the values, the modified flag and the cleared flag under one lock acquisition, for the callers that have to pair a branch decision with the values it acts on — `Manager.SaveSession` and the response path's own delete-or-save decision. **Breaking**: the accessor is added to `session/contract.Session`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface
- session: `session.NewManagerWithTombstoneRetention` builds a manager whose write-back refusal window is sized by the caller, for wiring the manager by hand; it refuses a non-positive window with a panic naming the rule, the same answer the constructor gives a negative ttl. `session.TombstoneRetention` is the default that window falls back to
- session: `session.NewManagerOwningStorage` is the constructor for a caller that built the manager and the storage by hand and wants one `Close` to end both — the cascade `NewManager` no longer performs. See the `Changed` entry for why the plain constructor gave it up
- config: `MELODY_HTTP_SESSION_TOMBSTONE_RETENTION` (`kernel.http.session_tombstone_retention`) sets how long a deleted session id keeps refusing a write-back, reaching the session manager through `Configuration.Http().SessionTombstoneRetention()`; `config.DefaultSessionTombstoneRetention` is five minutes. Only a positive value can describe the window, so zero and negative fail the boot instead of silently disarming the logout defence. **Breaking**: the accessor is added to `config/contract.HttpConfiguration`, which breaks an out-of-tree implementation of that interface

- cache: `cache.DeserializationError` is the typed error a read answers when a stored payload does not deserialize, with `NewDeserializationError` and `IsDeserializationError` as its doors. It exists so `Remember` can tell a corrupt payload apart from a cache that failed: the first is a miss the callback heals by overwriting, the second is a failure that must travel. A `Cache` implementation of your own inherits the self-healing by wrapping its own deserialization failures with it
- cache: `cache.NewManagerOwningBackend` builds the manager whose `Close` cascades into the backend, for the caller that built both by hand and wants one `Close` to end both — the cascade `NewManager` no longer performs. See the `Changed` entry for why the default moved
- cache: `(*Manager).NormalizeStoredValue` answers the shape a value stored through this manager reads back as: one serializer round-trip, run locally with no backend involved. `Remember` consults it so the computing call and the cached calls answer one shape, where an `int` used to come back a `float64` and a struct a `map[string]any` — but only from the second call on, which is what made the mismatch survive every first-call test
- cache: `RememberOption.WithContext` ties a caller's wait to the request that opened it, and `Context` reads it back. Without one the wait is unbounded, so a client that disconnected an hour ago still holds the goroutine that served it — and with the goroutine its scope, its session and everything the request reached. It ends that one caller's wait and never the computation, which belongs to the leader and is awaited by everyone coalesced onto it

### Changed

- cli: **Breaking** — `clicontract.Command.Run` receives `clicontract.Context` instead of `*clicontract.CommandContext`, `cli.NewCommandContext` is replaced by `cli.NewRoot`, and `clicontract.CommandContext` is gone. Every command in the framework, in the melody integrations and in the example is rewritten; a command outside the tree changes one signature, reads `commandContext.Writer()` instead of the `Writer` field, and reads `commandContext.Arguments()` instead of `commandContext.Args().Slice()`. `Flag.Names()` is gone with the alias: `flag.Definition().Name` is the reader, and no flag in the tree declares an alias. `cli.Register` takes `*cli.Root`. The mechanical rewrite is written out line by line in `UPGRADE.md`
- cli: `Root.SetWriter` and `Root.SetErrorWriter` reach the registered commands as well as the tree, whenever they were registered. The engine defaults each command's stream separately, to the process's standard output, so a writer set on the tree alone left every command writing past it — which is why the tests of this framework each had to reach into the command list and set the stream a second time by hand
- cli: `cli.Root` installs an inert exit handler on the engine and offers no door to remove it. The application already installed one by hand at its single call site, for the reason now written on the constructor: left at its default the engine ends the process itself, from inside `Run`, on any error a command returns, and the application's deferred `Close` and its final structured record would never run

- config, bag: the string grammar of the float accessors is plain decimal — an optional sign, digits and at most one decimal point. The shared parser read `strconv.ParseFloat` in full, so underscore spellings (`1_000.5`), hexadecimal floats (`0x1p10` is `1024`) and exponents (`1e3`) all parsed — a value the strict base-10 `Int` beside it refused was silently accepted as a float under a spelling nobody meant to support, and validation built on the float accessor accepted spellings the integer accessor was hardened against. `NaN` and the infinities stay refused on every branch, the string spellings now by the grammar itself. **Behavioural change**
- bag: `ParameterBag.Get` copies at the depth `All` copies — a stored `[]string` or `map[string]string` is answered as the caller's own. Handed back live, the value was the bag's: a caller mutating an element wrote into the bag behind its lock, visible to every later reader and racing a concurrent `All` or `StringSlice` copy. Scalars are returned as stored and pay nothing. **Behavioural change**
- cache: `NewDefaultRememberOption` arms a cancelable flight, so the shipped default no longer pins a key behind a hung callback for the life of the process. Under the old default the leader could not be told to stop, the entry was deleted only by the leader's own return, and the one path that replaces a live entry requires a canceled flight, which a non-cancelable one never becomes — every later caller of the key parked behind it, one goroutine each, and the remedy was an opt-in the caller had to know about. Now the last waiter leaving cancels the flight and the next caller replaces it with a fresh leader. The wait itself stays unbounded: a waiter that brings neither a caller context nor a wait timeout still parks while the callback runs, and `WithCancelable(false)` restores the detached leader deliberately. The single-flight unit already keyed on cancelability, so callers on either setting never coalesced across it and the flip changes no cross-option behaviour. **Behavioural change**
- validation: `regex`, `email`, `alpha`, `alphanumeric` and `numeric` refuse a value that is not a string instead of passing it in silence, which is the symmetry the numeric constraints already had. A `[]byte` handed to `regex` never ran the pattern and a field typed `any` satisfied every one of the five, so a rule that reads as enforced enforced nothing for exactly the payloads most likely to be attacker-shaped. A nil and an empty string remain a skip, which is what carries optionality. **Behavioural change**
- validation: `minLength`, `maxLength` and `notBlank` measure a string rather than a Go rendering, and refuse a value of any other type. They used to measure `fmt.Sprintf("%v", value)`, so an empty slice passed `min=1` because `[]` is two runes long, a five-byte `[]byte` failed `max=5` at a rendered width of twenty-one, and `notBlank` accepted `false`, `0`, `[]` and `map[]` — a consent checkbox arriving as `false` was a value the constraint declared present. **Behavioural change**
- validation: a rule that names a parameterized constraint WITHOUT parameters fails closed. The registered instance is the template `WithParams` is called on, never a fallback configuration, so a bare `regex` used to validate with `.*` and accept everything, a bare `lessThan` enforced "< 0" and a bare `max` enforced the constructor's 100 — three different silent configurations nobody wrote. The contract now says so beside the door. **Behavioural change**
- validation: `regex=` with an empty pattern is refused at construction. The empty pattern compiles to a regular expression that matches every string, so the rule that looked strictest of all enforced nothing; a pattern meant to match everything now has to say so. **Behavioural change**
- validation: a negative bound is refused where it is written — at `NewMinLength`/`NewMaxLength` by panic, and at the tag door by a parse refusal, because a tag is data a request path reads. `max=-1` used to reject every value including the empty string, with a message naming an impossible limit, and `min=-1` was a silent no-op on a field that visibly declared validation. **Behavioural change**
- validation: a numeric constraint parameter must be an integer in its entirety. `fmt.Sscanf("%d")` stopped at the first non-digit, so `lessThan=-0.5` became a bound of `0` and accepted `-0.2`, `1e3` became `1` and `5abc` became `5`; `strconv.Atoi` reads the whole string or refuses it. **Behavioural change**
- validation: a tag that survives the empty/skip-marker guard and then parses to no rule at all — a bare comma, for instance — is a syntax error rather than a request to validate nothing. Accepting it left a field that visibly declares validation silently unenforced. **Behavioural change**
- openapi: the schema mirror follows the repaired validator, so the generated document changes for every tag class the validation repairs above touch — in the restrictive direction, never the reverse. A bare parameterized constraint (`min`, `max`, `greaterThan`, `lessThan`, `regex`) is advertised as an unsatisfiable field where the mirror used to advertise the pre-repair defaults — `minLength: 1`, `maxLength: 100`, an exclusive zero bound, a match-everything pattern — three of which were silent configurations nobody wrote. **Behavioural change**
- openapi: an empty `regex` pattern (`regex=`) is advertised as an unsatisfiable field, mirroring the construction refusal, where the mirror used to compile `""` and publish a `pattern` that matches every string. **Behavioural change**
- openapi: a numeric bound is read as an integer in its entirety (`parseBoundStrict`, the strict twin of the validator's `parseIntStrict`), so `max=99.5` — which the pre-repair leading-integer scan read as `99` — and `greaterThan=5x` are advertised as the unsatisfiable fields they now are instead of as satisfiable bounds the server never enforces. **Behavioural change**
- openapi: a negative `min`/`max` bound is advertised as an unsatisfiable field, null included, mirroring the tag-door refusal — where a negative `min` used to be clamped to a satisfiable `minLength: 0` and a negative `max` on a nullable field kept its null. A negative `greaterThan`/`lessThan` bound stays the legitimate declaration it is. **Behavioural change**
- openapi: the string constraints (`min`, `max`, `regex`, `email`, `alpha`, `numeric`, `alphanumeric`, `notBlank`) on a non-string shape are advertised as the refusal they now are: a nullable field accepts exactly `null` (the constraints pass a nil pointer) and a non-nullable one accepts nothing — `notBlank`, which also refuses the null, makes the field unconditionally unsatisfiable on every non-string shape. The whole pre-repair apparatus this replaces is gone: the "known limitation" paragraph that left `min`/`max` on non-string fields advertised as satisfiable, the stringified-length reading of `max` on `integer`/`number`/`boolean` (one character for a digit, four for `true`), the `[]byte`/`time.Time` exemptions that existed because the constraints used to accept those blobs in silence, the brace-floor arithmetic for a `max` below 2 on a value embed with its `Stringer`/`Formatter` escape hatches, and the notBlank rule that cleared only the null on a `$ref`. Any string constraint on a promoted embed now contradicts the enclosing object — a pointer embed as a declared fail-closed over-approximation — and on a reference the same refusals apply against the shape the component stands for. **Behavioural change**
- openapi: a tag that parses to no rule at all (`validate:","`) is advertised as an unsatisfiable field, mirroring the parser's refusal, where the mirror used to iterate zero rules and advertise a satisfiable one. **Behavioural change**
- logging: the json logger weighs a record whose level is not one of the five known ones as an error instead of inheriting the default lowest priority. The zero-value `&exception.Error{}` a recovery handler can carry reaches `Log` with an empty level, and filed as debug its fatal record was dropped by every production threshold; the label still carries the raw level, so the record says what it was handed. **Behavioural change**
- logging: the json logger's timestamp carries nanosecond precision. Whole-second stamps made every record of a busy second indistinguishable, and the fraction still parses under the RFC 3339 layouts consumers already use. **Behavioural change**
- logging: the json logger takes its timestamp inside the write mutex, together with the encoding, so the order of the stamps is the order of the writes — which is what `LOGGING.md` promises and what the comment on that line claimed the precision was paying for. Taken above the lock, the stamp said when the record was FORMED, and the two orders diverged by however long the encoding took. The cost is the encoding serialized across writers; a logger writes to one destination through one lock either way, so what this buys is the ordering guarantee the document already sold

- event: `AddSubscriber` returns a `SubscriberRegistration` and `RemoveSubscriber` takes one, where both used to take the subscriber value. Filed under the value's pointer, two instances of a zero-size type were one identity: `RemoveSubscriber(a)` took down the listeners of `b` as well and reported a plausible count for it, and nothing in either value could ever distinguish them. The installation is now filed under an id the dispatcher issues, so two installations of one subscriber — of one zero-size subscriber included — are two registrations removed independently, and a subscriber that is not a pointer installs like any other. The frozen majors took a narrower repair for the same defect, refusing the second registration of one identity; this major takes the redesign instead, so the second registration is legal. Keep what `AddSubscriber` returns if you ever remove the subscriber. **Breaking**
- event: a dispatch that ended early with a listener marked required behind it returns `RequiredListenerSkippedError` instead of completing, or instead of returning only the ending listener's own failure. Both endings are covered — a listener that stops propagation and a listener that fails — because the listeners behind either never ran; the failure travels as the cause. The required scan runs BEFORE the listener's own error is returned, which is where the guarantee used to be lost by one line. **Behavioural change**
- event: the propagation test opens the listener iteration instead of closing it. An event that arrived already stopped — the object a previous dispatch returned — still ran the first listener and then had that listener named as the one that stopped it, while the required listeners behind it were skipped under a stopper that had stopped nothing. **Behavioural change**
- event: marking a listener through `MarkListenerRequired` or `MarkListenerMaySkipRequiredListeners` panics on a registration the dispatcher does not hold, where it used to be a documented silent no-op — a mark that lands nowhere leaves the guarantee unarmed while reporting that it was applied, and the caller has no way to tell. `EventDispatcherAdapter` likewise refuses a wrapped dispatcher that cannot mark required listeners, rather than absorbing the mark: callers probe for `RequiredListenerRegistrar` precisely to learn whether the guarantee is available, and the adapter satisfies that probe on its own behalf. **Behavioural change**
- event: `NewEventDispatcherAdapter` no longer takes a clock. The parameter was validated at construction and never read, and `EVENT.md` had documented the one-argument signature from the beginning. **Breaking**
- event: `AddSubscriber` validates every subscribed event before it registers a single listener, and refuses a subscriber that declares no events as well as an event name mapped to an empty list. Validating while registering left a subscriber whose second event was malformed half-installed, with the listeners of the first one live and firing under a subscriber the caller was told had been refused; an empty declaration registered nothing while reporting success, which is how a subscriber assembled from configuration ends up silently inert. **Behavioural change**
- debug: under `--verbose` the json document of `debug:events` carries `data.{events, listeners}`, where it carried the event list alone. The listener detail — including the marks that say whether the fail-closed guarantee is armed — existed only in the table format, so a machine consumer could not learn it at any verbosity. Without `--verbose` the document keeps its previous shape. **Behavioural change**

- config: a bare integer is refused where a duration is expected. Read as nanoseconds — the Go-native interpretation — `RegisterRuntime("ttl", 30)` followed by `Parameter.Duration()` produced a timeout that expires instantly, with no error anywhere, while the same value spelled as the string `"30"` was refused for its missing unit. The numeric spelling is now refused the same way, with the cause naming what is missing; a `time.Duration` and a string carrying a unit are unaffected. **Behavioural change**
- config: `Float64` refuses NaN and the infinities on every branch. `strconv.ParseFloat` parses `"NaN"`, `"Inf"` and `"Infinity"` without an error, and a NaN that reaches a threshold disarms it silently, because every ordered comparison against NaN is false — a guard written as `ratio < 0 || ratio > 1` simply stops guarding. The sibling `Int` already refused exactly these shapes. **Behavioural change**
- config: a typed-nil `map[string]string` reads as the absence it is. Reported as present it came back as an empty non-nil map, so a caller branching on the presence flag to apply a default silently got the empty map instead, while the strict accessors beside it already answered absent for a nil value. **Behavioural change**
- config: `IntWithDefault` answers the default only for a parameter that is ABSENT. A parameter that exists but does not parse now panics instead of quietly becoming the default, because a mistyped value that turns into a number nobody wrote is a misconfiguration running in disguise. **Behavioural change**
- config: a `.env` line is preprocessed byte by byte rather than through runes. Every character the walk decides on is ASCII, and a rune round-trip re-encodes whatever is not valid UTF-8, so a file saved as Latin-1 had its values rewritten — a password among them — where godotenv alone passes a quoted value through untouched. **Behavioural change**
- config: the comment cut matches godotenv's own. A `#` that opens before the key separator comments the whole line out; a `#` after it stays in the produced line and godotenv's countback decides where the value ends. Cutting in both places cut TWICE, and cutting at the first hash read `hello # world # x` as `hello` where godotenv reads `hello # world`. **Behavioural change**
- config: a `${...}` reference in a `.env` value whose closing brace arrived over a name outside the key grammar is refused instead of surviving as literal text. Nobody types `${...}` into a password by accident, so the braced form gets the policy the closed-but-misspelled `%env(...)%` already had; an unclosed brace stays data, like the bare dollar it is. **Behavioural change**
- config: an unterminated `%env(` and a `%name%` reference that nothing closed are both refused at boot. A `%env(` that never closes and a name-shaped run opened by a percent were surviving as literal template text, which is exactly what this reporting exists to catch — the contract already demands a literal percent be doubled. A percent standing in front of a character no name may start with is still data. **Behavioural change**
- config: `RegisterRuntime` refuses a blank name and one carrying leading or trailing whitespace. A padded name registered a parameter nobody could look up under the name they wrote. **Behavioural change**
- http: `NewRequirements` takes the pointers the `Require*` helpers return and refuses what it used to drop. Both drops failed OPEN: a requirement missing its pattern — a constant never filled in, a configured pattern that resolved to `""` — left the parameter with NO constraint at all, so a path segment declared numeric matched anything, and a name declared twice kept whichever entry the argument order happened to put last while the other declaration vanished. The router refuses an uncompilable pattern by name at registration; an absent one is the same class of mistake and is refused where the declaration is. **Breaking**: the signature moves from `...Requirement` to `...*Requirement`, which is what the helpers hand back — every call site had been dereferencing them by hand for a signature that could just as well accept them
- http: `JsonHandlerErrorResponder` is handed the failure itself beside the status and the message. The responder could not reconstruct what the door had already destroyed: the decoder's diagnosis and the per-field validation collection were gone before it was called, so an application that wanted the structured body had to abandon `JsonHandler` and hand-roll the binding. **Breaking**: the signature gains a trailing `cause error`
- http: a request path that names one parameter twice — `/orgs/:id/members/:id` — is refused at registration. The extraction writes both segments under one map key, so the handler can only ever read one of the two values and cannot tell which; the route is ambiguous by construction and the openapi document emitted for it is spec-invalid on duplicate path parameters. A parameter with no name at all, a bare `:`, is refused beside it rather than treated as an anonymous wildcard: it binds nothing, so the segment it occupies was matched and then discarded in silence. An unnamed catch-all, `*...`, stays the deliberate spelling of "swallow the rest and bind nothing". **Behavioural change**
- http: a route zone that is not one of the `RouteZone*` values is refused, at `ExposedRouteAttributes` and at registration. Accepted, it produced an artifact no filter can ever select — a misspelled zone on the route silently omits it from the zoned export while the unfiltered one still carries it, and a misspelled zone on `melody:routes:manifest` wrote an empty manifest over the good one and reported success, after which the frontend failed to resolve every route it asked for, at runtime, with the build green. An exposed route with no name, an expose attribute that is not a bool and a zone attribute that is not a string are refused at registration for the same reason: each was a developer stating an intention that the artifact then contradicted in silence. **Behavioural change**
- http: a trusted-proxy entry that parses as neither a CIDR prefix nor an address is refused where the list is ingested — `Kernel.SetForwardedHeadersPolicy` and `NewForwardedClientIpResolver` alike. Both readers skipped what they could not parse, so a typo (`10.0.0.0/33`, `10.0.0`) narrowed the trust in silence: the hop it named stopped being believed, `X-Forwarded-For` from it was no longer read, and every client behind that proxy collapsed onto the direct peer's single rate-limit bucket with no record anywhere. An empty entry is still skipped, because a list assembled by splitting an environment variable carries a trailing empty field for a trailing separator. **Behavioural change**
- http: route introspection publishes the requirement the caller DECLARED rather than the compiled form. The registration anchors and wraps every pattern, so the manifest, the openapi document and the debug listing carried `^(?:en|de)$` where the developer wrote `en|de` — a spelling that is not theirs, that re-wraps on every round trip through `NewRequirements`, and that hands RE2-only syntax to consumers whose engine is ECMA-262. **Behavioural change**
- http: `JsonHandler` reads the request body through the same door `Request.BindJson` is. A door of its own had drifted from all three of that one's judgements: an oversized upload was answered `400 "invalid json"` instead of `413`, so a client retried the same payload forever and the kernel's own 413-at-warning treatment was bypassed; the decoder's diagnosis — offending offset, field, type — died on the line that replaced it with a flat message; and a nil body panicked into a 500 where the sibling answers 400. Validation now travels the standardized envelope: the per-field detail reaches the client under `validationErrors` exactly as it does through `BindJsonAndValidate`, and a rule-wiring fault — a struct tag naming a rule that does not exist, which refuses every request that route will ever serve — is recorded at error instead of sitting at warning among the users who mistyped their address. **Behavioural change**

- http: every framework error body is the one standardized error envelope — `{"status": ..., "time": ..., "error": {"message": ...}}`, with `requestId` beside them where the request is known, the debug-only `context` and `cause` inside the error object, and the validation detail under its own top-level key. The envelope is shared by the exception listener, every kernel fallback path and `JsonErrorResponse` — the constructor the security entry points and the examples answer through — so a consumer of a melody error reads one shape whichever path rendered it; the status names the answer inside the body the way the status line names it outside. The success half of the standardization — a data envelope over handler results — is deliberately not here: it lands with the feature train, together with the machine-readable error `code` the envelope reserves a place for. **Behavioural change**: the flat `{"error": "<message>", "time": ...}` body is gone
- http: the public half of a validation failure's context travels under the key `validationErrors` — `BindJsonAndValidate` attaches it there, `exception.ValidationFailed` writes the same key, and the exception listener serves that key and only that key into the response body. The two frozen majors spell the key `errors`; v3 diverges deliberately, because the name says what the detail is, and a future kind of structured detail gets its own named key instead of widening an anonymous one. **Behavioural change**: a reader of the v3 context keyed on `errors` finds `validationErrors`
- exception: `NewHttpException` and `NewHttpExceptionWithCause` refuse a status code outside what an http response can carry — net/http's `WriteHeader` panics below 100 and above 999 deep in the response path, and a status the writer clamps serves an exception as success — naming the mistake where it is made instead of one response write away from it. **Behavioural change**: a constructor call with an out-of-range status panics at construction

- security: `NewAccessControlRule` builds a rule bounded to a path SEGMENT — `/admin` governs `/admin` and `/admin/panel` but not `/administrator` — which is the rule a caller reaches for by default, so the plain name is the bounded one. The cross-segment rule that matches every path merely beginning with the prefix is now the explicit `NewAccessControlRawPrefixRule`, kept behind its own name because, being the longest match, a raw rule shadows a correctly bounded rule that would have denied — which is why `PUBLIC_ACCESS` is refused on it. An empty prefix is refused rather than made a silent catch-all, and `NewAccessControlRuleWithSegmentPrefix` becomes a deprecated alias. **Behavioural change**: an existing caller of `NewAccessControlRule` gets stricter, segment-bounded matching, which can only refuse a request the old raw form would have granted
