- security: a global access control declared without any firewall now enforces instead of being silently dropped. `Builder.BuildAndCompile` returned nil the moment no firewall was registered, so the global `/admin ⇒ ROLE_ADMIN` rules an operator wrote enforced nothing, with no error anywhere; the resolution listener matches no firewall and the access control listener falls back to the global control, which the runtime is built and tested for. **Behavioural change**: a deployment that declared global rules behind a feature flag that turned every firewall off now has those rules apply
- security: a zero-value `FirewallOverrideConfiguration{}` inherits the global access control the way `NewFirewallOverrideConfiguration()` does. The exported struct's `With*` setters invite value-literal use, and the zero value carried `inheritGlobalAccessControl=false`, so a firewall built with it compiled an empty non-nil access control that matched nothing and blocked the fallback to the global policy — every route behind it reached its handler with no authorization while `SetGlobal`'s rules silently stopped applying. The struct also gains the `WithAccessControl`, `WithRoleHierarchy`, `WithAccessDecisionManager`, `WithMergeStrategy` and `WithInheritGlobalAccessControl` setters it lacked, and a setter called on the exact zero value first reads the constructor defaults. **Behavioural change**: `WithMergeStrategy` refuses a value that is none of the three by name, where an unrecognised one used to behave as `localFirst` in silence
- security: the role voters refuse a token that reports roles while answering `IsAuthenticated()` false — the shape a half-logged-in token takes. Both `RoleVoter` and `RoleHierarchyVoter` granted on the role alone, so a handler-level `IsGranted` check disagreed with the access control listener, which had always checked authentication first. **Behavioural change**: an unauthenticated token carrying roles is now denied on both paths
- security: `AccessDecisionManager.DecideAll` over an empty attribute list refuses instead of granting. Read as "every one of nothing is granted" it opened the decision to a caller that asked for nothing — an attribute list a configuration value resolved away — while `DecideAny` refused the same input; the compiled access control cannot produce an empty list, so the refusal is reached only through a direct caller, which is exactly the caller nothing else guards. **Behavioural change**
- example: the connection is declared in a `bunorm.ManagerRegistry` instead of opened directly. The registry validates the definition as it is built and is the one door the `db:*` commands resolve, so the operator running a migration and the repository resolving at first request reach one pool rather than two onto the same database; the `*bun.DB` the rest of the application holds is the registry's default manager, published under a name of its own, and closing the registry is what closes the pool. The retry configuration and the TLS opt-out travel in the provider definition. The credential keeps the marking `RegisterParameters` already gives it — this major names `MYSQL_PASSWORD` itself, so the registry is not asked to say it a second time
- example: each major's example application holds its schema in a database of its own rather than the one all three shared. The bunorm/migrate command family builds its migrator on bun's default bookkeeping tables and bun matches an applied migration by NAME, so three migration sets in one database would share one `bun_migrations` and the first set to land would answer for the others — a later major's `db:migrate` would report success having created nothing. The development mysql is still one container; the databases are created by an init script on a fresh volume and idempotently by the e2e harness on one that already exists

- example: the request-scoped service is the nomenclature's journal accumulator rather than a route that reports on itself. The event listener that follows a write records into it, and a middleware writes what it holds once the handler chain has returned — before the response is sent, so a caller that reads the journal on receipt of its 201 is not racing the write, and a journal write that fails fails the request instead of becoming a log line. The scope's own teardown remains the safety net for a request that never reached the middleware. Every entry now carries the request that caused it, and a request that changed several records costs one round trip rather than one per change. `/scoped/trail/demo` is gone: what it claimed about itself is now the journal row a real write leaves behind, which is a claim a response cannot fabricate
- example: every catalogue and directory write is recorded through `bunorm/audit` on the tables the application actually uses, so who changed which field, from what to what, is answerable after the fact for the products and the accounts rather than for a table kept for the purpose. The actor is the account the request authenticated as; the password column is recorded as changed with its value masked, because a trail that kept credentials is worse than no trail. `/database/audit/demo` and its `melody_example_products` table are gone, and so are `/database/demo` and `melody_example_secrets` — transparent encryption at rest is already carried by the two-factor enrollment, whose shared secret must be stored reversibly and is read back to verify every code
- example: a change to the nomenclature is announced on the websocket to every open page, emitted by the same listener that invalidates the cache and writes the journal entry, and the product list carries a bell that counts what arrived and reloads on click. The counter a ticker used to broadcast, the page built to watch it and `/websocket/demo` are gone: the socket now carries the application's own changes, and an end-to-end section proves two live clients are both told about one write
- example: the two-factor enrollment table carries the major, like every other table the application writes: `melody_example_v3_two_factor`. Three example applications share one database in development, and it was the one table whose name did not say which of them owned it. The audit trail's own transaction table keeps the integration's fixed `melody_audit_transaction` name, which is not per-major — only v3 records an audit trail today, so nothing collides, but two majors recording one would share it
- example: the routes and commands are named for what they do. `/platform/demo` is `/platform/check`, a readiness probe over the lock and the storage backend; `/messagebus/demo` is `/messagebus/dispatch`; `/encrypt/demo` is `/encrypt/roundtrip`; `/redis/token/demo` is `/redis/token/revocation`; `/storage/demo` is `/storage/object`; `example:grant:demo` is `example:grant:role`, `example:exclusive:demo` is `example:exclusive:tick` and `messagebus:demo` is `messagebus:dispatch`. `/cache/demo` is gone — it wrote a key and read it back while the real cache is the product listing, invalidated on every write and already read out of band by the harness

- logging: `LogOnRecover` only logs. It terminated the process when the recovered value carried an exit code, skipping every remaining deferred call, which is the one place in the framework where a library helper could end a program that had not asked to end. `LogOnRecoverAndExit` is the honestly named helper for that, and the two call sites that want it use it
- config: `Resolve()` reports an error once the application is serving. Resolution rewrites the parameter store, and by then every service holds values copied out of it, so a later pass cannot reconfigure anything that is running — it only rewrites the store under readers that expect it settled. Building a configuration by hand and resolving it before serving is unaffected
- config: a positive session ttl below one second fails the boot. Such a value does not describe a short session but a broken one: the file-backed store purges a lapsed entry on the same write that stores it, so a lifetime near the cost of that write returns success having persisted nothing, and one second is the finest unit an HTTP date can express in any case. Zero keeps its meaning of no expiry
- httpclient: the client sets `MaxIdleConnsPerHost` on its transport, exposed as `TransportConfig.MaxIdleConnsPerHost` and defaulting to `MaxIdleConns` (100), following an override of it unless pinned explicitly. It was never set, so `net/http` fell back to `DefaultMaxIdleConnsPerHost` (2) and the configured `MaxIdleConns: 100` was inert: since a `BaseUrl` means one client per upstream, a burst of 200 concurrent requests dialled 200 sockets, retained 2 and closed 198, each holding a `TIME_WAIT` entry for the 60-second MSL until the ephemeral port range was exhausted and every request failed with `connect: cannot assign requested address`, reported as `"request failed"` and indistinguishable from the upstream being down. **Behavioural change**: connection reuse against a single host now scales with `MaxIdleConns`; a caller who relied on the old two-connection ceiling must set `MaxIdleConnsPerHost` explicitly

- validation: a nil pointer embed is validated as "nothing was supplied" rather than "nothing to validate", so the constraints on its promoted fields run against their zero values exactly as a value embed's already did. A payload that mentioned no field of a `*T` embed previously skipped every constraint the embed promotes — naming any sibling field made `encoding/json` allocate the embed and re-arm them — so a body of `{"status":"open"}` on a request whose `*Audit` embed declares `ActorId` as `notBlank` passed validation and then dereferenced nil in the handler. **Behavioural change**: requests that relied on omitting the embed entirely are now rejected with the constraint errors the tags declare. A promoted field shadowed by an outer field of the same json name stays unvalidated, so the dominance rules are unchanged
- validation: exceeding the nesting-depth cap is reported as a validation error (`nestingDepthExceeded`) only when the truncated subtree could actually carry a `validate` tag, so nesting past the cap can no longer bypass validation while free-form tag-free client json is accepted up to the cap as before. An interface-typed member counts as possibly tag-bearing, its dynamic type being unknowable from the type alone: the walk descends into one and enforces the tags it finds, so clearing it statically let a payload nest past the cap and bypass them. **Behavioural change**: tag-free json reached through an interface — a `map[string]any` metadata field among it — is reported as `nestingDepthExceeded` past the cap rather than accepted at any depth. Past the cap the walk previously returned an empty error list, which `Validate` converted to `nil`, so nesting a payload one level deeper than the cap bypassed every constraint in it. The reachability check follows pointers, slices, arrays and map
  elements and is memoized per type; the cap value is unchanged. A member the payload sent as null or empty — a nil pointer or interface, a slice or map holding no element — is no longer reported at the cut: the walk returns no errors for it at any depth, so the error named a member that was never walked
- http: `JsonHandler` rejects a literal `null` request body with `400` when its request type is instantiated as a pointer. The four-byte body decoded without error and left the value nil, the validator took its nil-pointer early return and reported every constraint satisfied, and the handler then dereferenced nil. A value instantiation and a `{}` body were never affected, and a caller-supplied error responder still shapes the response. **Behavioural change**: that request is now a client error instead of a `500`

- http: a route pattern whose optional parameter is not its final segment is refused at registration instead of being accepted and half-served. An omitted optional is dropped wherever it sits in the pattern, while a match only ever ends early at the tail, so `/blog/:locale?/posts` let `UrlGenerator.GeneratePath` mint `/blog/posts` — a path the router matched with nothing and answered `404`; the canonical locale-prefix shape `/:locale?/blog` was broken the same way whenever the parameter carried no default, while a trailing optional worked correctly all along. A mid-pattern optional that carries a default is still accepted, because the default is always substituted and the segment is therefore never dropped. **Behavioural change**: an application registering a mid-pattern optional with no default now panics at the definition site with `optional route parameter must be the last pattern segment unless it has a default` instead of booting and emitting dead links — give the parameter a
  default, move it to the end, or register the long and short patterns as two routes

- cli: in json mode a command's standard output is the rendered document and nothing else — the ansi start/finish banner that `cli.Register` wraps around every registered command is suppressed, and `--format=json` implies `--no-color`. Nothing is lost: `meta` already reports the command, its arguments, the start time and the duration, and `error` reports the final status. **Behavioural change**: `jq` and `json.Unmarshal` now consume `debug:*` output directly instead of failing on the first byte; a consumer that scraped the banner off stdout must read `meta` instead
- cli: a command whose envelope reports an error exits non-zero. `output.Render` returns an exit-coded error after writing the envelope, so `debug:container <name>` fails when the service cannot be resolved instead of printing `[success]` and exiting `0` — a deployment gate such as `app debug:container app.repository.order || exit 1` now catches a broken container. A registered service that errors or panics while being constructed is reported as `debug.buildFailed` rather than `debug.notFound`, so the operator is not sent after a registration that is in fact present. **Behavioural change**: a command that reported an error in its payload while exiting `0` now exits `1`
- cli: `--format` and `--order` reject an unrecognised value instead of silently substituting the default. `--format=JSON`, `--format=yaml` and `--order=ascending` now fail during flag parsing with a message naming the accepted values and a non-zero exit, matching how `--limit=abc` already behaved, instead of quietly printing the human table. Omitting either flag still defaults to `table` and `asc`. **Behavioural change**: a script passing an unsupported value now fails instead of receiving the table

- http: the response path saves and advertises the session published on the request under `RequestAttributeSession` at the moment the response is written, rather than the one the kernel captured before routing. **Behavioural change**: replacing that attribute in a handler now takes effect

- cli: `--order=desc` reverses the listing in `debug:router`, `debug:events`, `debug:parameters`, `debug:middleware` and `debug:container`, applied before `--limit`/`--offset` so a descending window returns the end of the list rather than the beginning; `--order=asc` remains the default. **Behavioural change**: the flag was accepted and ignored before, so an invocation that passed it now gets different output
- cli: the `--fields` and `--sort` flags are withdrawn. No printer ever read them and no command ever sorted on a supplied key, so they are removed from the flag set, from `output.Option` and from the `meta.flags` block of the json envelope rather than left advertising a projection the document never received; `output.SplitFields` goes with them. **Behavioural change**: passing either flag now fails as an unknown flag instead of being silently ignored. **Breaking**: `output.Option` no longer has the `Fields` and `SortKey` fields, so a command that constructs that struct as a literal no longer compiles, and `output.SplitFields` is gone

- rate limit: the default key extractor now keys on the client IP alone instead of `ip:path`. `SimpleRateLimit(n)` is therefore a true per-IP budget of `n` requests per minute rather than `n` per IP per path — a client that varied the path could previously never trip the limit. **Behavioural change**: an application relying on the old per-path budgets must set a path-aware `KeyExtractor` explicitly. Both limiters also bound how many distinct keys they track (`SetMaxKeys`, default 1,000,000): once the map is full and an idle-entry prune frees nothing, a request under an unseen key is denied rather than growing the map without bound. The reclaim walk runs at most once per window, so a full limiter cannot be made to pay a whole-map scan for every request under an unseen key
- security: an access control rule whose attribute list normalizes to empty is refused at construction instead of being accepted. Such a rule still matched its path, so it granted every authenticated principal and shadowed any longer-prefixed rule that would have denied — a silent privilege downgrade from a variadic call with no attribute, or from a role that a configuration value resolved to empty. Blank attributes inside a non-empty list are still trimmed
- http: `TokenBucketLimiter` is now `FixedWindowLimiter`, with the old type name and both constructors kept as deprecated aliases so existing code is unaffected. The limiter restores the whole allowance at the window edge rather than proportionally to elapsed time, so it is a fixed-window counter and an instant straddling that edge admits up to twice the rate; the name now says so. `SlidingWindowLimiter`, which `IpRateLimit` and `UserRateLimit` already use, holds the rate over every trailing window
- security: `totp.OtpauthURI` is now `totp.OtpauthUri`, with the old spelling kept as a deprecated alias so existing code is unaffected
- http: the request attributes the kernel owns are reserved under the framework's own underscore prefix (`RequestAttributeSession` is `_session`, `RequestAttributeScheme` is `_scheme`) and are published after the route attributes, so a route attribute can no longer replace the session object or the resolved scheme. Every other framework attribute already carried that prefix. **Behavioural change**: code reading these attributes through the exported constants is unaffected; code that hardcoded the literal `"session"` must move to `RequestAttributeSession`

- cli: `--format=json` writes one document on one line, terminated by a newline, instead of an indented block. **Behavioural change**: the document is unchanged and `jq` reads either, but a consumer that counted lines or read the output as a single indented block sees the new shape; `--format=json-pretty` is the indented one from now on. Indenting by default made every document many lines, which every line-framed consumer — a log collector's json stage, a `while read line`, a `grep '\"failed\": true'` — receives as fragments that each fail to parse
- cli: text of server or client origin reaching the terminal is escaped rather than obeyed. The start and finish banners embed the command's own error, which routinely echoes downstream values, and the table cells carry whatever the command was reporting: an embedded carriage return or escape sequence could repaint the status line as another verdict, or forge lines in a captured log
- httpclient: the response body cap bounds `RequestStream` too, the inherited default of ten mebibytes included — the cap was completely inert on the streaming path: a caller who asked for ten bytes was handed everything the server sent, and a caller who never named a cap streamed unbounded behind a bounded contract. **Behavioural change**: a stream that legitimately carries more than the default names its own cap through `WithMaxResponseBodyBytes`; the body past the cap is refused with the cap, the method and the sanitized url in the record

- messagebus: the `Transport` contract's `Close` takes no runtime and answers only the error — `Close() error`. **Breaking** at compile time for a userland transport, and the fix is deleting the parameter: the old `Close(runtime) error` was structurally dead, because the container's ordered teardown recognizes `Close() error` and nothing else and no production caller existed anywhere, so every transport lived exactly as long as the process and every deploy tore its broker connection down abruptly
- messagebus: a consumed message whose type has no registered handler fails the dispatch instead of passing through with a warning, and `HandleOptions.RequireHandler` is replaced by its inverse, `AllowMissingHandler`. **Breaking**: on the consume path the old pass-through was immediately Acked, so a forgotten registration — or the pointer-vs-value keying trap the package document calls out — drained a production queue one warning at a time while the retry and dead-letter machinery, built precisely to never lose a message, never engaged; a bus that genuinely wants pass-through opts in
- mailer: configured smtp credentials fail closed when the server does not advertise `AUTH`, whatever `RequireAuth` says. **Behavioural change**: the old default skipped the whole auth branch and delivered the message as anonymous submission while reporting success — the common trigger being a relay that only advertises `AUTH` after `STARTTLS`, on a session where tls was not negotiated — so the operator's configured identity went quietly unused on exactly the deployments that thought they had set it
- translation: a plain placeholder whose parameter is ABSENT renders as the visible placeholder itself instead of as an empty string. **Behavioural change**: rendered empty, a renamed parameter key shipped every message quietly missing its amount, name or count, with no channel through which anyone learns of it — the visible `{name}` is the observable form, while a parameter present with an explicit nil value still renders empty because the caller said so
- security: `PutWithTtl` on the in-memory token store refuses a non-positive ttl instead of storing the token forever. The likeliest caller of a ttl that is zero or negative computed a remaining lifetime that had already elapsed, and the silent fallback to the never-expires sentinel stored exactly the token that was asked to expire with no expiry at all — the precise inversion of the request. **Behavioural change**: a caller that relied on `ttl <= 0` meaning "no expiry" must call `Put`, which is that contract's name

- http: a urlencoded body whose read or parse failed is refused before the handler — 413 when the size limit stopped the read, 400 for a body the client broke — the way the json binding path refuses the identical condition. **Behavioural change**: the failure used to be a warning beside a form the parse never populated, so the handler saw a syntactically valid request whose form was simply empty and answered 200 — "field missing" about a field the client sent — and an oversized submission was processed as an empty one
- http: a session-storage outage on the save path answers 500 rather than the response the handler produced, with the session cookie suppressed either way. **Behavioural change**: the handler wrote to the session and returned success on the assumption the write would land — a login answering "welcome" with the identity never stored, an attempt counter that never grows while the backend is down, which is fail-open exactly under attack — and the client could not tell the difference; the delete path is unchanged, still expiring the cookie whatever the backend answered
- http: two routes identical on everything the matcher discriminates — pattern, methods, host, schemes, locales, requirements and priority — are refused at registration, because the later one could never be dispatched: the tie falls to the first registered, and the second was shadowed silently by the single registration channel with no collision handling. **Behavioural change**: a boot that carried such a pair now fails naming the pattern, the methods and the route name
- bag, http: a request parameter that appeared once is stored as the string it is, a genuinely repeated one stays a string slice, and reading the repeated one as a single string is refused loudly toward `StringSlice`/`StringAt` instead of answered with a guess; `Request.Input` answers the first value of a repeated key, as `FormValue` beside it and `url.Values.Get` already do. **Behavioural change**: `bag.String` used to answer `("", true)` for every list — `url.Values` carries even a single occurrence as one — so `Input` returned the empty string for ANY present query or post parameter, `StringOrDefault` skipped its default, and `HasNonEmptyString` lied; only route parameters worked
- http: a handler-returned failure files one record at the level it deserves — a deliberate 4xx is a refusal recorded at warning, a client's own cancellation is named for what it is at warning, everything else keeps the error level — and the record marks the error, so the exception listener attaches its request coordinates to it instead of filing the same failure a second time. **Behavioural change**: every handler failure used to produce two error records, the first at error even for a routine 404 or 429, so a flood the rate limiter was correctly refusing read as an error storm and an operator counting incidents counted double
- http: a response status outside net/http's `[100, 999]` is refused by name on both write paths — the public `WriteToHttpResponseWriter` answers the error its signature promises before anything is written, the internal path logs and answers the rendered 500 — because handing the code to `WriteHeader` panicked deep inside the response path; on the recovery path that panic re-entered the writer, read the commit flag the failed `WriteHeader` had already raised, classified the response as a committed stream and skipped the write, so the client received an implicit empty 200 for a caller's arithmetic mistake
- http: a header key the response names is owned by the response: the writer's values for it are replaced rather than appended to, so a header both sides set — the request id the kernel puts on the raw writer among them — reaches the client once instead of twice
- http: `Router.Match` hands out a deep copy of the winning route's attributes instead of the registry's own map, which was alive for every request of the process: a sort or an append through the match result — or through `request.Attributes()`, where the kernel publishes these values — rewrote the route table with no lock
- http: `AllowedMethods` applies the locale filter the matcher applies, through the one `matchesLocale` both now share, so the method list announced for a path no longer names the methods of locale-scoped routes that path can never reach
- http: the response the terminate event and the access log report is the one the client received: a stream that committed its own headers records the committed status instead of the synthetic 204 the kernel substituted, and a rendered-but-never-written 500 after a mid-stream panic no longer lands in the journal as the served status — status-distribution queries over the access log were wrong for every streaming route; a hijacked connection records no status and keeps the substitute
- **Breaking** http: the kernel's configuration doors and the router's registration doors are BOOT-ONLY and now refuse rather than corrupt. `Use`, `SetNotFoundHandler`, `SetErrorHandler`, `SetForwardedHeadersPolicy`, `SetSessionCookiePolicy`, `SetMethodPolicy` and every route registration write state that each request goroutine afterwards reads without synchronization, so a call made once `ServeHttp` has built the handler was a data race — and for the route tree's maps a concurrent write is an unrecoverable fatal error, not a torn read. Configuration is compiled once and then served; the refusal is raised on the calling goroutine, naming the door. The READING doors stay open, because the route table is introspected from inside handlers — that is how the openapi document and the route manifest are served
- **Breaking** http: a route is matched on the path as the client SPELLED it, through `URL.EscapedPath`, with each segment unescaped after the split. Matching the decoded path made `%2F` a segment separator, so `/admin%2Fusers` — one segment naming a resource called `admin/users` — became two and reached the `/admin/users` handler, while a proxy or WAF rule written against the raw request line matched neither spelling. A parameter can now legitimately carry an encoded slash
- **Breaking** http: `matchesHost` compares host names the way the header is defined — case-insensitively, and with the port significant only when the route declared one. A route bound to `example.com` was unreachable behind any non-default port, which is the whole of local development and every non-443 deployment, and the request fell through to a broader route on the same pattern or to a 404 with nothing recorded. A route bound to `example.com:8443` still matches only that port
- **Breaking** http: route defaults are merged BEFORE the locale gate reads them, in `Match` and in `AllowedMethods` alike. A route declaring `Locales{"en", "de"}` beside `Defaults{"_locale": "en"}` was unreachable by every url — the gate rejected it before the value meant to satisfy it was filled in — while the kernel read the same map after the merge, so the router and the kernel disagreed about whether a default counted as the request locale
- container: the teardown breaks a tie the dependency graph leaves open on **creation order, latest first**, not on the node key descending. The string comparison decided by nothing but spelling — a worker named `app.worker` lost its shutdown records while the same worker renamed `zz.worker` kept them — while creation order carries the only causal claim this container has: a service built during the construction of another was needed by it, whether or not the edge was declared, and a logger resolved at boot is beneath everything resolved afterwards. The scope's teardown applies the same tie-break, because the two share one walk; a node with no recorded creation closes last, where the key keeps deciding exactly as before
- container: closing has two states, and a service's own `Close` may still resolve. The closed flag is raised before the first service `Close` runs and refuses every new CREATION for the whole teardown; `teardownFinished` is raised after the last one returns and refuses every RESOLUTION from then on. Between them the memoized instances are still served, because a worker reporting its drain at `Close` is entitled to the logger it reports through — which is the whole reason the logger is closed last — while after them a resolution used to answer a closed instance with a nil error saying it was fine
- container: a closed container refuses new registrations and new overrides the way its scoped registrar always has. `Register` accepted silently, reporting success for a service whose every resolution the creation guard then refuses; an override landing after `Close` enumerated the instances was stored into a map no teardown will ever read again — served by later lookups and closed by nobody. The READ paths keep serving what was built, so a shutdown-racing request degrades gracefully instead of half-working. **Behavioural change**
- container: an override must fit every type its name is registered under. The override propagates to every type-keyed resolution of the name with no re-check at read time — the call-time assignability guard of the provider contract does not see overrides — so an incompatible value poisoned `GetByType` and the name and type maps learned two different answers. It is refused before anything is written, naming the registered type and the value's; the guard judges the value the way the readers will, raw assignability for an interface registration and canonical identity for a value-typed one, whose canonical key already holds raw values built by the provider itself. **Behavioural change**
- container: a typed-nil provider function is refused where it is registered, at the one gate all three registration paths go through. `var f containercontract.Provider[T]` handed in uninitialized passes the callers' own `nil == provider` checks as a signature-valid function that panics on its first call, so boot reported success for a service every resolution of which fails. **Behavioural change** (fail-fast at boot)
- container: two DIFFERENT types that share one identity key refuse to coexist, at the container door and the scoped one alike. The identity key of a pointer-to-unnamed-composite type drops its package path — `*[]alpha.Bus` and `*[]beta.Bus` of two same-short-named packages read identically — so one creation-guard key and one close node stood for two types: a resolution of one read as a cycle through the other, and the teardown merged what it should order. The second, different type is refused at the boot line that declares it
- container: `Has` and `HasType` answer under the same suspension `Get` enforces. They consulted the scope on `nil != scopeInstance` while every resolution beside them reads `scopeVisible()`, so a container-owned provider — whose scope is suspended for the duration of its construction, because a process-lifetime singleton must not be assembled out of one request's values — was told "yes" about the very scope-only entries its own `Get` would refuse. An existence check that disagrees with the resolution it gates is a wiring decision made on one request's substitutes, or a `Has`-then-`MustGet` panic. The scope's own `TypesImplementing` two lines below already read `scopeVisible()`, so the package held both readings at once and the stale one was on the public door. **Behavioural change**
- container: the protected `service.` namespace holds at both lifetimes. The override path refuses to substitute a `service.` name, and a scoped registration of the same name — with `Replacing()` — performed exactly that substitution inside every scope, where the kernel resolves through: the framework's own singleton was silently replaced for the whole life of the process, once per request, with nothing refused anywhere. Both scoped registration doors — `Container.RegisterScoped` and `Scope.RegisterScoped` — now refuse the prefix outright, with or without `Replacing()`. **Behavioural change**: an application registering a scoped service under `service.` fails at boot; the container-lifetime registration of such a name is unaffected
- container: `Scope.RegisterScoped` refuses a closed container, the answer the other two registration doors already gave. The third door reported SUCCESS for a service whose every later resolution the creation guard then refuses, so a registration made during shutdown looked like it had landed and produced a refusal at each use instead. The flag is read under the read lock the block already takes, so the documented container-then-scope lock ordering is untouched. **Behavioural change**
- container: a scope override is answered by the type-keyed resolutions of every type its name is registered under, exactly as the container-level override propagates. Filed under the canonical type of the VALUE alone, a scope override left the name answering the override while `GetByType` answered the container's instance whenever the container had already memoized it — one request seeing two different services under one registration. The propagation covers the container's registrations, the scope plan's and the scope's own, and it writes only into the scope, so nothing about it reaches the container the scope layers over. **Behavioural change**
- container: the override assignability guard judges the value the way the readers will, on the scope as well as on the container. The container-level door already refused a value a registered type cannot hold; the scope's did not, so a scoped override of an incompatible type was admitted and then poisoned every type-keyed resolution that read it. The rule is the same at both levels: raw assignability for an interface registration, whose stored value is asserted against the interface at resolution, and canonical identity for a value-typed registration. The refusal happens before anything is written, so the name and type maps never learn two different answers. **Behavioural change**
- container: a lazy handle follows the scope of the resolver it was built over instead of memoizing forever. `container.Lazy` memoizes its first successful resolution, and a handle built over a request scope was serving that request's state — its per-request logger, its request context, whatever was overridden into it — to every later caller for the life of the process. The handle now asks the resolver whether it has closed, and the first closed answer makes it terminal: the scope-is-closed error on that call and on every later one, with the value, the closure and the resolver dropped so nothing keeps a path to the dead request. A resolver that cannot answer the question is read as OPEN, exactly as the exit handler reads a logger that cannot — so a handle built over the container, which is the ordinary case, is untouched. `Scope.Closed()` is the door the question is asked through; it lives on the concrete scope rather than on the contract, and a caller holding a bare `Resolver` or `Scope` reaches it through an interface assertion. **Behavioural change**
- httpclient: `buildUrl` resolves the target against the base url by RFC 3986 reference resolution — the rule Symfony and Guzzle implement — instead of joining the base as a prefix: an absolute-path target replaces the base path entirely, a relative one merges over the last segment of the base path, an empty target names the base resource WITH its trailing slash, and the network-path form (`//host/x`) takes the base scheme onto the host it names. Because the merge cuts the last segment of a base spelled without its trailing slash, such a base is refused by panic at `NewHttpClientConfig` and `SetBaseUrl` — the mistake falls at wiring instead of running as a 404 in production; a base with an empty path stays legal. The foreign-origin refusal is judged on the RESOLVED url, which covers the network-path reference no spelling test can see and makes the released majors' case-sensitivity defect — `HTTP://` read as a relative path — impossible by construction. A relative target on a client with NO base url is refused by name, where it used to fail inside `net/http` as `failed to create request` with the cause unnamed. **Breaking**: a base url whose path lacks its trailing slash panics at construction, and an absolute-path target under a base with a path now names a different resource than the old prefix join did
- httpclient: the eight `TransportConfig` fields become pointers (`*time.Duration`/`*int`): nil means "not set" and falls back to the default beside it in `DefaultTransportConfig`, and a SET value reaches `net/http` VERBATIM — so the meanings zero and negative carry there become expressible for the first time: `MaxIdleConns` zero is an unbounded pool, `IdleConnTimeout` or `ResponseHeaderTimeout` zero waits forever, a negative `KeepAlive` disables the dialer's probes. Under the old non-positive sentinel every one of those spellings silently became the default beside it. The per-host rule is unchanged — `MaxIdleConnsPerHost` follows an override of `MaxIdleConns` unless pinned. **Breaking**: a `TransportConfig` literal spells its values through `TransportDuration`/`TransportCount`, and a zero-valued field that used to mean "inherit the default" now has to be LEFT nil to mean it
- httpclient: header maps are canonicalized at the door and two spellings that collapse onto one header are refused by name — the serializer's convention for its mime keys. The maps are applied to the request with `Set`, which canonicalizes, so `x-api-key` and `X-Api-Key` in one map stayed two entries whose survivor was chosen by map iteration order — a different value per request, in what is often a credential header — and a rotation spelled differently from the configured key left two live entries. `NewHttpClientConfig` and `RequestOptions.SetHeaders` refuse a colliding map at the door; `HttpClient.SetHeader` and `RequestOptions.SetHeader` store the canonical spelling, so the last sequential write wins deterministically. **Behavioural change**
- httpclient: `RequestOptions.Headers` and `Query` hand out copies. The live map invited writes that bypass the canonicalization `SetHeader` exists to enforce, and a non-canonical spelling planted through the getter next to the canonical one made the request-time winner a map-iteration choice; the setters remain the one door that writes. **Behavioural change**

- debug: `NewMiddlewareCommand` takes a description provider and a build provider, where it took one provider returning the built chain. Listing the pipeline meant building it — every factory run, every dependency resolved — as the price of a table, and the inactive entries the selection dropped could not be reported at all because a built chain does not carry them. An application wiring the command by hand hands in `describe` and `buildForInspection` instead of the single builder. **Breaking**
- debug: `debug:container` and `debug:middleware` describe by default and build only under `--build`. The bare `debug:container` listing reports names, lifetimes, built state and the provider's declared return type from the registration records without running a single provider, where it used to call `Get` on every name — on a production wiring that dialled every pool, opened every connection and built every singleton as the price of a listing. Naming a single service still resolves it. The bare `debug:middleware` listing describes the pipeline the build would run, with the inactive entries carrying their reasons. **Behavioural change**
- debug: the `--build` sweep reports its failures on the envelope, so `app debug:container --build --format=json || exit 1` fails a deployment gate the way the single-service door always has — `error.code = "debug.buildFailed"`, the failure count and the failed names in the details, the first failure as the cause. `errorCauseChain` and `errorContextJson` keep one json type across every row of one document, so `jq` reads the whole listing. **Behavioural change**
- debug: `debug:version` reads the version rows off the envelope meta, where `NewMeta` has already applied the fallback chain — the command's explicit value, then the process-wide declaration made through `output.SetApplicationVersion`, then nothing. Reading the command's own field directly meant an application that declared its version process-wide, which is where a composition root declares it, saw an empty application row. **Behavioural change**

- cache: `NewManager` builds a manager that does NOT own its backend — `Close` leaves the backend open. On the container path both are registered services and the container closes each one itself, so the cascade closed the backend twice, which a backend wrapping a connection typically reports as a failure on the second call and turns a clean shutdown into a reported one. **Breaking**; `NewManagerOwningBackend` keeps the cascade
- cache: the key grammar is stated on the `cachecontract.Backend` contract — non-empty, no spaces, no newlines, at most 1024 bytes — and the in-memory backend enforces it with the same refusals the redis backend always answered. Two implementations of one promise refused different keys, so a key built from user input worked in development and failed against the production store on every operation. The order of the refusals is shared too: a closed backend answers the closed refusal before the key or the ttl is judged, and a batch write judges the ttl before its keys. **Breaking**
- cache: the in-memory backend refuses four degenerate inputs it used to absorb. The empty key answers `cache key is empty` on every operation, where it used to be a real key that every caller whose key came up empty silently shared. A negative ttl on `Set`/`SetMultiple` answers `cache ttl is negative`, where it used to store an immortal entry — the exact opposite of a ttl computed from an already-passed deadline; zero keeps meaning no expiry. `Increment`/`Decrement` over an existing empty or blank payload answer `cache value is not a valid int64` the way any textual payload does, where it used to be adopted as a zero counter and overwritten, destroying the marker. A negative `maxItems` panics at construction, where it used to mean unbounded and disarm the eviction the operator believed was armed. **Breaking**
- cache: an existing counter payload is parsed against the redis integer grammar rather than Go's lenient one — an optional minus, a first digit of 1-9 unless the whole number is the single digit `0`, and nothing else. Whitespace padding, a plus sign and leading zeros used to increment through this backend and error through redis, and the parity the contract claims has to hold spelling by spelling. **Breaking**
- cache: `Remember` coalesces concurrent callers only for pointer-kind `Cache` implementations, whose address tells two instances apart. A value-kind implementation used to share one flight per TYPE: two instances over different backends collapsed onto one leader and the second caller received the value computed for somebody else's cache. A value-kind instance now runs every callback itself, which costs the stampede optimization and never the answer. **Breaking**
- cache: the zero-value `&RememberOption{}` reads as `NewDefaultRememberOption()`. Its fields are unexported, so a literal is the only option an external caller can build without the constructor — and it silently disarmed the stampede protection it never asked to configure while its zero wait made every miss answer an instant timeout. A deliberate protection-off option built through the constructor carries a non-zero shape and stays what it says. **Breaking**
- cache: a `waitTimeout` of zero means no WAITING rather than no answer — a result the in-flight call has already memoized is taken without blocking, and only a flight still in the air answers with the timeout. The previous form returned the timeout error without ever consulting the flight, so it refused a result that was already sitting there. **Breaking**
- cache: `Remember` answers one shape on the miss and on every hit — the computing call returns the value passed through the manager's serializer round-trip, the exact shape every cached call answers. One key used to have two shapes, so a type assertion worked on the cold path and failed on the warm one from the second call on. The loss is now uniform rather than deferred: an integer beyond 2^53 comes back changed on the computing call as well. **Breaking**
- serializer: a range carrying `q=0` refuses the types it covers rather than being ignored, and `ErrNotAcceptable` is answered only when every registered type is refused. `Accept: application/json;q=0` against a manager that also holds plain text is answered with plain text, where it used to be a 406 — and the fallback steps past json when json is what was refused, so the negotiation can no longer serve the very type the client rejected. **Breaking**
- serializer: a manager deliberately configured without `application/json` answers an empty or unmatched accept header with its first configured serializer in lexical mime order, where it used to refuse with `no default serializer configured` while a serializer sat beside the refusal — and in the real chain the result handler swallowed that refusal and served HARDCODED json, exactly the representation the configuration had excluded. **Breaking**
- serializer: the accept header is read on the one strict grammar the http readers already shared — `internal.ParseQualityValue` for `q` and `internal.SplitOutsideQuotes` for members and parameters. A malformed `q` had three fates depending on its spelling: `q=abc` scored as full acceptance, `q=-1` clamped into a fabricated refusal, `q=NaN` rode through both clamps as a weight no comparison could select or refuse. It now drops its whole member, neither an acceptance nor a refusal. And a comma inside a quoted parameter value no longer splits its member, so the `q=0` in `text/plain;version="1,2";q=0` keeps covering the type it names instead of being detached from it and the refused type served. **Breaking** — `q=1e-1` and `q=1.5`, functional until now, lose their member
- serializer: `NewSerializerManager` refuses a map in which two spellings collapse onto one normalized key, naming the normalized key and both spellings in sorted order. The overwrite was silent and map iteration order decided the survivor, so the winning serializer could change from one boot to the next with the loser dropped without a word. **Breaking**

### Removed

- config: the unexported `loadRequiredDotEnvFile` is gone — a required-file loader with its own two diagnostics that nothing has called on any major, drifting beside the optional loader that does the work.
- cli: the dead unexported error presenter is gone — an error-printing path with sorted context and a bounded cause chain that nothing has called since the envelope renderer became the one presentation, drifting beside the live path it duplicated
- application: `HttpTimeoutConfiguration` and `HttpShutdownConfiguration` are removed. They were dead API: the configuration melody builds implements neither, nothing could inject one, and the branch that read them would have applied their zero values verbatim to the server — a silent disarming of every per-request limit — had it ever become reachable. The per-request limits stay fixed in this major and the shutdown wait becomes configurable through `MELODY_HTTP_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT`. **Breaking**

- debug: `debug.MiddlewareProvider`, replaced by the two providers named in `Added`. It could report the built chain and nothing else, which is why the listing had to build. **Breaking**

- session: `NewManager` does not close the storage it was handed. A storage handed in was built by someone else and is closed by whoever built it — which on the container path is the container, so a manager that closed it too closed it twice, and a storage wrapping a connection typically reports the second call as a failure and turns a clean shutdown into a reported one. `NewManagerOwningStorage` is the constructor that keeps the cascade. **Behavioural change**
- session: `Manager.SaveSession` and `Manager.DeleteSession` hold a lock keyed to the session id across the storage call rather than one lock for the whole manager. The tombstone check and the write are still one critical section for the SAME session, which is the invariant that stops a logout being undone; two requests acting on different sessions no longer wait on each other for the length of a storage round trip. A userland `Storage` may therefore now be entered concurrently for different session ids — both storages melody ships already took a mutex of their own. **Behavioural change**
- session: burying a tombstone prunes only the burials that have lapsed, walking them in the order they happened, instead of sweeping the whole record on every call. Every tombstone is held for the same window, so the burials lapse in the order they happened and the walk stops at the first one still inside it; the whole-map sweep cost a step per remembered deletion on every login and every logout, with the manager's lock held for all of it
- session: the in-memory storage sweeps expired sessions in chunks, releasing the lock between them, and reads each entry again under the lock the chunk holds — the ids were listed before the lock was released, so a session saved again in between must not be signed out on the strength of the first reading
- http: a session another request ended while this one was running is recorded at **warning**, under its own name, and the response path expires the browser cookie and serves the handler's response unchanged. Filed at `error` beside the two genuine storage failures, a user logging out in one tab produced one indistinguishable "failed to save session" per concurrent request against a perfectly healthy backend. The three session-persistence records carry the request method and the path beside the one-way reference to the session id. **Behavioural change**

### Fixed

- event: the `EventDispatcherAdapter` hands a listener the ORIGINAL dispatched event, not a reconstruction of it, so a custom event type stays type-assertable and a field a listener writes is seen by the caller. The adapter wrapped every listener to receive an event rebuilt from name, payload and timestamp, discarding the concrete type and every non-propagation field — the same listener saw a different object through the adapter than through the plain dispatcher, though the two are drop-in replacements for one contract
- session: the file storage's handle-owning path keeps the in-memory state when a truncate or sync fails AFTER the new document was already written. The write lays the whole snapshot at offset 0 and a single decode reads exactly it, so a later flush-step failure leaves the file holding the new data; rolling the in-memory entry back made it disagree with the disk — a "failed" save whose data was persisted, or a "failed" delete that had already removed the session — so the rollback is skipped for a failure the write already outran
- bag: reading a present non-string scalar (an int, a bool) as a string reports it ABSENT rather than present-but-empty, so `StringOrDefault` substitutes the default and `HasNonEmptyString` answers false. Returning `("", true)` defeated the default-fallback contract the sibling accessors keep, and was asymmetric with the loud panic a string slice already earns
- wiring: the generator's collision key canonicalizes a returned type the way the container does — a value type `T` and its pointer `*T` register under one name — so a package declaring both a `func() Foo` and a `func() *Foo` is refused at generation instead of passing and panicking at the second `Register` on boot; the key's own comment had claimed the container derives different names for the two
- wiring: a scoped constructor whose name or type the container also registers is emitted with `WithReplacesContainerService()`, so the deliberate scoped-shadows-container shape the generator's own comment describes boots instead of being refused by the container at the first registration
- container: a `LazyService` built over the container turns terminal after the container closes instead of serving the dead service. Its liveness probe asked for `Closed() bool`, which only a scope carries — the container spells it `IsClosed()` — so a handle that memoized a value went on answering with it after `Close` had torn the service down, and kept the value pinned for the handle's life. The probe now recognises the container's spelling and the resolver context reports its scope, so a memoized handle turns terminal the way the contract already promised
- container: the live `RegisterScoped` door refuses a type whose identity key another scoped type already claimed, the way the container and the boot scope plan already refuse it. Two distinct pointer-to-unnamed types from same-short-named packages fold onto one identity key, so a colliding pair registered on a live scope shared one teardown node and one creation-guard key — one instance's `Close` was skipped and a resolution of one read as a cycle through the other
- container: an instance override no longer poisons the type-keyed resolution of an unrelated service. The override was written under the override value's own canonical type even when that type belonged to a different service's registration, so overriding one name answered that other service's `GetByType` with the unrelated value; the override now reaches only the types the overridden NAME is registered under, and the scope exposes the value's own type only when no service claims it
- container: a teardown that fails to close two override-evicted or replaced instances now reports both. The failure map keyed those instances under one constant node key, so the second failing close overwrote the first's record and the close error named one failure where two happened
- config: a runtime parameter registered before boot with a template refuses to be read until the boot pass resolves it, instead of handing a reader the raw `%env(...)%` as its value. Such a registration is marked deferred — the same refusal a `.env` parameter with an unsettled reference gets — and the boot pass resolves it and clears the flag
- config: `MarkSecret` reaches a reader that referenced the `kernel.*` alias of a `MELODY_*` key. The post-resolve propagation scanned templates by the marked name alone, so a value assembled from a secret through the other spelling of the same parameter stayed unmarked and printed in full beside the redacted key; the scan now seeds and extends through every alias the parameter answers to
- config: the reserved-prefix boot refusal names the same environment key every run. It walked the environment map unsorted and blamed a different one of two offending keys each time, so an operator who fixed one saw the same failure return under a new name; the keys are sorted, the way `resolveAll` and the dot-env expansion already sort
- http: `FileServerConfig.SetExcludedPathList` normalizes an entry without a leading slash to the shape that matches. An excluded path is compared with a prefix test against a request path, which always begins with a slash, so an entry like `admin` could never match and silently excluded nothing; the configuration door already refuses such an entry
- application: the container logger closes the log-file descriptor it opened when arming the rotation signal watcher fails. The descriptor was left open before the panic, owned by nobody — the container stores only what a provider returns, and a creation failure is not memoized — so each later resolution would have opened another
- security: a security token-source resolution failure is filed once. The listener logged it and then dispatched it on `kernel.exception` without marking it logged, so the exception listener filed a second full record for the one failure; it is marked now, the discipline the kernel's own writers keep and the frozen majors already keep here
- cli: the `command not found` header escapes the argv-derived command name, the way the run banners and the suggestion table beside it already do, so an embedded carriage return or escape sequence cannot repaint the header as another verdict
- cli: the table printer measures, pads and wraps in terminal display cells instead of runes, through `internal.DisplayWidth`. A CJK ideogram, a fullwidth form or an emoji occupies two cells and a combining mark none, so a rune count rendered exactly those cells out of line with the separators it had measured — a column holding `世界` closed its pipe two cells past every other row's. The width table is deliberately the principal East Asian Wide and Fullwidth blocks with ambiguous-width characters counted narrow, which is the convention of terminals outside legacy CJK locales, and the wrap takes at least one rune per line so a rune wider than its whole column cannot wrap forever
- application: the process context of a cli run and the unknown-command suggestion envelope take their instants from the kernel's clock instead of `time.Now()`, so a frozen-clock test reads the same start instant everywhere the run's identity travels and the suggestion envelope's duration is measured on the clock the rest of the process answers. The signal watcher's escalation debounce deliberately stays on the process clock: it measures the spacing of real deliveries, which a frozen clock would misread
- validation: `greaterThan` and `lessThan` judge a float-typed field against the declared bound exactly, at every magnitude. The bound was converted to `float64` at the moment of comparison, and a bound above 2^53 is not exactly representable there — it rounds to a neighbour up to one ULP away, so a float value adjacent to such a bound was misjudged against the declared number: a field holding `9007199254740996.0` was refused by `greaterThan` with bound `9007199254740995`, which rounds to `9007199254740996`. The integer and unsigned branches always compared exactly; the openapi document already advertised the declared bound verbatim, so the published spec and the validator now agree on these values instead of disagreeing by one ULP
- cache: the in-memory backend's expiry sweep enumerates the keys under the read lock rather than the exclusive one. The deletions were already chunked with the lock released between chunks, but the snapshot itself held the write lock while walking the entire map, so once per tick every concurrent `Get` stalled for as long as the map was large — for an enumeration that only reads
- example: the error presenter reads every `Accept` line and answers a header that refuses everything with 406. `Header.Get` returns only the first line of a repeated field while `Accept` is list-typed, so a refusal a client sent on a second line vanished; and the presenter tested only `nil == err`, so a header refusing every available media type got a 406 for a success and a JSON body for a 403 or a 500 — the very representation it had refused — because the negotiation refusal reached the error path through a second door. Both doors now behave as the result handler already does on the success path
- example: the entry point and the access-denied handler decide html through `melodyhttp.PrefersHtml` instead of their own `isHtmlRequest`, which read the first `Accept` line only and matched `text/html` with a substring search, so it ignored q-values, ignored a refusal, and answered html to a client that had asked for anything containing that token. The local helper is deleted
- example: `service.CacheKeyUserByUsername` folds the username itself. Five copies of `strings.ToLower(strings.TrimSpace(...))` — the service that fills the entry, the three listeners that drop it, and the repository — spelled the fold out beside the call, so one of them drifting would have written the entry under one key and cleared it under another, leaving a renamed or deleted user served from the cache. `repository.NormalizedUsername` is exported as the one fold, and the three listeners ask one helper whether there is a name left to key on
- example: the admin-versus-admin rule is asked once. Both the user update and the user delete door spelled the same security question out, differing only in the words they refuse with, so the two could drift apart on who is protected; `protectsAnotherAdmin` is the single question both call
- example: the health handler stamps its response from the injected clock rather than `time.Now()` — it was the one handler of an application whose own timing middleware exists to show why the clock is injected
- example: the product create request refuses an id containing whitespace. The id becomes a cache key component and the backend grammar refuses a space or a newline inside a key, so a spelling the grammar refuses used to land in the database and fail every later cache write. The pattern is written in the ECMA-262 subset `OPENAPI.md` requires, because this tag reaches the published document as a `pattern` facet
- documentation: `ROADMAP.md` no longer lists as near- or mid-term three capabilities this major has shipped — the static server's filesystem and embedded modes, the firewall system, and the router's named routes, url generation, grouping and constraints. What they do is described where a current capability belongs, under `.documentation/package/`
- documentation: `DOCUMENTATION.md` carries the five passages it was behind on — the archetype-free documents, the lowercase exception for `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/`, the rule that an integration module documents itself in its own readme, the clause that the upgrade guide covers only releases made since it was introduced, and the `Exported API` exception the command-surface documents take — and names its own `README.md` the **module** root rather than the repository root, which is what `../README.md` resolves to from a major's documentation directory

- application: a teardown failure can no longer go unreported when two closes of the same application race each other. Only the claim's winner now enters the container: when every racing sibling called the container's `Close` too, whichever sibling arrived FIRST ran the actual teardown — the container serializes on its own once — and when that first arrival was a claim loser, the winner then read the closedness probe as "somebody else's close" and suppressed the report, so the single failure was returned by nobody and an exit path gated on it proceeded over a failed teardown. A losing sibling now waits for the performer's whole teardown instead, which also makes the probe's answer mean only a close that genuinely came from outside the application. The same repair lands on v1 and v2 in this commit — the site is byte-identical there and the fix is patch-sized
- example: the eagerly opened redis client is finally closed at teardown, through the rueidis module's new `Connection` owner service: the raw client's `Close` returns nothing, so registered alone it could never join the container's ordered teardown, and it lived exactly as long as the process — the same ownership class the amqp connections were repaired for. The composition root wraps the client with `NewConnection` and hands both to the module; the first client-backed service a run resolves (the token store on the auth path, the cache backend on the first cache read, the locker) records the edge that closes the connection after it
- example: the amqp connections of the message bus and of the outbox are finally closed at teardown, by giving the transports only a DIALER. A transport closes a connection it dialed itself and nothing else, so the connection each factory opened eagerly and handed over was owned by nobody and lived exactly as long as the process; the outbox transport additionally moves from a private object of the relay factory to a registered service (`service.outbox.transport`) — the shape the outbox module's own documentation prescribes — so its `Close() error` joins the container's ordered teardown once the relay has resolved it. The message bus keeps its boot-time fail-fast on a bad DSN as a dial PROBE that is closed at once
- openapi: a field whose rule set rejects every value is listed `required`, because the validator validates the absent zero value through the same refused rule — a payload omitting the field is rejected exactly like one carrying it. An unsatisfiable field left optional was the one fail-open corner of the mirror: a spec-driven client omitting it produced a payload the document calls valid and the server refuses. The same holds one level down for a non-pointer field whose referenced component a poisoned embed made unsatisfiable — the zero struct runs the embed's tag — while a pointer field stays optional there, its nil skipping the descent; a component unsatisfiable only transitively, through a required contradictory property of its own, is not recognized, that being the full satisfiability question rather than a stamped form
- openapi: the lockstep with the validator is held by an executable proof instead of by comments. the oracle lives in `schema_test.go`, beside the mirror it holds in step, and that is the only file in the tree where `openapi` reaches the `validation` package — a test-only import — and its oracle is semantic, over values: every tag class crossed with every field shape goes through the real generator on one side and `validation.NewValidator()` on the other, and the document is never allowed to advertise a value, or an absence, the validator refuses. It replaces `TestParseLeadingIntMatchesValidator`, which carried the guarantee in its name and pinned a hand-written copy of the pre-repair acceptance table in its body, never touching the validator — so it stayed green while the two parsers diverged, which is how the whole divergence this release repairs could be silent by construction
- validation: the cycle set is scoped to the current descent path, an entry leaving it when its subtree returns, because only an ancestor still on the path can close a cycle. A whole-call set read a shared non-cyclic pointer — the same `*T` reachable through two sibling fields — as a cycle and skipped every constraint under its second occurrence
- validation: a typed-nil error from a custom constraint is read through the interface rather than compared to nil, so it reports the success it means instead of being dereferenced on the request path
- validation: `createConstraintWithParams` refuses a name the registry cannot answer instead of handing a nil interface to `Validate`. The registry is append-only today, so the branch is latent; it keeps a future removal or replacement path from turning a missing registration into a nil dereference
- validation: `ToExceptionError` copies the internal context map into the exception instead of letting it escape by identity. The constructor copies on the way in and `Context()` copies on the way out precisely to keep the map private, and handing the live map to the exception let any consumer mutate the validation error through it
- validation: the time-codec memo settles on ONE verdict under a concurrent first touch. It was the only memo in the package using a plain `Store` and the only one whose producer runs application code — the decode probe — so two goroutines computing opposite verdicts froze whichever the store ordering happened to pick
- validation: the reason a parameterized constraint refused its parameters travels in the error context, and the parser names the comma-separated segment it could not read. One generic code covered three different conditions — a constraint that takes no parameters, a rejected parameter value, a construction that answered nil — memoized for the life of the process, and a long tag reported only that "the tag" was invalid
- logging: a typed nil no longer panics the error-reporting path anywhere in the package: at `LogError`'s entry, on the logger it is handed, on the cause of an exception, on an error sitting under a context key, and on the value a recovery handler carries. Each of these passed a plain `nil ==` comparison and dereferenced on the very line that renders a failure
- logging: `LogError` reads the already-logged mark at the depth `exception.MarkLogged` writes it — the nearest `AlreadyLogged` implementer in the chain. The read searched for the nearest `*exception.Error` instead, so on an `HttpException` wrapping an `Error` the writer marked the wrapper and the reader never saw it, and one failure produced two records
- logging: `LogError` anchors the record on the error the caller handed over. `errors.As` anchored on the exception buried deepest in the chain, so a wrapping `HttpException` at 500 over an info-level cause was filed at info and thrown away whole by a production threshold, with the wrapper's message and context lost
- logging: the cause keys a caller set survive the record. `cause`, `causeChain` and `causeContextChain` were overwritten by the walk, so a caller that had already named the cause it wanted reported had that naming replaced by the chain's own
- logging: an error nested inside a context value renders as its message however deep the containers this package builds put it. An error left to the encoder marshals as `{}` — every field unexported, no marshaler — so a cause carried inside a chain entry was lost in silence while the same error one level up rendered fine
- logging: an error in a log context that also implements `json.Marshaler` is handed to the encoder instead of being flattened to its message, so a validation error collection says the same thing in the journal that it says in the response body
- logging: the json logger's marshal fallback keeps the context as text rather than dropping it. One unmarshalable value used to cost every other key of the record — the service name, the cause chain — exactly when the record described a failure
- logging: the json logger reports its own failed write, once, on stderr. A `/var/log` that is full, read-only or on a vanished mount otherwise silences the entire journal with no signal on any channel, and the operator reads a healthy-looking empty file. It writes to stderr directly rather than through the emergency logger, which would re-enter the very write that just failed, and stays silent when its own output already is stderr
- logging: text of unknown origin reaching a plain-text log line is escaped rather than obeyed. The message and the context values regularly embed request-derived text, and an unescaped line break ended the record and started a fully-formed fake one at whatever level the payload named — the json sibling gets the same guarantee from its encoder
- logging: `NewJsonLoggerWithLabels`, `NewLoggingConfiguration`, `LoggingConfiguration.LevelLabels` and `NewDefaultLoggerWithLabels` copy the label map instead of sharing it. The map is read lock-free on every `Log` call, so a caller mutating the map it still holds is a fatal concurrent map access no recover reaches
- logging: `LoggerFromRuntime` records the resolution it cannot serve. It returned nil without a word, and the caller's own nil check then skipped its own record too, so the failure disappeared twice
- logging: a typed-nil logging configuration registered under the module name is refused where it is registered. The typed nil passed the `nil ==` guard and was accepted by the type assertion under it — a nil pointer satisfies the interface — so the boot died inside the logger provider, naming `service.logger` instead of the configuration that was registered wrong

- http: the kernel fails closed when a `kernel.request` or `kernel.controller` listener stopped propagation ahead of a listener marked required, even when that listener produced a response. The kernel closed only on `nil == Response()`, so a listener that stopped and answered — an http cache at a priority above the access-control listener — delivered its page with access control never consulted: `/admin` served from the cache to an anonymous caller. The response it produced is now dropped for the error page, and its body is closed rather than leaked. The error is judged on itself rather than through its cause chain, so an application event dispatched by a listener that skips a required listener of its own stays an ordinary listener failure
- event: a typed-nil event is refused through the interface, at the dispatch door and inside the recovery handler alike. A typed nil passes a plain `nil ==` comparison and dereferences on `Name()`, and the recovery handler dereferenced the same value while building its own context — a second panic raised inside the recovery, which discarded the diagnostic being built and left the caller with a bare memory address. A typed-nil clock is refused at `NewEventDispatcher`, and a typed-nil dispatcher at `NewEventDispatcherAdapter`, where the wiring mistake is, instead of at the first dispatch
- event: an error a listener returns is read through the interface, so a listener handing back a nil pointer of its own error type reports the success it means. Read as a failure it aborted the dispatch, skipped every listener behind it and failed the request closed for a listener that had reported success
- event: a subscriber is refused at the identity door before `SubscribedEvents()` is called, so a typed nil produces the framework error naming the mistake instead of a bare nil dereference raised inside the application's own subscriber at boot — the sibling `RemoveSubscriber` already gave the precise message for the same value
- event: a listener's failure travels unmarked, so the caller's own record is the one written. The record this package wrote carried the listener's identity and duration but never the error, and marking the wrapper as already logged then suppressed the http kernel's record — the only one that renders the cause chain — so "the database is down" appeared in no log at all and the request answered 500 with nothing saying why. A failure whose PRODUCER already logged and marked it is different: the wrapper inherits the mark, so one failure is not filed twice
- event: the value a listener panicked with travels as the cause of the error the recovery fabricates. Kept only in the context slot it collapsed to its bare message at the render boundary — a json logger stringifies an error found in a context — so the context map and the cause chain of that very error reached no record at all: the reason a cache write failed was gone while its stack survived. The record is written through `logging.LogError`, which renders the chain, and the wrapper is marked only after it is written; a panic value that reports itself already logged is not written a second time
- event: an `*exception.ExitError` passes through both recovery handlers to the process boundary, instead of being absorbed into an ordinary listener error with the deliberate exit code replaced by the generic one — the policy `logging/recover.go` documents and implements for the same value
- event: `NewEventFromEvent` carries the stopped propagation. A listener served through the adapter receives a copy, and the copy told it the propagation was live on an event that had already been stopped
- event: `EventDispatcherAdapter.RemoveListener` scrubs its own bookkeeping whether or not the wrapped dispatcher still held the listener. Returning early on false left a record of a listener that no longer exists — reported by `RegisteredEvents` forever and removable by nothing, since every retry took the same early return
- event: the inspector breaks equal priorities by the wrapped dispatcher's listener id, the same tiebreak the dispatch uses. Any adapter-side counter is issued under a different lock than the id, so two concurrent registrations could receive the two counters in opposite orders and the inspection reported — permanently — an execution order the dispatch never uses
- event: a whole subscriber installation is serialized against another, so a removal interleaved with the installation that issued its registration can no longer take down the half already installed while the rest keeps arriving
- event: the level question is asked once per dispatch rather than per record, and a listener's name is resolved where it is USED rather than ahead of every branch that might use it. The kernel dispatches at least three events per request; each debug record assembled a context map at the call site and a `reflect.Value` plus `runtime.FuncForPC` per listener per dispatch, all of it discarded unread by a journal above debug, on the hottest path the framework has
- debug: the `debug:events` summary counts distinct subscribers across the whole dispatcher. Summing the per-event distinct counts reported one subscriber listening on three events as three subscribers — a number nothing in the process matches. The per-event column is unchanged
- debug: the verbose listener detail of `debug:events` follows the listing's window AND its direction. `--order=desc` reversed the listing and left the detail ascending, so the two halves of one document contradicted each other; the direction is applied to the events and never to the listeners inside one event, whose rows carry the dispatcher's own slice order — which IS the dispatch order — so the order column cannot lie
