- debug: `debug:events` is registered with the declaration channel, so a console dispatcher renders the security pair the serving process wires instead of an absence

- application: a shutdown that begins in the same instant the listen fails reports the listen failure. The two arms of the select are chosen arbitrarily when both are ready, and the shutdown arm used to return without ever reading the serve channel, so a process that never served a byte — a port already bound, a permission denied — exited zero announcing a clean stop. `Shutdown` closes the listeners before it returns, so the serve goroutine has already been released and the receive is bounded
- application: the http failure the process exits on is rendered once instead of three times. `runHttp` logged it, then wrapped it afresh on the way out, and the exit handler logs whatever arrives unlogged — so one fall was written to the journal by the run, by the wrap and by the handler. It now travels marked as already logged
- application: the framework exception listener steps aside for an error handler the application installed at boot. The kernel consults the handler only when the dispatch produced no response, and the listener answers every `kernel.exception` dispatch, so a handler installed at boot could never run — the composition root's own error rendering was dead code in every application that wrote one. Asked through a `Has` door, so a replacement kernel that cannot answer keeps the listener exactly as before
- application: the kernel's default listeners are wired at the end of `Boot`, in every process shape, rather than inside `runHttp`. They are inert in a console that never dispatches a kernel event, and a dispatcher inspected there now answers with the same set the serving process runs — the introspection command used to report an empty dispatcher for a correctly wired application
- application: the application's own routes register before any module's. The router breaks a dispatch tie on registration order, and the composition root wrote its route against the application, not against whichever module boots beside it; with the http boot running last, a module route silently won every collision. The duplicate-route refusals also reach the aggregated boot report now, instead of panicking one at a time before the report exists
- application: a route queued from inside a module boot hook is refused. The queue drains early in the boot, before the module phases, so such a registrar never ran and the route was silently absent from the application; a module registers its routes through `RegisterHttpRoutes`, the hook made for them. **Behavioural change**
- application: a module registered from inside a module boot hook is refused. The phase loops iterate a snapshot of the module list, so the module received only the hooks of whatever phases had not run yet — a half-booted module reporting a successful boot. **Behavioural change**
- application: the same module instance reached through two providers boots once. Its services registered twice, and the loud half of that — a duplicate service name — hid the silent half: its listeners and its middlewares were each attached twice. An instance of an uncomparable type keeps the previous behaviour rather than a runtime panic, and two distinct instances sharing one name stay two modules
- application: a module provider that is itself a module is booted as that module. The door kept only its children and silently dropped the provider's own registrations, so the same value registered a different application depending on which of the two doors it went through
- application: `RegisterConfiguration` refuses any name but `logging`. The registry is consumed in exactly one place under exactly one name and exposes no accessor, so every other name — a misspelling of the supported one is the ordinary way it happens — stored a configuration the operator believed was active while nothing could ever consult it. **Behavioural change**
- application: an http process whose `.env` artifacts contributed no keys at all refuses to boot. Every built-in parameter has a development default — environment dev, profiler, debug commands, debug log level — so a production binary run from a directory without its `.env` files served on all of them, announced by nothing louder than one warning. A cli process stays permissive: development commands legitimately run without any environment file. **Behavioural change**
- application: a middleware factory that yields nil, or a typed nil, fails the pipeline build naming the definition. The pipeline skipped a nil middleware without recording it anywhere, so an operator who registered a rate limiter ran every request without one and was told nothing; the typed-nil yield was the same absence one assertion later, joining the chain and panicking per request. **Behavioural change**
- application: the logger, the url generator, the serializer manager, the default serializer and the validator can be substituted. Each was registered unconditionally, so an application or a module bringing its own met the boot's duplicate-registration exit rather than a substitution — content negotiation in particular was closed to everything but a fork. They are gated the way the cache, the session and the firewall manager already were
- application: `service.serializer` is registered. The two documented resolvers `SerializerFromRuntime` and `SerializerMustFromRuntime` named an id nothing ever registered, so the Must door panicked for every caller and the soft one answered nil, both by construction
- application: the logger is resolved once, eagerly, at boot. Its provider is the one whose failure `bootLogger` swallows — the container recovers the provider panic into an error and the fallback answers the emergency logger — so a boot reported success for a process whose next logger resolution could only panic, attributed to the run instead of to the configuration that broke it
- application: the firewall manager is registered whatever the process mode. It is a plain view of the compiled configuration with no request in it, and gating it on http sent a console process asking for it to a "service is not registered" panic that reads as a wiring mistake when the actual difference was the process shape. The two kernel listeners stay http-only: they are the enforcement, and a console process has no request to guard
- application: a cli command name is judged trimmed at registration, because that is the name it is dispatched under. A padded name registered under a spelling no argv can produce, with the suggestion table then blocking every invocation of a command that exists, while a duplicate differing only in padding panicked at every dispatch instead of being reported once
- application: a typed nil is refused at the module, module-provider and cli-command doors, read through the interface the way the sibling doors in the same package already read theirs. Stored, it booted reporting success and surfaced later as a bare dereference inside the module's own hook
- application: an embedded `fs.FS` that is a typed nil is refused by name at both doors. It passed the plain comparison and died later as an anonymous nil dereference inside `fs.Stat`
- application: `go run`'s temporary build directory is recognised by a whole path segment — `go-build` followed by nothing but digits — instead of by substring. An installation path merely containing a segment that starts with `go-build`, `/opt/go-builder/bin` among them, was classified as a go-run build and had all of its configuration discovery silently redirected from the executable's directory to the working directory
- application: a configuration that fails to build is reported as a failure to initialize rather than to resolve. `NewConfiguration` also fails on validation and on building the cli, kernel and http views, so a port out of range was diagnosed as a placeholder problem, while a genuine resolve failure already carries that exact message from below and was therefore doubled
- application: a typed-nil `*ExitError` in the cause chain is not read as an exit. `errors.As` matches the type on such a link and reports success, so the run handed `exception.Exit` a nil it refuses and the command's real error was discarded in favour of a message about melody's own plumbing
- application: `debug:version` leaves the application slot to the application. Melody's own version filled in there made the command print the framework version twice; an application that never declared its version now reads `<unknown>` instead of a lie
- application: the name of the function a middleware registration hands in is captured at registration, on both doors. Without it the pipeline listing had nothing to call any middleware — the one moment the value is certainly present and running nothing is when it is registered
- application: each module boot hook runs across every module before the next hook begins. Four of them had been folded into one sweep, which silently changed the granularity the contracts document: a module may rely on every sibling's listeners existing before any middleware registers

- config: the deep copy is linear in the number of distinct nodes where it was exponential in the shared ones. A node reached through two edges doubled the work at every level, so a twenty-eight-level value whose every node had two parents never finished copying — with the caller's lock held the whole time — and the depth bound the traversal was supposed to be caught by never came near. A cycle now closes onto its own copy instead of burning ten thousand levels and planting an ALIAS to the live original in the copy, and a node reached twice is copied once and stays shared, slices keyed on their backing array AND their length so two subslices of one array stay two nodes.
- config: the three refusals `Int` raises on a float carry three distinct causes and the value they refused. Collapsed into one "is not a 'int'" they blamed the type, which was never the problem — the same branch accepts `2.0` — and the non-string value was omitted from the context entirely, so an operator could not tell whether to change the encoding, the value or the magnitude.
- config: a runtime parameter names itself in the cause of its own conversion error. Passing the empty environment key to the shared parsers put a nameless `parameterName` inside the cause of an error whose outer context did name the parameter, so the operator reading the cause chain concluded the parameter was anonymous.
- config: a runtime registration whose resolution fails leaves no half-made parameter behind. The parameter was published into the store before the failible resolve, so a template that could not be resolved left a parameter readable under its name holding its raw text.
- config: `Resolve` is refused once the application is serving, and `MarkServing` takes the write lock the refusal is read against. A re-resolution can no longer reconfigure anything — every service copied its values out while it was being built — but it does rewrite the whole store underneath readers entitled to treat it as settled, so it is refused rather than half-honoured.
- config: the failure that names the MELODY_ENV resolution window says which files a reference in it can see. The environment name selects the `.env.<name>` files, so those are not read yet, and a key defined right there in `.env.prod` was reported "undefined" to an operator who could see it defined.
- http: the server-sent-event writer is safe for concurrent use. The documented shape of a stream is a handler emitting events beside a ticker emitting keepalives, and a `net/http` ResponseWriter is not safe for concurrent use, so two unsynchronized frames interleaved into one corrupt frame that the client reads as a truncated event with no error raised anywhere
- http: the writer refuses a stream it cannot serve, before it commits. The response must not already be committed — appended to a body in flight, the frames left no trace but net/http's own "superfluous WriteHeader" line on the process's stderr, outside the journal entirely — and the connection must really support streaming. The capability probe reads THROUGH the kernel's recording writer rather than at it: that wrapper always carries a `Flush` method and forwards it only when its own delegate can flush, so the refusal was dead code for every in-framework caller and the handler went on to subscribe and write events into a buffer nothing would ever flush
- http: a frame that failed partway poisons the writer. The torn bytes are on the wire and no later frame can repair them, so a well-formed frame appended onto one was read by the client as a single corrupt event; every subsequent call is now refused instead
- http: the writer refuses the event shapes the grammar reads as something other than what the caller wrote — an event name with no data, which the grammar discards without dispatching anything, so the listener the caller named never fired; an id or an event name that would collapse to empty once its control bytes are removed, which silently resets the client's resume cursor or makes the browser fire the default message type instead of the one named; and a negative retry, a unit-confusion fault whose silent drop was indistinguishable from never setting the field
- http: `ServerSentEventHub.Shutdown` closes the backplane it owns. The interface declares `Close` for a reason — the shipped backplanes hold a goroutine, a cancel func and a live subscription — and the hub is the only holder of the reference, so a hub that shut down without it left them running for the life of the process while its own replication had already stopped publishing through them. `SetBackplane` refuses to install a backplane over a live one rather than orphaning it — closing the previous one from that door cannot be the remedy, because the shipped backplanes clear themselves from the hub as the first step of their own `Close` and would re-enter it to clear the one just installed — and refuses to install one into a hub that has already shut down. Clearing stays allowed on a shut-down hub, because that re-entry is exactly what a backplane's `Close` performs
- http: a broadcast that has passed the hub's closed check finishes before the backplane it holds is closed under it. Read and acted on with nothing between them, a shutdown landed in that window and the hub published through a backplane it had already reported closed — which a backplane whose `Close` shuts an internal channel answers with a send on a closed channel. The publish still runs outside the lock, because it is a network round trip and holding the lock across it would block every shutdown behind the slowest broker
- http: `SetBackplane` reads a typed nil as the nothing it means. A composition root that builds its backplane conditionally hands back a nil pointer boxed in the interface, which a bare comparison took for a live backplane and dereferenced on the first broadcast — off the request goroutine, where no recovery covers it. The publish itself now runs under a guard as well, because it is third-party code called from framework internals on whatever goroutine broadcast
- http: the zero value of `ServerSentEventHub` subscribes instead of panicking. The struct is exported with only unexported fields, so a composition root writing `&ServerSentEventHub{}` compiled, booted, shut down and reported a subscriber count, then panicked on an assignment to a nil map inside the first request that connected. A negative subscriber buffer is refused by name; zero remains the caller's own zero value and takes the default
- http: `JsonHandler` can no longer turn a refused request into a success. An error responder that answered `(nil, nil)` — the natural shape of "I decided not to render anything" — made the handler return the same pair, which the kernel reads as a handler that answered nothing and serves as an empty 204: a rejected write reported success to its client with no record filed anywhere. The refusal now stands whatever the responder answers, the responder runs under the kernel's own containment discipline, a nil handle or a nil responder is refused at construction rather than at the first request that passes validation, and a `null` body is read as empty for every nilable kind rather than for pointers alone — a bulk endpoint typed on a slice took the same `null` past the guard, the validator reported the nil collection valid, and the handler was entered with it
- http: `melody:routes:manifest` mirrors the openapi generate command in the four places that decide whether its artifact is trustworthy, none of which it did while its own comment said it mirrored that command. A relative `--out` is anchored at the project directory rather than at whatever directory the process happened to start in; a target that is not a JSON document is refused rather than destroyed by a mistyped flag; the write lands through a temp file and a rename, with the parent directories created, so an interrupted run leaves the previous manifest intact instead of a torn document the frontend loads as `window.melodyRoutes`; and the output travels through the command writer rather than process stdout, which the cli layer redirects and which in json mode a raw print splices into from the first byte. A marshal failure is wrapped with the context only this call site has
- http: the pipeline build report travels with the refusal instead of being dropped for it. Returned nil, the caller reporting a factory failure had the name of the offending middleware and nothing about the selection, the gating or the cycle detection around it — while `Describe`, the same selection without the factories, has always returned it. A factory that succeeds and hands back nothing is recorded as inactive under the reason it is inactive for: requested, selected and ordered, the middleware used to vanish from the chain without appearing in the selected names OR in the inactive list
- example: the two doors onto the route table now honour one gate. `/routes` walked the registry directly and listed every named route in the application — the internal whoami endpoint, the token revocation endpoints, the user delete endpoint — each with a worked example url, to an anonymous caller, while the sibling door onto the same data applied the expose opt-in. The zone gate is applied against the caller as well: the frontend zone is the admin surface, and the manifest injected into every page carried it into the anonymous login page

- http: the per-field detail of a failed validation reaches the client. The exception listener rendered only the flat message beside the moment, so the detail the validator computed — attached to the exception by `BindJsonAndValidate` — reached neither the client nor, structured, anything else; the listener now projects the `validationErrors` context key into the response body through `WithoutRuleWiringContext`, so an entry blaming the declaration keeps its field, message and code for the client while its context — the developer's own typo, the refused parameters — stays in the record where it belongs
- http: a deliberate 4xx a handler returned is recorded at warning while a 5xx and every non-http error keep the error level — a refusal is not an incident. The one exception is a 4xx whose validation errors blame the DECLARATION: a struct tag naming a rule that does not exist refuses every request that route will ever serve, and at warning it sat in the dashboard among the users who mistyped their address while the route stayed permanently broken; `carriesRuleWiringError` raises exactly those to error
- http: the exception listener resolves the status through the one guarded door instead of asking the same question twice, once without the guard. `errors.As` matches the dynamic type of a typed-nil `*HttpException` and reports it found, so `StatusCode()` dereferenced nil; `exception.AsHttpException` itself gains the typed-nil pre-check and the nil-result guard its v2 twin has, and `IsHttpException` answers through it so the two doors cannot disagree
- http: the exception listener renders the error text and the debug cause through `debugErrorMessage`, the containment its siblings already had — an error whose own `Error()` panics on exactly the nil that made it panic-worthy killed the whole debug payload one dispatch up, and the client received the kernel's fallback body instead of the degraded page the listener exists to serve
- http: the error body honours the same negotiation the success path honours. Both error renderings — the exception listener's and the kernel's own fallback paths — served hardcoded json whatever the Accept header named; they now collapse onto one door that resolves the serializer manager against the joined Accept lines, serializes under a recover (`serializeErrorPayloadSafely`, because the door is consulted from inside the kernel's recovery defer), keeps the error's own status when the header refuses every media type — an error status is the signal itself, and masking a security refusal behind 406 would hide it from the client that has to react to it — and falls back to the json envelope whenever any of that cannot answer. The kernel's fallback paths also gain the `X-Request-Id` header and the status line on the html page, which only the listener used to set
- http: `PrefersHtml` reads the Accept header under the serializer's rules — every line of a repeated field joined before parsing, members and parameters split outside quoted sections through `internal.SplitOutsideQuotes`, and a member whose q parameter falls outside the RFC 7231 qvalue grammar dropped whole, where a bare float parse honoured `q=Inf` as an infinite weight and let `q=NaN` poison every comparison, so the two negotiators of one response disagreed on the same header
- http: the result handler reads every line of a repeated `Accept` header, joined in order, where `Header.Get` answers only the first; `NormalizeResultToResponse` asserts against the `httpcontract.Response` contract, the same question the controller registration door asks, with the typed nil read through the interface — a caller's own `Response` implementation fell through to the serializer and was rendered as a value, all unexported fields, an empty body under a 200 that replaced the status the caller chose; and a serializer resolution failure that is not the not-acceptable refusal is recorded at warning before the fallback serves the default representation, where it was dropped whole and a client that named an available type and received another had no diagnostic anywhere
- http/static: the file server refuses a spelling that `path.Clean` folds into the mount root — `/..`, `/.`, `//`, `/open/..` — which is the refusal the branch resolving every other path already carried. The matchers in front of the application compare the raw path, so those spellings are urls no rule on the mount ever saw, and answering them served the mount's index page from behind whatever rule the other prefix carries; canonical is the mount root itself, with or without its trailing slash. **Behavioural change**: the folded spellings answered the index page and now fall through to the application
- http/static: the file server consults its excluded-path list against the index file the mount root resolves to. The exclusion was consulted only with the spelling the client sent, and the root resolves to the index file after that consultation, so an operator who excluded `/index.html` — handing that page to the application, behind its own authentication — still had it served off the disk for a request to `/`
- http/static: `NewFileServer` copies the configuration at construction, struct and both lists, so the server is immutable once built — the constructor used to write its defaults into the caller's own struct, and a setter called after construction re-configured a server already serving, racing the in-flight requests that read those fields with no lock; the setter contracts now say a later call configures the next server. `NewFileServer` also refuses nil options by name instead of panicking on the dereference — refusal rather than a default, because the default here would be a live file server over the `public` directory, and a nil that is almost always a wiring mistake must not start serving files nobody asked served
- http/static: an embedded file server proves its public directory against the embedded filesystem at construction and refuses by name when it is absent or is a file — `MELODY_PUBLIC_DIR` naming a directory the build did not embed passed every validation, booted, and answered 404 for every asset the binary carries
- http/static: in the embedded mode the cache validators stop being derived from a modification time that does not exist. A filesystem that carries no modification time reports the zero instant: `Last-Modified` rendered it as `Mon, 01 Jan 0001 00:00:00 GMT` — a validator that is not one — and the zero time is never After anything, so every conditional request carrying `If-Modified-Since` and no entity tag was answered 304 for the life of the deployment; the header is now absent for an undated file and the date branch does not fire, leaving the entity tag as the only validator, which is where the build version answers: an undated file's tag carries `version.BuildVersion()` instead of the zero timestamp, so a redeployed asset of unchanged size stops revalidating as unchanged forever
- http/static: the entity tag reads the modification time at nanosecond resolution rather than whole seconds — two rewrites within the same second that keep the same byte length produced an identical tag, so a bundle swap revalidated 304 and stayed stale until its length or its second changed
- http/static: an explicit cache max age of zero is honoured as `Cache-Control: public, max-age=0` — always revalidate, with the ETag and Last-Modified machinery intact — because the configuration door validates zero as a distinct choice; only a negative value reads as unset and takes the 3600 default. **Behavioural change**: zero was silently coerced to an hour of freshness
- http/static: the file server's two ordinary-control-flow exits — the non-retrieval method and the out-of-prefix path — are recorded at debug instead of info: with the middleware registered globally they fired once per api request, exactly the per-request noise the package's own logging comment keeps out of the journal
- http: `acceptsGzip` reads Accept-Encoding under the one negotiation grammar — every line of the repeated field joined, members and parameters split outside quoted sections, the `q` parameter compared case-insensitively where the bare prefix check let `Q=0` pass unread and the client that refused gzip received it, a member whose q falls outside the RFC 7231 grammar dropped whole where `q=Inf` switched the compression on and `q=NaN` switched it off, and a repeated coding resolved to its higher quality instead of last-wins
- http: the compression middleware pools its gzip writers (`sync.Pool`, one pool per level) — `gzip.NewWriterLevel` allocates the deflate window and hash tables on every call, close to a megabyte of garbage per compressed response on the hot path; the peek buffer grows in chunks instead of allocating a mis-configured `MinSize` upfront; and `CompressionMiddleware(nil)` reads as the default configuration while a non-nil configuration is copied at construction instead of being normalized in place in the caller's own struct
- http: the rate limiters' idle prune holds its threshold for a window past the midpoint of the duration range — `window*2` wrapped negative for a window past `MaxInt64/2`, the threshold read as already elapsed for every entry, and each cleanup emptied the map and refilled a budget the window promised to hold (`idlePruneThreshold`, saturating, shared by both limiters)
- http: the rate limit middleware inspects what its limit handler answered instead of passing it through blind — a handler that returned `(nil, nil)` refused the request but produced nothing, and the nil response was normalized downstream into an empty 204, the over-budget request served as a success; a typed-nil response is read through the interface and both fall back to the 429 json body. A limiter store failure that is the caller's own cancellation is recorded at warning under its own name rather than paging the operator as a store outage, a failure the limiter already recorded is not filed a second time (`exception.IsAlreadyLogged`), `RateLimitMiddleware(nil)` is refused by name instead of panicking on the dereference, a typed-nil limiter is read through the interface and refused at construction under the same name — it passed the plain comparison, looked live, and dereferenced its nil receiver on the first metered request — and `UserRateLimitWithResolver` refuses a nil user-id callback at construction instead of dying once per request inside the closure
- http: `SlidingWindowLimiter.Allow` frees exactly the expired prefix through `sort.Search` instead of rebuilding the whole slice under the shared lock on every call — an O(limit) reallocation an attacker paid on his own refusals while every other key blocked behind him
- http/cors: a schemeless allow entry is port-significant — `extractOriginHost` kept `url.Hostname()`, which cuts the port, so `app.example.com` matched `https://app.example.com:9000` and any other port anything can bind on that host, a grant `RestrictiveService` couples with credentials; the wildcard suffix path carried the same cut
- http/cors: an explicitly empty (non-nil) `AllowOrigins` list denies every origin instead of silently becoming the wildcard — a misconfigured environment variable arriving empty turned "allow nobody" into "allow everybody"; nil keeps the permissive default, `NewService` reads a nil method or header list as the one default `DefaultService` grants — Authorization included, where the fallback list silently dropped it — and keeps an empty list as the expressed preference; and credentials beside an explicitly empty origin list are refused at boot under their own name, where the empty-to-wildcard rewrite used to trip the wildcard-with-credentials panic and blame a wildcard the caller never wrote. **Behavioural change**: an empty non-nil origins list now denies
- http/cors: `RegisterRequestListener` and the `RegisterListeners` façade arrive, and the cors preflight is answered ahead of the security chain — the request listener sits at priority 100, before token resolution at 50, because a preflight carries neither cookie nor Authorization by spec, so aimed at an access-controlled path it was refused opaquely and the browser reported a bare network error; a preflight another listener already answered is decorated instead of going back bare. The middleware form applies the cross-origin headers to the writer before the handler runs and keeps Vary on the error path, so a streaming handler and a `(nil, err)` return stop losing them; `CorsMiddleware(nil)` reads as the default service the way `cors.Middleware(nil)` always has, instead of panicking on the dereference
- http: `RegisterRateLimitRequestListener` meters every request on kernel.request, before authentication and access control — `RateLimitMiddleware` meters only what reaches the handler path, so a credential-stuffing burst the security chain refuses consumed no budget at all, exactly the traffic a limiter exists for; the listener charges it ahead of the authenticator round and answers it once the budget is gone, rendering the refusal through kernel.exception so a deliberate 429 stays a 429
- http/pipeline: `Builder.Describe` lists a pipeline without running the factories it names — `MiddlewareDescription` carries the definition's name, function, priority and gating, `Definition.SetFunctionName` names the function, and the listing mirrors the nil-factory refusal of `Build` so it cannot report as healthy a definition the build refuses; a definition's constraint lists and `Report.SetInactive` keep their own copies, so a registrant reusing one slice across two definitions no longer rewrites the ordering constraints the pipeline was registered under
- http: the forwarded client-ip resolver copies the trusted-proxy list at construction — the returned closure read the caller's own slice on every request to decide the limiter key, so a caller that reused or mutated its slice rewrote the trust decision mid-serving as a data race; the same copy `Kernel.SetForwardedHeadersPolicy` already performs
- exception: `LogContext` and the cause-chain rendering produce an error's text under a recover (`renderErrorText`), the containment the container teardown's close-error rendering already applies — the function runs inside recovery defers, where a value whose `Error()` panics would raise a second panic past the recovery reporting the first, and the connection was reset with the original failure reaching no record at all
- exception: `LogContext` anchors the cause walk on the top error's own wrap links instead of the nearest `*Error` a deep search found — anchoring deep drops the context of every link above it — and walks both unwrap shapes the standard library defines, so a joined error contributes its branches side by side instead of ending the chain at its own link; a typed nil anywhere in the reading — the error itself, the context provider, a chain link — is read as the nil it means instead of being dereferenced
- exception: the mutable halves of `Error` and `HttpException` — context and the already-logged mark — are guarded by a lock. Error handling stays single-threaded within one request by design, but a creation failure memoized by the container is reachable from the owner request and from every waiter request at once, and an unlocked map write against a map iteration is a fatal runtime error no recover reaches; `SetContextValue` also allocates the map a zero-value construction leaves nil instead of panicking on the first write
- security: a refusal on the access-control and resolution paths writes a fail-closed response instead of a nil the kernel reads as "no decision" and proceeds past. Every deny branch materialized its refusal as the response the `kernel.exception` dispatch produced, with no fallback, so an application-registered exception listener that stopped propagation without setting a response — or an entry point that answered `(nil, nil)` — let the refused request reach its handler; the response is now the dispatch's, or a generic 500 when it produced none, and an entry point that answers nothing falls through to the fail-closed 401
- security: a login, logout or authenticator failure whose event dispatch also fails keeps the original authentication error as its cause instead of dropping it for the dispatch error. A caller that read `errors.Is`/`As` on the credential failure to answer 401 rather than 500 saw the listener's error, and the real reason never crossed the boundary; the denied-handler failure branch likewise keeps the authorization decision as the cause, so a broken denied handler no longer turns a 403 into whatever its own failure maps to. `CompiledFirewall.Logout` gains the nil-result guard `Login` already had, refusing a handler that returns `(nil, nil)` before the request path dereferences it
- security: `NewApiKeyHeaderRule` refuses a nil matcher at construction, as every sibling constructor does, instead of dereferencing it on the request path outside any recover; the typed nil is refused with `IsNilInterface`, the boxed nil a caller writes by hand for "no matcher" that a plain comparison takes for a live value
- security: a typed nil is refused at both walls of the security configuration. The matcher, token source, login and logout handlers, rules, authenticators, voters and the wrapped token are read with `IsNilInterface` where a plain comparison let a `(*myType)(nil)` through the eager validation and through `Compile` to be dereferenced on the first request behind the firewall; the global access decision manager, entry point and denied handler an override carries are refused by name at `SetGlobal` and at compile, naming the source, because a typed nil that arrived through the global configuration and one through a firewall's own override are two different mistakes. `AuthenticatorManager` and the resolution listener read a typed-nil token from userland as the anonymous it means rather than the authenticated one `IsAuthenticated` answers without touching its receiver, and `SecurityContext.IsGranted` answers false for a typed-nil token instead of dereferencing it in a goroutine no recover covers
- security: the firewall, authenticator manager, access decision manager, compiled configuration and firewall builder each keep their own copy of the caller's slice, the way their `Rules()`/`Voters()`/`Firewalls()` readers have always copied on the way out, so a caller that held the reference can no longer change a rule, a voter or an authenticator after the boot that validated them, in the decision path of a live request
- security: `SecurityContextSetOnRuntime` and `SecurityContextFromRuntime` guard the runtime and its scope with `IsNilInterface` and resolve the logger through the tolerant `LoggerFromRuntime`, so a custom runtime whose `Scope()` is a typed nil, or a closed scope read from the goroutine `IsGranted` is documented to reach, answers `(nil, false)` — the denial the caller can act on — instead of a panic no recover covers. The recovery around a panicking token source reads the source's name defensively, so a nil token source panicking on `Resolve` no longer panics the deferred handler a second time and loses the original diagnostic
- security: an absent firewall dependency reads `SourceNone` in the compiled firewall's sources rather than `SourceFirewall`, so the debug view names where a role hierarchy or decision manager actually came from
- wiring: two constructors that would register the same service — two `New*` returning one type, or two naming one service constant — are refused at generation, naming both sites. The generated file registered both, so the application panicked at its first boot with an error naming only the type, while `melody:wiring:generate` had reported success and `--strict` had nothing to say; the check is keyed exactly as the emitted registration keys the container, per lifetime, so a scoped and a container constructor of one type still coexist — that shadowing is what scoped registration is for. A name constant's value is not read from the source, so a type registration colliding with a named one, or two distinct constants holding one value, remain out of reach and still fail at boot
- wiring: a malformed exclude pattern is refused before anything is walked, and an exclude that matched no constructor is reported — and fails `--strict` — the way an unused bind is. `path.Match` answers `ErrBadPattern` for an unterminated character class on every name, and the scanner read that as "does not match", so the exclusion the operator declared matched nothing and the constructor it named was registered anyway; a pattern that merely went stale — a renamed type, a typo — was equally silent. **Behavioural change**: a `--strict` run that carried a dead exclude now fails instead of succeeding
- wiring: an unknown `//melody:` directive is a hard error naming the file, the line and the text, instead of being silently ignored. Every one of the four directives fails open when it is dropped — a mistyped `//melody:scoped` demotes a request-lifetime service to a process singleton that is never closed with its scope, a mistyped `//melody:ignore` registers the very constructor it acknowledges — and nothing anywhere said the comment had not been read. **Behavioural change**: a source tree carrying a mistyped directive now fails the generation until the spelling is corrected or the comment stops using the `//melody:` prefix
- wiring: `//melody:ignore` accepts a trailing reason — `//melody:ignore kept as a test double` — instead of matching only the bare form. The natural spelling was not an ignore at all: the constructor was scanned and registered while the comment beside it said the opposite, and under the new unknown-directive rule the reasoned form would have been refused rather than silently inverted
- wiring: a `//melody:bind` assignment spelled without the equals sign, or with an empty half, is a hard error naming the assignment and its position. It was dropped without a trace, so the override written right beside the constructor fell back to a broader package or global bind — the read-replica dsn quietly resolving to the primary — and the unused-directive report could not see it, because the malformed assignment never became a directive bind to go unused
- wiring: an empty import path or directory on a package binding is refused at generation. An empty directory joined to the project root and silently scanned the whole tree as one package, attributing every constructor in the project to the binding's import path; an empty import path rendered an import of `""`
- wiring: a symlink standing where the declared package directory should be is resolved before the walk. `filepath.WalkDir` does not follow symlinks, so a symlinked root was read as an empty package — no constructors, no skips, no error — and every service under it left the generated wiring while `--strict` reported success; a symlinked subdirectory stays out of the scan, exactly as the go tool leaves it out of a build, and the contract now says so
- wiring: the generator's promise to say when it cannot check the bind targets is kept. `GenerateRequest.DeclaredParameters` documented that an empty set is announced rather than silently assumed complete, but nothing implemented the announcement: every misspelled parameter passed validation and surfaced as a `MustGet` panic at container boot, far from the generation that was supposed to catch it. `GenerateReport.BindTargetsUnchecked` now carries the fact and the command prints it
- wiring: `--strict` carries every violation in one refusal — unused binds, unused excludes and skipped constructors together — instead of returning on the first list it found. The run is inspected through its exit and its error record, and an error naming only the bind typo attributed the failure to it while the lost constructor coverage beside it never crossed the process boundary
- wiring, openapi: the two generate commands land their output through a temp file and a rename instead of truncating the target in place, and refuse to replace a file that is not theirs — wiring by the `DO NOT EDIT` marker its own output opens with, openapi by the file not holding a JSON document. `os.WriteFile` truncates before it writes, so a write that died partway — a full disk, a killed process — left a torn artifact where the previous good one had been: a `wiring_gen.go` that fails the whole build as a syntax error in a file nobody edited, or a torn `openapi.json` published as the API's description; and a mistyped `--out` pointing at a hand-written source file destroyed it before writing anything
- wiring: an `--out` path inside a scanned package directory is refused. The constructor's documentation always said the output package must not be a scanned one; unenforced, the generated file was read back by the next scan with a package clause the surrounding sources do not carry, so the package stopped compiling and the tool could no longer regenerate its way out of its own output
- openapi: a relative `--out` is anchored at the project directory, exactly as the wiring command has always anchored its own, and the parent directories are created on the way. The documented invocation is relative, and anchored at whatever directory the process happened to start in — `/` under systemd, the checkout root in one pipeline and a subdirectory in another — the document was written into a different tree per launcher while the command reported success, so the publish step shipped yesterday's file; the same flag on the two sibling commands also disagreed about what `--out foo.json` means
- openapi: `melody:openapi:generate` writes through the command writer instead of the process stdout, and a marshal failure is wrapped with the command's context instead of returned raw. A harness that captured the command's output got an empty buffer while the document went to the real stdout; the raw `*json.UnsupportedTypeError` crossed the cli boundary with no mention of the command or the output path
- openapi: the responses of a descriptor are visited in status order, so the generated document is byte-stable across runs. The range over the responses map was the one unordered driver of first-touch component naming: whichever type a run visited first took the bare component name and the collision took the numbered sibling, so two runs over one registry disagreed on every `$ref` to a colliding name — a churning committed artifact, and a served document that could disagree with the CLI-written one for the same application state
- openapi: a route registered with no method list is documented on all eight path item verbs, each with its own operationId. The router treats an empty method list as answering every method, and the document wrote an operation-less path item — `"/webhook": {}` — an endpoint that reads as answering nothing while the server answers everything. **Behavioural change**: a document carrying such routes grows eight operations per route where it carried none
- openapi: a verb outside the eight the format models — the router registers any string — is named in the path item's description instead of its operation being built and dropped without a trace. The route stayed absent from the spec while answering in production, with no channel to say so; `PathItem` gains the `description` field the note travels in
- openapi: a catch-all pattern is converted the way the router matches it: the path stops at the catch-all segment, because the router's registration returns there and every segment written after it is discarded and never matched. The document advertised `/assets/{rest}/thumbnail` for a route registered as `/assets/*rest.../thumbnail` — a template no request the route answers can ever spell — while the path the route does answer was absent; a mid-pattern `*name` without the dots remains the single-segment wildcard it is to the router and keeps its tail
- openapi: a route whose converted path collides with an earlier route's — a placeholder pattern against a brace literal that convert to one template — no longer silently replaces its operations; the earlier registration wins, exactly as it does in the router's match order, and the shortened mirror of an optional tail still yields to a real route from either direction
- openapi: a response status outside the registered table keeps a non-empty description. `nethttp.StatusText` answers `""` for a code it does not know, and the response description is required by the format, so `WithResponse[T](599)` produced a document most tooling rejects
- openapi: the generated document no longer aliases the registry's memory. The descriptor arrives by value but its tags slice shared the registry's backing array, so a caller post-processing the returned document — appending a tag, sorting — wrote through into the boot-time registry and every later generation, from the CLI file to every served response
- openapi: `notEmpty` behind a pointer to a fixed-length array drops its `minItems` floor exactly as the value form always has. Once the nil pointer was admitted, `len(*[N]T)` is `N` whatever the payload spelled — `[]` decodes zero-filled and passes — so the floor advertised a rejection the server never issues; the field stays required, because the validator does reject the nil pointer
- openapi: the auto-registered `melody:openapi:generate` says when the info service is absent. The auto-registration gate reads the registry service alone, so a container carrying it without `service.openapi.info` reached the command, the tolerant resolver answered an empty `Info`, and the run wrote a document whose required title and version are empty strings while reporting nothing but success
- lock: `RunExclusive` dates the lease from the instant the acquire or renewal was ISSUED rather than from when it answered — the leader gate's own documented rule, which this path did not follow. Dated at the answer, a store whose reply crawled back handed the believed lease the whole response latency past the real one, so in the degraded-store regime where slow answers and failed renewals coincide, the callback kept running through a window in which a second instance could legally acquire — the one thing `RunExclusive` exists to prevent
- lock: a failed lock release is logged with the lock name and the cause — it was discarded entirely, so the lock stayed held for up to a full ttl while every peer's next tick skipped with a record blaming a run that was not happening, and the operator could not tell a failed release from a crash
- lock: in session mode (a non-positive ttl) the leader gate's lease clock no longer decides demotion, because there is no lease — `enterTerm` dated one from the acquire instant with a non-positive ttl, a deadline already in the past, so the FIRST failed liveness probe of every term demoted immediately, overriding the documented three-failure tolerance and even a negative never-on-count threshold; the consecutive-failure threshold is the session-mode signal, as its own documentation always described
- lock: the leader gate's lease deadline lives in monotonic time, as an offset from a construction-instant anchor, instead of wall-clock unix nanoseconds — a backwards wall-clock step (an ntp correction) extended perceived leadership past the real lease, so `IsLeader` kept answering true while a second instance legally acquired and the recovery margin refused to demote for the whole step; `RunExclusive` was already immune, keeping `time.Time` values with their monotonic reading
- lock: the leader gate shields the `OnElected`, `OnLost` and `OnCampaignError` hooks and both refresh goroutines behind a recover, and leaves its term through defers — a panicking hook or backend unwound a goroutine the documentation says to start bare, killing the whole process with the lock held for the rest of its ttl on every peer, and in a fleet where every replica runs the same hook the panic cascaded instance by instance as each won the lock; a recovered hook panic is logged with its stack, and a recovered refresh panic is the same demotion signal a returned error is
- lock: a leader gate with no `OnCampaignError` hook logs each failed campaign, and one with no `OnLost` hook logs each lost term — the hooks used to be the only channel, so without them a store outage and a permanent misconfiguration were both indistinguishable from a deployment that quietly elects no leader and does no work; a wired hook replaces the record rather than being echoed by it
- lock: `ExclusiveCommand`'s skip record distinguishes a shutdown from contention — a SIGTERM landing during the acquire was logged as "an exclusive run is already in progress on another instance", a phantom peer, and under the documented exit-zero design that log line is the single source of truth for "did not run"
- lock: when the lease is lost while the callback also failed for a reason of its own, the callback's error joins the cause chain instead of being flattened into a context string — flattened, its identity was dead for `errors.Is` at the process boundary, so sentinel-matched alerting and retry classification silently stopped matching
- messagebus: an overlapping `Run` of the auto-registered consume command no longer races itself — the container hydration wrote the singleton's own `bus`, `transports` and `retryPolicy` fields at the start of every run, while the workers of a run already in flight were reading them; the in-process cron runner overlaps a run with itself whenever an entry outruns its interval, which makes that a supported deployment shape, and the collaborators now resolve into run-local state the runs do not share
- messagebus: the retry-delay guard computes in `int64` — truncated to `int`, the quotient of the one-hour default cap over a microsecond-scale `BaseDelay` wraps negative on a 32-bit build, turning the very first retry into a full `MaxDelay` wait
- messagebus: `NewInMemoryTransport` refuses a negative buffer size in the framed form every sibling constructor uses, instead of reaching `make(chan)` and dying with a raw runtime panic
- messagebus: a delayed requeue that the in-memory transport drops is logged through the logger of the runtime the `Nack` was called with — the drop happens after the `Nack` already answered success, on a detached goroutine, and the only channel used to be a logger nothing in any production wiring ever configured, so the consumer logged "requeueing" and the message evaporated with no record anywhere; a requeue strictly after `Close` is also refused deterministically, where one select used to weigh a ready queue slot against the closed transport and Go picks between ready cases at random
- messagebus: a dispatch that succeeded while nothing sent, handled or received the message is logged — a send-only bus with no route for the type, or a bus assembled with no middlewares, was a total silent no-op reported as success, so a forgotten `RouteType` line meant every dispatch of that type returned nil while the message ceased to exist
- messagebus: the consume command's error records fall back to the emergency logger when the runtime resolves none — those records include the only trace of a panic recovered outside the handler, and a runtime without a logger made the panic, its value and its captured stack evaporate behind a redelivery pattern with zero forensics
- mailer: a typed-nil runtime is refused by `SmtpTransport.Send` with the same clean record the untyped nil gets — the plain comparison waved it through onto a nil dereference at the dial
- mailer: the `MAIL FROM` and `RCPT TO` failures say "command failed" instead of asserting "rejected" — the error may as well be a per-step deadline expiry or the cancellation watcher closing the connection, and a record claiming a server verdict for those sent the operator investigating a policy decision that never happened
- mailer: the two post-acceptance quit warnings carry their cause — with only the address beside them, a recurring quit failure could not be told apart from a timeout, a protocol error or a closed socket
- translation: a `.json` file in the translations directory whose name does not parse as `<domain>.<locale>.json` is a hard error naming the file — it was skipped with the load reporting success, so a naming typo (`en.json`, `messages_en.json`) shipped a translator answering raw message ids in production with nothing anywhere pointing at the mis-named file, while every other failure in the loader was already a hard error
- translation: `NewManager` refuses a nil catalog instead of skipping it silently — the skip built a translator answering raw message ids for a whole locale with nothing pointing at the wiring hole
- storage: a later `Put` of a key sweeps that key's abandoned temp objects — a crash mid-write (kill -9, oom) left a `.tmp-` file the in-process error paths could never clean, with no sweeper, no `List` to notice it through, and unbounded growth under a crash-looping uploader; the sweep touches only regular files carrying exactly the generated suffix shape whose modification time is at least an hour old, so a concurrent writer's live temp and an operator's own files are never its to remove, and the negative-size "length unknown" convention `Put` always applied is now written on the contract itself, beside the package document that already taught it
- storage: the copy failure inside `Put` says "could not copy the payload into the storage object" — `io.Copy` answers one error for both sides, and the likelier one on an upload streamed from a request body is the reader dying with the client, so a message blaming the storage write sent the operator at the disk when the source connection failed

- exception: `MarkLogged` marks the nearest `AlreadyLogged` implementer in the chain rather than only the value it is handed, and answers a typed nil unchanged instead of attempting to mark it. A record filed against a wrapped melody error left the mark unwritten before, so the reader at the same depth found nothing and the same failure was rendered a second time
- documentation: fifty-six exported doors this major has always carried are listed at last, added beside the same repair on the two published majors so a reader of any of the three finds the same surface. The access control merge strategies were given as the three string literals and never as the exported constants a caller is meant to pass; the session-fixation section taught the manual two-step rotation without naming `http.RegenerateRequestSession`, which does both; and the rest are doors of the same kind — the deferred-resolution handle and the container's four registration sentinels, the process-identity family and the signal context every `Run` example is handed out of thin air, the config resolver a provider actually comes through, the setters of the cache remember option, the session cookie constant, the interface a userland constraint has to implement, the cause-chain builders, four http contracts declared beside two siblings that were listed, both cookie helpers, the file
  server path every request actually takes, and the deprecated cors doors that were half listed. Where this major's code differs, the entry says what this major does rather than what the published ones do: `config.IntWithDefault` answers the default for a value that does not parse, where they refuse it
- example: the shared icons every page links — `favicon.ico`, `assets/favicon.svg`, `assets/logo.png`, `assets/apple-touch-icon.png` — are produced by the same `npm run build` that produces the frontend bundle, so the one command a fresh clone needs for the browser interface delivers everything a browser asks for. They used to be produced only by a hand-written copy loop inside the development container's entrypoint, so anyone who did not run the Docker stack got a 404 for all four while the packaging matrix promised a binary requiring "nothing else" at runtime. They still exist in exactly one place in the tree, `<root>/.assets`, and are git-ignored under every example, so no second copy can go stale: `assets/sync-icons.mjs` owns the mapping from source name to served name and is what copies them, run as the `prebuild` step of the bundle and by the entrypoint for each major. The README also names that build step for the first time — `melody_static_embedded` freezes `public/` into the
  binary at compile time, so a binary built before it carries neither bundle nor icons and cannot gain them afterwards
- example: the comment in `.env` no longer claims that an already-set process or host environment variable overrides the value beside it. melody reads configuration only from the `.env` files and writes a warning naming each process variable it ignored, so the pattern the comment taught — commit `.env` with development values and inject the secrets through a `env:` entry in an orchestrator manifest — started the application against the values in the file with the injected secret reaching nothing. The comment now names the real override, `.env.local` or `.env.<MELODY_ENV>`, and says that a key is unset in the file rather than out of the environment
- security: `InMemoryTokenStore` no longer drops the members of `Claims` it was not written to know about. The clone named the fields it carried, so every field added to `Claims` afterwards vanished through `Put` and `Lookup` with no compile error to say so, while the redis store round-tripped through JSON and carried them — the two stores would have disagreed about exactly the members a revocation is decided on. The clone now starts from the value and replaces only what would otherwise alias the caller
- container: `HasType` canonicalises the type it is asked about, so it can no longer answer "no" for a service `GetByType` resolves happily. Registrations are filed under the canonical type — a provider returning `*T` is indexed under `*T` — and every resolution canonicalises before it looks, but the two `HasType` implementations did not: asking with the value type reported the service absent, and a caller that guards a resolution with `HasType` took the branch for a service that is registered. The scope answered the same way for an installed override, which is filed under the canonical type of the value.
- container: a provider that panics no longer leaves the resolution around it blind to its scope. Scope visibility was restored inline after the provider call, so a panic unwound through the guard's recovery and skipped the restore, and every service resolved afterwards on that resolution saw a suspended scope. Nothing observed it while no userland service could be scope-owned; it is load-bearing now that one can
- http: a scope-close failure is reported through the emergency logger when the request never got a logger of its own, instead of being dropped. The report is written after the scope has closed, which is safe only because the request logger is an installed override and a scope leaves overrides alone — so the one path that had no override to report through said nothing at all about a failure that must never go unreported
- openapi: a validation tag on a field whose type is published as a component reference is described from what that component actually is, so a `notEmpty` on a promoted collection advertises the same floor an inline one would instead of being published as impossible to satisfy. Only a type reached through a reference is affected, and only where the validator agrees: a tag the validator rejects for that shape is still advertised as unsatisfiable, and a fixed-length array keeps its own reading, since a short payload is zero-filled rather than refused
- container: a request scope closes the services it built. Only what the scope created is closed — an override placed into it from outside and a singleton that came from the root container are left alone, because the scope did not build them and does not own their lifetime
- debug: an error context that refers to itself is rendered with the loop marked instead of exhausting the stack. Redaction deliberately runs before serialization, so it walked the raw context ahead of the cycle detection `encoding/json` would have applied, and a stack overflow is a fatal error no recovery catches. The walk marks where a loop closes and keeps the surrounding keys; a container two sibling keys happen to share is not a loop and is still rendered
- application: a panic inside an http shutdown hook is logged and the drain continues, instead of being able to terminate the process. The hook recovery reached a helper that exits when the recovered value asks for an exit code, so a hook could end the process in the middle of the shutdown it was registered to participate in
- openapi: a type that reaches itself through a named collection — `type Metadata map[string]Metadata`, `type Nodes []Nodes`, or a mutual pair — is described through a component reference instead of exhausting the stack. A cycle that never passed through a struct met neither the visited set nor the component placeholder that break struct cycles, and a stack overflow is a fatal error no recovery layer can catch: because the specification is generated per request, the first client to fetch it killed the process and every restart killed it again. Only a type that previously could not be described becomes a reference, so a document that already generated is unchanged. The walk is additionally bounded, and a position that reaches the bound is described as unconstrained rather than abandoning the document
- openapi: a pointer that refers to its own type no longer live-locks the walk. Six places stripped pointers in an unbounded loop and now share one bounded dereference, where a chain that outlasts the bound is left as a pointer and each caller reads that as "not the kind I asked about". The schema walk itself keeps a bound of its own rather than sharing this one, because it has somewhere to put the answer: a chain that outlasts it is published as an undescribed nullable position instead of being declined
- lock: a renewal that never answers can no longer leave two instances believing they hold the lease. Leadership was derived from whether the last renewal returned an error, and the renewal itself ran without a deadline, so a store that stopped answering left the incumbent claiming a lease that had already lapsed while a second instance acquired it. A renewal is now bounded by a deadline of its own and leadership is true only while the lease the store actually granted is still in the future
- lock: **Behavioural change** — `RunExclusive` decides demotion from the lease clock rather than from the outcome of one renewal. The deadline on a renewal bounds the call so a wedged store cannot park it forever; it is no longer a verdict on the lock. A renewal that answers slowly still renewed — the budget was a quarter of the ttl, so a call that succeeded at forty percent of it cancelled work that was never in danger — and a single dropped renewal is now survived by the one behind it, which is the whole reason the cadence is half the ttl. Demotion follows once the lease last written is within half a cadence of lapsing. The per-call budget is also clamped to the cadence after the minimum-interval floor rather than before it: below two milliseconds both were floored independently and the budget came out LARGER than the cadence, so every attempt overlapped its successor. `LeaderGate` follows the same lease clock, with a `MaxConsecutiveRefreshFailures` threshold (three by default,
  configurable, negative to disable) for the one case the lease clock cannot see: a gate whose cadence is far denser than its lease would otherwise run leader work for the whole lease against a store that has gone. Leaving on the FIRST failed renewal turned a store failover into a cancelled term, a re-election and leader work restarted from the beginning, for a lock no other instance could have taken — the cadence is half the lease precisely so a lost renewal is survivable. Renewals that land clear the count, so scattered losses never accumulate. At the default cadence the threshold is unreachable and the lease decides, which is why the existing demotion tests are unchanged
- lock: `IsLeader` stops claiming a lease the moment it is lost, rather than when the elected hook finishes unwinding. A hook that ignores its cancelled context kept the claim alive indefinitely, so the two signals the documentation tells you to combine disagreed
- http: a handler that returns no response reaches the `kernel.response` event like every other outcome. The empty `204` was synthesized on a separate exit path that skipped the dispatch, so a listener never saw it: a cross-origin request answered that way carried no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` and was blocked by the browser, and the access log recorded status `0`. The response is normalised before the dispatch, so there is one exit path
- container: a service whose creation read an entry out of a request scope is kept in that scope and dies with it, instead of being stored in the root container. The first request to build such a service froze its overrides — the logger carrying that request's identifier, the request context — into a process-wide singleton every later request then received. A creation that reads nothing from the scope is still a singleton, and a store that would put a scope-resolved instance in the root container is refused with the service and the entries it read
- container: a service that cannot be created reports its own failure. A provider returning an error alongside a nil value had that error replaced with the generic report and demoted to the cause, so a missing registration was announced as a provider returning nothing. The generic report is kept only for a provider that returns neither a value nor an error
- config: reading a parameter no longer races the resolution that rewrites it. Guarding the incidental write left the primary one — the resolution pass over every parameter — unguarded, and a consumer holding the parameter reads its value without the configuration lock
- config: a `${...}` reference in an `.env` file resolves against every file the environment loaded, and an undefined, self- or mutually-referencing one fails the boot. The underlying loader expands each file on its own, so a reference to a key defined in a sibling file silently produced an empty string — a credential assembled that way degraded to a blank rather than refusing to start, which is the opposite of what `%env()%` does with the same mistake. A `\$` still writes a plain dollar and a single-quoted value is still literal
- http: the compression middleware emits `Vary: Accept-Encoding` on every path, including the one taken when the response already carries a `Content-Encoding` and the excluded-path and excluded-content-type paths. A publicly cacheable pre-encoded response was stored under the url alone and served to a client that had asked for a different encoding
- http: a middleware may only reference, through `before` or `after`, a middleware enabled in at least every environment it is itself enabled in. A reference to an environment-gated middleware was dropped wherever that middleware was filtered out, turning into a missing reference that failed the pipeline build and panicked the boot — so a pipeline that started in development failed to start in production. The rule weighs the declared environment sets, so the mistake fails identically in every environment instead of only where it breaks; the group a build is asked for is left to the selection, since several groups are built in one process and a reference unsatisfiable in another group says nothing about this one
- http: `NewForwardedClientIpResolver` reads a bracketed IPv6 literal that carries no port, so a trusted edge emitting `[2001:db8::1]` no longer collapses every IPv6 client onto the proxy's own rate-limit budget while IPv4 clients keep theirs. The trusted-proxy check reads the same four authority forms
- http: the compression middleware bounds the read it uses to decide whether a body reaches the compression threshold. A body reader answering zero bytes with no error pinned a request goroutine at full processor use indefinitely; the read fails the request after the same number of consecutive empty reads `bufio` tolerates
- http: the static file server no longer trims whitespace off the path it resolves, so `/%20internal/secret.json` no longer retrieves `internal/secret.json`. Access control matches on the raw request path, so the trim let a padded spelling reach a file a path-prefix rule protects — the same aliasing the router already refuses, and it also made the two build modes answer the padded spelling differently
- http: the static file server refuses a path that is not already canonical instead of serving the file `path.Clean` folds it onto. `/open/../internal/secret.json` and `//internal/secret.json` reached files that a path-prefix access-control rule, matching on the raw path, never saw. The spellings that fold onto the root still serve the index file
- http: the static file server answers `GET` and `HEAD` only. Every other method received the full file body, so an `OPTIONS` preflight for a path that resolved to a real file was answered with the file instead of the cors headers, and every asset was a `POST` endpoint answering `200`
- http: the static file server ignores `If-Modified-Since` when the request also carries `If-None-Match`, as RFC 9110 requires. A non-matching entity tag combined with an unchanged modification time answered `304`, so any deploy that preserves timestamps while changing content left revalidating caches serving the old bytes indefinitely
- http: the static file server accepts all three HTTP date formats for `If-Modified-Since` (RFC 9110 requires accepting the obsolete RFC 850 and asctime forms, not only IMF-fixdate)
- http: the static file server records a refused resolution on the streaming path. `ServeReader` is the only path any request takes — `Serve` has no caller outside its own tests — and it discarded the logger, so a traversal attempt, a symlink escape and an invalid path were all indistinguishable from an ordinary miss. A resolution that simply found no such file is recorded at debug level, along with the successful ones: the static server is consulted for every request, so anything louder would file one record per request that is not a static asset. A refused resolution — a path that is not canonical, a dot-prefixed element, a path the filesystem rejects — is still recorded at warning
- http: a route whose only segment is a trailing optional parameter is served at the root again. `/` is the one path whose trailing slash cannot be trimmed away, so it splits into two empty segments and the empty-segment guard refused the optional — while `UrlGenerator` mints exactly `/` for that shape and the OpenAPI document advertises it, so the framework answered a url it had produced itself with a `404`. The optional is treated as omitted and left unbound, as it is everywhere else
- http: a `RouteGroup` no longer writes its name prefix and its merged requirements and defaults back into the caller's `RouteOptions`. One options value reused across two grouped registrations accumulated the prefix, so the second route registered under a name nothing could generate, and the first group's requirements leaked into a later registration through a different group. **Behavioural change**: reusing one options value for two routes in the same group now raises the documented duplicate-name panic instead of silently registering the second under a doubled prefix
- http: a route declared with an empty method no longer contributes an empty token to the `Allow` header of a `405` response; an empty element is not a valid method token (RFC 9110)
- validation: a field promoted through stacked embed levels below a diamond is validated again. The dominance walk counted how many paths from the root reached a type instead of how many times one level reaches it, so an ancestor diamond cascaded its multiplicity onto every type below it and annihilated a name `encoding/json` does populate, silently skipping every constraint declared on that field. The count now increments once per embed occurrence, exactly as `encoding/json`'s own field walk counts it
- openapi: the schema mirror publishes a property promoted through stacked embed levels below a diamond, matching the validation walk it mirrors — the same multiplicity cascade dropped the property from the document while the walk dropped its constraints, so the specification and the enforcement agreed with each other and disagreed with `encoding/json`
- lock: a `LeaderGate` starts renewing its lease before `OnElected` runs, and the hook receives a runtime whose context is cancelled when the lease is lost. Nothing renewed the lease while the hook ran, so a hook slower than the ttl let the lease lapse: another replica acquired, both reported leadership, and the incumbent never demoted because demotion only follows a failed renewal — which could not happen while the hook held the campaign loop. **Behavioural change**: `OnElected` is handed a term-scoped runtime rather than the run runtime, so leader-only work that respects its context now stops when the lease is lost instead of running alongside the new holder
- messagebus: `melody:messagebus:consume` separates the shutdown signal from the handler lifetime — the signal stops the pull of new deliveries, while in-flight handlers and their `Ack`/`Nack` keep a live context for the whole `WithShutdownGrace` window. One shared context meant a signal cancelled the running handler immediately, so the grace protected nothing that respects its context, and the acknowledgement ran on the cancelled context too: any transport that honours the runtime context on publish failed the `Ack` of a message whose side effects had already committed, so the broker redelivered it on every deploy, while a failed `Nack` dropped the `RedeliveryStamp` increment and `MaxRetries` never converged. **Behavioural change**: a handler that respects its context is no longer cancelled at the signal; it is cancelled when the grace expires
- http: equal-priority middlewares now run in the order the application registered them. The pipeline resolved ties on the definition's generated name, which carries the registration counter as decimal text, so `middleware.10` sorted ahead of `middleware.2` and every factory-provided middleware sorted ahead of every directly registered one — a cors middleware registered before an authentication factory ended up inside it, and a preflight was answered `401` with no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`. Explicit `before`/`after` edges and priorities decide the order as before. **Behavioural change**: any application registering two or more middlewares at the same priority gets the nesting it asked for, which for some is a different pipeline than before
- validation: the parsed validate tag and the constraint a parameterized rule resolves to are memoized instead of being rebuilt for every value the validator reaches. A `regex` tag recompiled its pattern once per element, so a body of about 80,000 elements performed 80,000 `regexp.Compile` calls — measured at roughly 3.1 seconds of CPU before the handler ran, now about 37 milliseconds. The parse cache is keyed on the tag string and the constraint cache on the rule name and its parameters, both length-prefixed so no two distinct rules can share a key; the constraint cache is per-validator, so custom constraints registered under the same name in different validators stay separate. Tag strings come from struct tags, so neither cache can be grown by a request. **Behavioural change**: a parameterized constraint is now configured once and the same instance is reused for every value it validates, so a custom `ParameterizedConstraint` whose `WithParams` result carried per-value state, or was
  not safe for concurrent use, must be made immutable
- openapi: a struct that promotes an embedded `time.Time`'s codec is advertised as the `date-time` string `encoding/json` actually writes, instead of an object of properties that never reach the wire. The advertisement follows Go's promotion rules rather than mere reachability: the codec at the shallowest embedding depth wins, embeds tied at that depth promote nothing, and a non-struct embed competes like any other — so a depth-1 marshaler embed writing an object keeps its object schema even when a `time.Time` sits below it, where reachability alone would have promised a string for an object body. The recognition stays limited to `time.Time`, the one embed whose wire form is derivable from its type. Where reflection cannot settle which type won — a codec with a pointer receiver behind a pointer embed, a codec reached through an embed cycle — the object schema stands rather than a guess. Still advertised as objects, unchanged: a promoted `MarshalText` fallback, a user `json.Marshaler`
  embed, and an embedded marshaler interface; and a `MarshalJSON` declared on the enclosing type over a `time.Time` embed still reads as the promoted one, `reflect.Method` carrying no declaring type
