- openapi: an instantiated generic gets a component key strict validators accept and a `$ref` that resolves. Go reports the type arguments as part of the type name (`Page[github.com/acme/app/api.User]`), whose brackets fall outside the `^[a-zA-Z0-9.\-_]+$` key grammar for every instantiation, and whose import-path slashes split the `$ref` json pointer into four tokens where one was meant, so the reference resolved to nothing. Import paths are dropped and the remaining punctuation folded to a single underscore, giving a key derived only from the type and therefore stable across runs; two instantiations that fold alike are separated by the existing numeric suffix
- openapi: a route with a trailing optional parameter is documented as both of the paths it serves. The router answers `/users` and `/users/42` for `/users/:id?`, but the document carried only `/users/{id}`, silently omitting a path the server answers. OpenAPI 3.0 forbids `required: false` on `in: path`, so the two shapes are emitted as two path items sharing the described operation — summary, tags, request body and responses — with the shortened form carrying no path parameter and a distinct operation id (`<id>.without.<parameter>`, since operation ids must be unique). A route explicitly registered at the shortened path keeps its own operation, whichever order the two are registered in
- debug: `debug:router`, `debug:events`, `debug:parameters` and `debug:middleware` apply `--limit` and `--offset` to the items they render instead of only reporting them in the payload. `total` continues to report the unwindowed count and the table summary reports how many rows are shown, so a client paging with `offset += limit` walks each item exactly once instead of receiving the full list on every page. **Behavioural change**: an invocation already passing `--limit` or `--offset` received the full list and now receives a window; with `--verbose`, `debug:events` also narrows its listeners block to the windowed events
- http: the kernel loads the session inside its panic-recovery guard, so a session-storage outage answers with a logged `500` instead of escaping `ServeHttp`. Such a request previously had its connection closed by `net/http` with no response at all, the `kernel.terminate` listeners never ran and the access-log line was lost. The guard now covers every failure in that window, not only the session's
- http: `Kernel.SetSessionCookiePolicy` no longer drops the `SameSite=Lax` default when the caller names only `Path` or `Domain`. The zero `SameSite` is treated as unset and falls back to `Lax`, the same way an empty `Path` falls back to `/`, so a policy that only pinned the cookie domain no longer silently emitted no `SameSite` attribute at all. **Behavioural change**: `SameSiteDefaultMode` remains the way to omit the attribute deliberately
- http: a session is no longer stored for a response that is discarded because the handler already committed its headers — the `Set-Cookie` could never reach the client, so a first-time visitor on a streamed response left one unreachable session behind per reconnect. A session the request already names is still stored, since it needs no cookie to be reachable, and a cleared session is still destroyed
- validation: the parsed-tag and constructed-constraint memos settle on a single instance under a concurrent first touch, so every goroutine validates against the same constraint. `contract.ParameterizedConstraint` now documents what that requires: the constraint returned by `WithParams` must be immutable and safe for concurrent use, because one instance is shared for the process lifetime across all requests
- openapi: an optional-parameter marker inside braces (`{name?}`) no longer expands into two paths. The router matches brace segments literally, so the shortened path was an endpoint nothing served; the `:name?` spelling the router does support is unchanged
- cli: when a command fails and the container or scope also fails to close, the aggregated error carries the exit code itself, so the shutdown failures it reports survive to the log instead of being skipped in favour of the command's own exit error. The aggregate was previously discarded and visible only through the table-mode status line, so a json-mode run reported the command failure alone
- http: `UrlGenerator.GeneratePath` substitutes a non-empty route default for a parameter supplied with an *empty* value, not only for an absent one. A non-trailing optional segment is accepted at registration precisely because its default keeps the segment present, but generating `/:locale?/list/:page` with `{"locale": "", "page": "2"}` dropped it and produced `/list/2`, which the router answered with a `404` — the generator and the matcher disagreeing on the one class of pattern the registration guard newly admits. A required parameter supplied empty is likewise filled from a non-empty default instead of erroring; without a default it is still refused
- validation: a struct whose promoted json codec is `time.Time`'s is no longer walked for constraints. Such a value is an RFC 3339 string on the wire, so `encoding/json` fills the embedded time and populates no other field: a `notBlank` on a sibling ran against a permanent zero value and rejected **every** body the type can decode. The skip requires both halves of the codec: the wire form must be time's RFC 3339 string *and* the type must refuse an object body. Reflect cannot tell a promoted `UnmarshalJSON` from one the struct declares — same method set, same signature, same func name — so the decode question is put to `encoding/json` itself, once per type. A struct that promotes time's marshaler while declaring a decoder that accepts an object keeps its constraints enforced. The openapi mirror stays marshal-side, as an advertised schema should, so the two predicates are deliberately no longer identical; what they share is the promotion-origin walk. A tag declared inside such a struct
  is neither advertised nor enforced; an ordinary, non-embedded `time.Time` field is unaffected and its siblings stay validated

- config: registering a runtime parameter after boot no longer writes back onto a referenced parameter's resolved value, so a consumer holding the parameter pointer and reading it without a lock no longer races the write — the referenced value is stored only during boot, and the post-boot re-resolution is deterministic anyway
- container: two distinct zero-size services (`struct{}` pointers, which share one address), or a service pointer that aliases another service's first field, are each closed on teardown instead of being collapsed onto one representative by address alone and one of them silently skipped — the teardown identity now pairs the address with the concrete pointer type
- openapi: a bare unsigned integer field carries a zero floor so the spec rejects the negative numbers the decoder rejects, instead of advertising the whole negative range as valid; the floor is raise-only and never weakens an exclusive bound the validator already placed at or above zero, so a value-less `greaterThan` keeps its exclusive zero minimum and a `lessThan` at or below zero advertises an empty window. An unsigned collection element carries the same floor, so the items of a `[]uint` and the values of a `map[string]uint` no longer advertise the negatives the decoder rejects either
- cli: a command that returns an exit-coded error now exits through the application's own shutdown and logging path (structured error log, container close) instead of the cli library calling `os.Exit` from inside its run and bypassing them
- wiring: `melody:wiring:generate` accepts `--tags` (comma-separated build tags the target binary carries) so a constructor gated on `//go:build <tag>` is scanned instead of dropped, its service silently missing from the generated wiring; a build-excluded file that holds a constructor candidate is named on request through `--report-excluded`, so a missing service traces back to the tag it needs without failing `--strict` on a file legitimately excluded for another target. A build context carries plain tag identifiers, so a constraint expression such as `--tags '!postgres'` is rejected instead of silently matching nothing and leaving `--strict` green with the service still missing; the generated source is specific to the tag set it was generated with and must be built with the same tags
- openapi: `notEmpty` on a non-pointer fixed-length array (`[N]T`) no longer advertises the field as `required` with `minItems 1` — the validator measures a length that can never fall below the array's fixed size, so it never rejects the field and the spec now accepts any array length as the server does; a `*[N]T` stays required, since the validator rejects the nil pointer
- config: a parameter registered between construction and boot keeps its template for the boot pass instead of being resolved on the spot. The constructor's own resolution pass marked the configuration resolved, so every composition-root registration was resolved eagerly against whatever existed at that moment — which made registration order significant and reported a forward reference as a failure "after boot" that boot had not yet reached. The boot pass resolves them all in one order-independent batch, so registering a parameter that references one declared later now works
- http: a conditional request for a static file is answered `304 Not Modified` when the `If-None-Match` header carries the entity tag anywhere in its comma-separated list, or carries it in the weak form a proxy may have rewritten it into. The header was compared as one exact string, so both shapes missed and the whole body was re-sent. The wildcard form is deliberately still not honoured
- http: the access log records the scheme the kernel actually resolved through the configured forwarded-headers policy instead of re-detecting it without that policy. Behind a proxy that terminates TLS, every request was logged as `http` while the kernel had routed it as `https` and issued the session cookie `Secure` — so the log contradicted the request it described, for all traffic
- openapi: a zero-length fixed array carrying `notEmpty` is advertised as unsatisfiable instead of merely floored — the validator measures a length fixed at zero, so it rejects every payload, while the spec promised a non-empty array would be accepted. A bare unsigned request or response type carries the zero floor the struct fields and collection elements already carried, so it no longer advertises the negative range its decoder refuses
- http: a handler that panics with `net/http`'s `ErrAbortHandler` now aborts the connection silently, as that sentinel documents, instead of being converted into a 500 response plus an error log line — a reverse proxy raises it on every client that disconnects mid-stream
- logging: the json logger takes the same lock for closing its writer that it takes for writing to it, and drops later writes instead of handing them to a closed writer. A goroutine that outlives the container teardown could be inside a write while the writer was being closed, which is a data race for any writer holding state, and its line was discarded with no diagnostic either way
- serializer: accept-header negotiation gives each available media type the quality of the most specific range covering it, so an exact range wins over a wildcard whatever the header order, and a range carrying `q=0` refuses that type instead of being ignored. A request that explicitly refuses every type the application can produce is answered `406 Not Acceptable` rather than being served the very type it rejected; a request that simply matches nothing available still receives the default representation, as before
- bag: a key present with a nil value reports as unset from `String`, `StringSlice`, `StringStrict` and `StringSliceStrict`, matching what `Int`, `Bool`, `Float64` and `Duration` already reported for the same state — `Has` and the typed accessors no longer contradict each other. **Behavioural change**: a caller that distinguished a present-but-nil value from an absent one reads `ParameterBag.Get` for that distinction
- cache: the single-flight waiter counter uses the atomic wrapper type rather than a bare 64-bit field, so its alignment no longer depends on where it happens to sit in the struct; on a 32-bit build a bare field that loses its alignment panics on every counted call
- event: two distinct subscribers whose types carry no fields no longer collapse onto one registration, so removing either one leaves the other's listeners registered instead of silently unregistering both. Every zero-size allocation in Go shares one address, so the registration key now pairs that address with the subscriber's concrete type, the same identity the container teardown uses
- http: a request path that differs from a route only by leading or trailing whitespace no longer reaches that route's handler. The path was trimmed before matching, so `/admin%20`, `/admin%09`, `/admin%0a` and `/admin%c2%a0` all resolved to `/admin` — aliases a reverse proxy, ingress or firewall rule matching the exact path never sees, so a rule denying `/admin` could be walked around. Route patterns are still trimmed at registration
- http: an empty path segment no longer satisfies a named route parameter, so `/users//profile` no longer matches `/users/:id/profile` and binds an empty identifier a handler cannot distinguish from a supplied one. A trailing catch-all still matches its bare prefix, which is what the url generator emits for an absent remainder. The url generator refuses to mint such a path in the first place, so the two stay in agreement
- http: the compression middleware no longer panics on a response whose header map is nil, matching the guard the cors middleware already carries. The response normalizer repairs such a response only after the middleware chain has unwound, which is too late for a middleware that runs inside it
- logging: `EnsureLogger` replaces a typed nil logger with the no-op logger instead of returning it unchanged and letting the first call panic — the case the function exists to guard
- container: two distinct services of one type that carries no fields are each closed at teardown instead of one of them being silently skipped. Every zero-size allocation shares a single address, so pairing that address with the concrete type still could not tell them apart; a genuine alias is one whose value came through the resolver from the other service, and that relationship is what the dependency graph already records, so an unrelated node keeps its own representative while a resolver-mediated alias is still closed exactly once
- cache: the in-memory backend no longer holds its exclusive lock across the whole map. The periodic expiry sweep takes the keys once and expires them in chunks, releasing the lock between them, and eviction from a full cache probes a bounded number of least-recently-used entries for an expired victim instead of walking the entire recency list on every insert. `Get` takes the same exclusive lock to touch that list, so both walks stalled every concurrent request in proportion to the number of entries
- example: the embedded static build embeds dot-prefixed and underscore-prefixed paths (`all:public`), so it serves the same file set as the filesystem build — a `.well-known` directory added to `public/` would previously have been served by one build and reported missing by the other. The committed frontend route manifest artifact was regenerated and now carries every exposed route
- cors: an `OPTIONS` request carrying an `Origin` but no `Access-Control-Request-Method` is no longer treated as a preflight. A browser sends that header on every preflight, so no real one is lost; what changes is that a plain `OPTIONS` now reaches the router instead of being answered with a 204 advertising the whole configured method list. A route registered for `OPTIONS` was unreachable from an allowed origin — only from an origin the service rejected — and every middleware inner to cors, a rate limiter among them, never saw such a request. The cors response headers are still applied to the answer on the way out
- cors: `Vary: Origin` is emitted on every response, not only on one whose origin was allowed. A response produced for a rejected origin, or for a request carrying no `Origin` header at all, carried no `Vary`, so a shared cache keyed on the url alone could store it and later hand that header-less body to an allowed cross-origin requester, whose browser then blocks the response. The header is still added at most once, since the emission scans the tokens already present
- cors: `NewService` no longer panics on a credentialed configuration that decides origins through `AllowOriginFunc`. An empty `AllowOrigins` is defaulted to the wildcard before the credentials guard runs, so the guard refused a wildcard the service never consults once a function is set. A credentialed configuration with neither an origin list nor a function still panics on that defaulted wildcard, which is the combination the guard exists to prevent
- example: the in-memory repositories guard their slice with a read-write mutex, and `All` hands back a copy of it. Every repository is a process-wide singleton and every request is served on its own goroutine, so a listing request ranging the returned slice raced a deleting request rewriting it — both the slice header and the backing array, which `DeleteById` compacts in place under a caller that is still iterating it
- example: the api error presenter emits the raw error message, the concrete Go type and the unwrap chain only when the kernel environment is the development one, the same gate the framework exception listener applies, and stays closed when that environment cannot be resolved at all. The presenter read no configuration, so a production 500 response carried the whole cause chain byte-identically to a development one. The status code, the timestamp, the request identifier and the caller-supplied message stay unconditional
- example: the openapi descriptors bind the handler's own request and response types instead of hand-written copies of them declared beside the registry. Both copies had drifted: the create-product body documented two of the seven fields, so the body the published specification advertised — `{"name":"Widget","price":10}` — was answered `400` by the very route it described, missing `categoryId`, `currencyId` and `description`, and neither `id`, `name` nor `stock` carried its real bounds; the documented `201` payload carried three of the nine fields the handler returns, and the greeting payload three of its own minus `cart`. The types are now exported from their handler packages and referenced directly, so a field added to a handler cannot go undocumented
- documentation: fifteen exported symbols this major has always carried are documented at last — `cache.Item` and `cache.NewItem`, `logging.LoggerFromResolver` and `LoggerMustFromResolver`, `output.NewTablePrinter`, `NewMeta`, `NewWarning`, `NewError`, `NewErrorCause`, `security.NewFirewall`, `NewFirewallManager`, `NewFirewallRegistry`, and `application.MiddlewareFactory`, `RouteRegistrar` and `SecurityModule` — along with three caveats only one major carried: that `InMemoryBackend` owns a cleanup goroutine which stops on `Close`, that `NewJsonLogger` serializes writes through an internal mutex, and that `ApiKeyHeaderAuthenticator` compares with `crypto/subtle.ConstantTimeCompare` so timing does not leak the expected key. `CONFIG.md` gains the environment key table, the kernel parameter names and the environment/mode/role constants it never carried while v1 documented them, and `APPLICATION.md` gains the middleware ordering constants.
- debug: a secret parameter whose value is nil renders as `(empty)` instead of the mask. `fmt` renders nil as a non-empty placeholder, so the redaction reported a value being present — the opposite of the one answer the secret column exists to give

- cli: `output.MergeFlags` refuses a duplicated flag name at the line that declares it, and refuses a nil flag. The parser resolves a name to the first declaration, so a command-specific flag reusing a standard name was silently inert — its default and its validator never applied — while the help listed the name twice. **Behavioural change**: a registration that collided now fails at the declaration instead of running with the flag it thought it had
- cli: `TableBlockBuilder.AddRow` refuses a row whose cell count disagrees with the block's declared columns, naming the block and both counts. The width pass and the printing pass both iterate the columns, so a surplus cell was never rendered at all and the report looked complete. **Behavioural change**: the single-token separator row stays exempt and a block with no columns stays valid
- cli: the standard integer flags — `--verbosity`, `--limit`, `--offset`, `--table-width` — refuse a negative value at parsing, naming the flag, the way `--format` and `--order` have always refused an unsupported one. Zero means unlimited for the limit and "from the start" for the offset, and a negative was clamped to zero, so an argument asking for at most `-5` items silently delivered every one of them with exit 0. The clamp in `NormalizeOption` stays as the defensive floor for an `Option` assembled in code
- cli: the table format prints the warnings under `--quiet` too, and renders the envelope error whole — message, code, details and cause. `StandardFlags` defaults `--quiet` to true, so a command's own `AddWarning` was invisible in the default format at any verbosity, and the envelope error was rendered nowhere in the table format at all: the one-line red status was the whole report, while the code, the details and the cause existed only in the json document. **Behavioural change**
- cli: a failure reported through the rendered envelope reaches the application log. The exit-coded error the renderer returns travels unmarked, so the exit path files it: the rendered report lives only on the output streams, and a run that failed was invisible to anything reading the log file
- cli: a table printing failure fails the command. Every write threw its error away, so a report truncated by a full disk was crowned with a success banner and exit 0 — the opposite of the json path, which failed on the same disk. The first write failure is remembered and returned
- cli: a command that dies by panic prints the failed banner instead of `[finished] [success]`. The finish banner reads the command error, which the unwinding path never assigns, so a dead command was reported as a successful one; the panic itself is re-raised unchanged, an `*exception.ExitError` keeping its exit code
- cli: a command or a substituted runtime that hands back its error through a concrete typed pointer is read as the success it means. A typed nil boxed into a non-nil interface passed the nil comparison and reached `Error()` on a nil receiver at the very line that renders it, turning a successful run into a masked panic
- cli: the container is left to the handler that owns the process exit rather than closed by the command action. That handler resolves the final record's logger through the container and closes it between the record and the exit, so closing it earlier handed the handler a closed logger and downgraded a failed command's final record to the stderr fallback. The scope stays the action's to close, and its failure the action's to report
- cli: the start and finish banners and the command-not-found header keep the promise of `--no-color`. A `--no-color` run redirected into a file carried escape codes around an output that honoured the flag, and the suggestion header printed ansi beside a table rendered colourless by its own option
- cli: `output.NewMeta` keeps a `version.Go` the caller supplied instead of always overwriting it with the running toolchain's — the only one of the three version fields without that fallback
- cli: `output.NormalizeOption` clamps a negative `VerbosityLevel` to zero, the way it already clamped the limit, the offset and the table width. A negative level travelled into the `Option` beside `Verbose=false`, two answers to one question
- application: the record that explains a dying process is written through a logger that still writes, before the teardown instead of after it. `Run` deferred the teardown above the exit handler, so the teardown ran first and closed the very file logger the final record was then written through — the record of the dying error survived only as a one-line stderr echo
- application: the final record of a process that dies without a live container logger is written to the destination the configuration names, not only to stderr.
- application: a boot that dies after the container was built closes it between the record and the exit — `os.Exit` runs no defer, so the exit handler's before-exit hook is what tears it down, and the log file the logger service held open from that moment is released instead of leaking into the exit
- application: a teardown failure on Run's normal return exits non-zero.
- application: two closes racing each other report the one teardown failure once.
- application: the process-boundary exit handler survives an application assembled without `NewApplication`.
- application: a relative `MELODY_LOG_PATH` is anchored to the project directory — the rule `ensureRuntimeDirectories` already applies to the logs and cache directories, now through the one shared helper — and the file's parent directory is created before the open.
- application: the logger provider reads the module configuration before it opens the log file.
- logging: `LogOnRecoverAndExit` and `LogOnRecoverAndExitAfter` refuse an exit code outside 1..255 with a panic naming the rule — the rule `exception.NewExitError` has always enforced, applied to the code the handler itself would exit with.
- logging: a recovered `*exception.ExitError` carrying an out-of-range code is no longer honored as an exit.
- logging: a recovered typed-nil `*exception.ExitError` — the value someone panicked with — is normalized as a plain panic value instead of dereferencing the nil receiver inside the last handler of the process, and `LogOnRecoverAndExit` survives an exit error carrying no error value
- logging: each step of the exit handler — the record, the before-exit teardown — is abandoned after ten seconds instead of being waited on forever.
- logging: every step of the exit handler runs under its own shield, the resolve of the recovered value included — a recovered value whose `Error()` panics is answered with a generic record under the caller's own exit code, where its panic used to unwind into `main` and the process died with the Go runtime's exit code 2: no record, no certificate, no stderr echo, no before-exit teardown
- logging: the exit handler writes one certificate record at emergency level — "process exiting after unrecovered error", with the exit code and the error in its context — through whatever logger it resolved, always.
- logging: `LogOnRecover` logs the exit wrapper that carries no error value as the anomaly it is.
- logging: an error logged by `LogOnRecover` is marked as logged whether or not it panics again.
- logging: the recover helpers capture the stack of the panic still in flight.
- logging: the correlation decorator writes the real id under the context key unconditionally — a non-empty string already under the key made the decorator keep it and drop the id, so whatever wrote that value forged the correlation of every later record; a different string claim now survives beside the real id under the key suffixed `Claimed`
- logging: `jsonLogger.Close` recognizes the process console by identity — the `os.Stdout` and `os.Stderr` values themselves — instead of by file name.
- exception: `NewExitError` refuses an exit code outside `[1, 255]`.
- exception: the exit error's accessors answer the nil receiver instead of dereferencing it — `errors.As` matches the type on a typed-nil link and reports success with a nil pointer, which the callers that decide how the process ends read the code straight off
- config: `MarkSecret` after the boot resolution travels to every parameter that reads the marked key, and follows the marking to a fixpoint — a reader of a freshly marked name is scanned in turn, so a whole derivation chain is covered however late the mark arrives, the way the early marking always did — the late call redacted the key while the dsn assembled from it printed in full in `debug:parameters`, and the end-of-boot retry reported that unapplied markings had landed when their propagation had nothing left to travel through
- httpclient: a basic credential travels whenever the caller asked for one, empty halves included — an api key spelled as the password of an empty user is the ordinary shape of curl's `-u :key`, and the username guard dropped the whole credential and sent the request unauthenticated with nothing to say so; a typed-nil authorization or basic half now leaves the header unwritten instead of dereferencing nil on the request path
- httpclient: an invalid response-body cap is refused before anything is dialled, on the buffered and the streaming path alike — validated after the exchange, a POST that had already committed its side effect answered with an error phrased as though nothing had been sent, and a caller retrying on it duplicated the operation; the refusal carries the cap, the method and the sanitized url instead of a bare message
- clock: `FrozenClock.Advance` is forward-only and panics on a negative duration — moving the frozen clock backwards silently broke the monotonic invariants the code under test relies on; `TravelTo` remains the deliberate door for backwards motion
- clock: the frozen ticker's `Stop` returns only after its relay goroutine has exited, so no tick can be minted after `Stop` returns — a Stop-then-TravelTo sequence used to find the traveled time in the channel of a stopped ticker, a tick `time.Ticker` can never produce
- httpclient: a negative per-request timeout bounds a stream instead of unbounding it — a caller computing what is left of a deadline that has already passed hands over a negative duration, and the streaming path turned that exhausted budget into a stream with no deadline at all; it now folds into the configured timeout, the way the buffered path always read it
- httpclient: `StreamResponse.Body` after `Close` answers a reader that fails on the first read instead of a nil one — a watchdog goroutine closing an indefinite stream while the consumer is deciding to read is the ordinary shape, and a consumer written as `io.Copy(destination, streamResponse.Body())` dereferenced that nil and took the process down; the stand-in reader's own `Close` succeeds, so a deferred close stays correct
- security: the in-memory token store reads the `IssuedAt` stamp under the same critical section that inserts the entry, on `Put` and `PutWithTtl` alike, so a revocation boundary can no longer be published between the stamp and the insert — a "revoke everything, then log in again" sequence could find the fresh login stamped before the boundary and born already revoked, bouncing the user the revocation was carried out for
- security: `newJwtTokenValidator` refuses a negative `RevocationEpochSkew` at construction. The allowance exists to WIDEN a revocation boundary against clock skew, and a negative value carried it backwards instead — tokens issued before the revocation verified again, a bypass reachable from a config typo, with the redis store's option guarding the sign while the validator did not
- security: the JWT validator keeps its own copy of the configured secret, the way the HMAC secret provider has always copied on ingest — retained by reference, the caller's slice stayed mutable under every later signature check, so zeroing or rotating it in place silently changed what the validator verified against
- security: an infrastructure failure under a token source is filed at Error, told apart from the routine Info a bad credential earns. Every rejection already failed closed to anonymous, but the single funnel logged a nonce guard whose backend is down, a token store that cannot answer and a revocation epoch store outage with the same Info line as a forged signature — a whole fleet silently degrading every caller to anonymous produced no record above Info anywhere. The bearer, HMAC and impersonation paths distinguish the two; the impersonation resolver's error, previously dropped on the floor, now travels with its record either way

- http: a `Response` typed nil — the zero value of a `*Response` a listener or handler left unassigned — is normalized to the nil it means at every door it can enter: the kernel events store it as absent, `writeResponse` and the public `WriteToHttpResponseWriter` read the interface through `IsNilInterface`, the handler result falls through to the empty 204, and `closeDiscardedResponseBody` no longer dereferences it inside the recovery defer. Carried as a value, the first `Headers()` call panicked at the writer — on the panic-recovery path a second panic after recover had run, so `ServeHttp` returned nothing and the connection reset
- http: the first `router.Match` call reads its result through the nil guard the second call four lines below always had — the contract returns a pointer and a flag, so an implementation reporting "no match" as nil dereferenced above the recovery defer, closing the connection with no response, no terminate event and no access-log line
- http: the session is read through `IsNilInterface` where the kernel loads it — the manager is a replaceable service, and an implementation reporting "not found" with a nil pointer of its own session type was taken for a live session, `NewSession` skipped, and the response path dereferenced it inside the recovery defer
- http: the application's error handler runs under the kernel's own recovery at all three call sites — it is called while the failed response's body is still open and held only by the caller, so a panic escaping it unwound past every close the kernel performs, leaked that body, and on the recovery path reset the connection; the panic is logged with the stack of its site and answered by the default error response
- http: a last recovery guard covers the window the main one cannot — everything between the scope opening and the main guard's installation (the request logger, the request context override, the config and dispatcher resolutions, the routing), plus a panic raised inside the main guard itself after it has already recovered once. A panic there used to escape `ServeHttp` with the connection reset, no record, no access-log line; it is now answered 500 with an emergency record carrying the request id, the method, the path and the panic stack
- http: the main recovery's record carries the stack of the panic site (`panicStack`), captured inside the recovering defer where the frames are still live — the error value alone names the symptom, never the line that raised it, and net/http's own stack print never fires for a panic this recovery absorbs
- http: the already-logged mark is read through `exception.IsAlreadyLogged` at every kernel reader — the concrete `*exception.Error` assertion it replaces saw no mark on a marked `HttpException`, nor on anything wrapping a marked error, so a failure the application had already recorded was filed a second time; the exception listener reads the same mark and attaches its request coordinates to the error instead of rewriting a duplicate record
- http: an outer middleware that panics after its `next()` returned no longer leaks the response the chain produced — a recording shim under every middleware layer publishes each layer's returned response to the recovery defer, which closes it when nothing else holds it; a file-backed body was otherwise pinned for the life of the process
- http: `BindJson` saturates its one-byte-over-limit allowance at the top of the int64 range instead of wrapping it negative — at `MaxInt64` every body read as empty and answered 400 "empty request body" — and its refusals carry their causes: the read error that stopped a body and the decoder's own diagnosis (offending offset, field, type) reach the log instead of the flat message alone
- http: a `*MaxBytesError` a handler surfaced — multipart is the one body path the kernel does not pre-read, so `ParseMultipartForm` past the limit refuses at the handler — is answered 413 "payload too large" like every other oversized body, not rendered as a 500 at error level
- http: `SetCookie` writes through a nil header map instead of panicking into it — `SetHeaders(nil)` is a state the contract permits, and on the session paths the write landed on a response the handler had already produced, on the panic-recovery path as a second panic after recover had run
- http: `SetForwardedHeadersPolicy` copies the trusted proxy list instead of retaining the caller's slice — every request's proxy-trust decision reads that list, and a caller reusing its slice after the call rewrote the trust decision mid-serving, as an unsynchronized write racing every request goroutine
- http: a response that carries the session cookie is kept out of shared caches — the `public` token is dropped and `private` added unless the response already says `private` or `no-store` — because a response stored under its URL alone by a shared cache and replayed to another client hands that client the first one's session id
- http: the session-persistence records carry a one-way reference to the session id — a SHA-256 truncated to 16 hex characters — beside the request method and the path; the id is a live bearer credential, and logging it verbatim handed anyone with log-read access a cookie value they could present as that user. A write suppressed because the response was already committed says so at warning instead of staying silent, which made a rotation on a committed response — both entries gone, both calls reporting success — indistinguishable from a first-time visitor on a stream
- http: a response-write failure the client caused — the request context net/http cancels on disconnect, a broken pipe, a connection reset — is recorded at warning under its own name, with the method and path; at error every impatient download paged the operator, for a class net/http suppresses entirely
- http: a discarded response body whose `Close` panics is contained — the close runs inside the kernel's recovery defer, where a body whose `Close` dereferences the state the panic invalidated raised a second panic past the recovery and reset the connection with nothing served; the failure is logged, never served
- http: `RecoverToError` normalizes a typed-nil error to the generic branch — passed through as-is it read as a non-nil error whose `Error()` dereferences a nil receiver — and the debug-mode response body renders the error's text under a recover, so a value whose `Error()` panics degrades to a named rendering failure instead of a second panic while the first is being rendered
- http: an empty request path is read as the root. Answered as no segments at all, the tree walk reached only the tree root, where a route registered as `/` does not live, so a request whose target normalized to nothing answered 404 against an application that had registered `/`. The same door makes an empty route PATTERN — which `JoinPaths` produces legitimately under a root group — register the root route instead of a phantom that was resolvable by name and generable, unreachable by every request, and holder of the dispatch identity that then refused the real `/` as a duplicate
- http: a request target that is not a path is no longer path-routed. net/http hands the asterisk-form of `OPTIONS` through as the path `*`, which the splitter read as a single segment, so a server-wide OPTIONS was offered to every `/:param` route in the application and bound the asterisk as that parameter's value
- http: the abort sentinel no longer leaks the response it aborts. `http.ErrAbortHandler` was re-raised ten lines before the kernel captured the response in flight and seventy before either close ran, so a deliberate abort raised by a middleware after its `next()` returned dropped the only reference to a file-backed response and leaked one descriptor per aborted request. The comment on `invokeErrorHandlerSafely` already refused to honour the sentinel for exactly this reason
- http: a route pattern written in a parameter syntax the router does not speak is refused at registration. The router binds `:name` and `*name...`, so `/users/{id}` — the spelling every other Go router and every openapi document uses — registered a route matching only the literal url `/users/%7Bid%7D`, binding nothing, refused by no validator, with the url generator emitting the braces back unescaped
- http: a route whose locale declaration contradicts its pattern is refused at registration, in both directions. Declaring `Locales` with no `_locale` in the pattern or the defaults made the route dead for every url, silently; carrying `:_locale` with no `Locales` list left the segment binding whatever the client sent, which the kernel then published verbatim as the request's locale
- bag: `ParameterBag.Set` allocates the map the zero value of this exported type does not carry, so `ParameterBag{}` accepts a write instead of panicking inside the assignment
- container: a resolution performed after the teardown finished is refused instead of being answered out of the maps. The maps still hold what was built, and answering out of them handed the caller a service every `Close` in the process has already run on, with a nil error saying it was fine — a caller that kept a resolver got a handle to a dead service and no way to know it
- container: the panic a service's `Close` raises is recorded with its cause and its stack. The containment boundary is the only place the frames that ran still exist, and an error-shaped panic value now travels as the CAUSE rather than only as its own stringified message — the context map and the cause chain of the very error the `Close` raised used to reach no record at all
- container: `MustGet` and `MustGetByType` panic with the failure the way `FromResolver` and `FromResolverByType` return it — a melody error travels out whole with the service name or type written into its context in place, and only a foreign error is wrapped naming it; the container's own `MustGet` delegates to the resolver context the way `MustGetByType` always has, so the two doors dress a failure identically
- container: the wrapper a coalesced waiter receives for a failed creation inherits the already-logged mark of the failure it carries — the mark is read at the nearest implementer, so a fresh unmarked wrapper shadowed it and every coalesced waiter filed a full record for the one failure the owner already logged
- container: `FromResolverByType` dresses its failure the way `FromResolver` does — a melody error travels out whole with the service type written into its context, a foreign error is wrapped naming the type — instead of returning the raw error with nothing to say which resolution failed
- container: `FromResolver` and `FromResolverByType` refuse a nil resolver with an error naming the service — or the type — instead of dereferencing it on the next line. The crash surfaced as an opaque nil-pointer panic of the runtime, not an error value, so recoverers classed it as a panic and no context named the unwired resolver; `Lazy` defers the call, so the crash also appeared far from the wiring line that produced it
- container: a typed-nil error from a resolver implemented outside the package reads as the success it means, on both the name and the type path. Read as a failure, it entered `errors.As` — where `Unwrap` on a nil receiver panics — or was wrapped as a false "service not registered"; on the type path it passed RAW to `exception.FromError`, which reads a typed nil as nil, so `Panic(nil)` replaced the whole report
- container: a value built while its store was refusing — the scope it was built for closed while the provider ran — is closed best-effort before the resolution fails, the scope-side twin of the container-close race whose value the guard already closed: nothing else holds it, so it leaked whole
- container: a provider panicking with a typed-nil error no longer takes the process down. The recovery's error assertion passed — a non-nil interface whose `Error()` dereferences a nil receiver — and the handler runs with the container mutex unlocked, so the second panic unwound through the caller's deferred `Unlock` as a fatal unlock-of-unlocked-mutex, with every waiter on the creation parked forever. The typed nil is normalized away, and the context rendering is contained on its own, so an error whose rendering panics costs its context and nothing else
- container: a provider declared with a concrete error type can succeed. `func(...) (*T, *MyErr)` boxes a nil `*MyErr` into a NON-nil error interface, so a healthy service failed every resolution and the typed-nil cause exploded at the first logging; a typed-nil error is the provider saying "no error", and it is normalized to exactly that
- container: the owner of a finished creation drops its waiters' wait-graph edges under the lock that wakes them. A woken waiter clears its own edge only after re-acquiring the mutex, and in that window the edge is stale: the owner's next resolution read it and fabricated a "circular dependency across concurrent resolutions" between two resolutions sharing nothing but the lock they queued on — a transient 500 at warm-up, unreproducible by the time anyone looked
- container: an override installed while the provider ran wins the slot. An override answers before anything is built, so blindly overwriting it revoked an installation the overrider was told succeeded — and the creation overwrote the NAME but not the TYPE, so the name-keyed and type-keyed maps diverged permanently. The store hands the occupant back, the creation that lost closes the value it built, and the resolution answers the override
- container: an override replacing an instance the container itself built no longer leaks it. The replaced instance left the only maps the close sweep reads, and nothing else held it; it waits in a graveyard the teardown closes under the same identity marks that keep a pointer handed to several names from closing twice — not an inline `Close`, which would close it under callers still using it. An evicted override stays its installer's, on the container and on the scope's `ClosedWithScope` filings alike
- container: a close that both fails and cycles reports the cycle's nodes. The node list survived only in the branch without failures, so the one close that both failed and cycled reported WHICH services failed but not which ones cycled, and the operator got half the diagnosis
- container: a melody error passes back through `FromResolver` whole, with the service name added to its context in place. The re-wrap shed the log level, the already-logged mark, the capture stack and every wrapper above it, so a refusal that was already logged was re-logged at the kernel with an escalated level
- container: a scope's teardown collapses the name and type filings of one instance into one node before it orders anything. One value filed under both is two teardown nodes, and a dependency edge recorded against either has to constrain the one close that actually happens: the type alias carries no edges of its own, so it was free to close the shared instance ahead of a dependent that was still open — and a non-pointer value neither comparable nor addressable was closed TWICE, once under each node. The link from the type node to the name node is written at the moment of the dual filing, the only place that knows the two are one; the teardown collapses along it, merging the edges and taking the alias group's creation stamp from its oldest member, and the pointer and value marks still catch whatever the links do not cover
- container: the teardown closes a service before what it resolved through a resolver it KEPT, not only before what it resolved during construction. The dependency edge was read off the live resolution stack, which is empty the moment a provider returns, so a service that holds its resolver and reaches through it later — a `container.Lazy` handle at first use, any replay of deferred work — recorded no edge at all and the teardown fell back to closing the pair by creation order, which for a late resolution closes the dependency FIRST and runs the holder's own `Close` over a service that has already ended. The view of the resolution handed to a provider now carries the node that provider is building, and a resolution made through it afterwards belongs to that node. A handle built over the CONTAINER keeps the old behaviour, because there is no node the container could be said to belong to. The suspension a container provider runs under rides on that same handed view rather than being written onto the shared context and undone afterwards, so a provider that panics can no longer leave the caller's own resolution blind to its scope
- container: a scoped dependency the scope already holds is depended on as hard as one this resolution builds. The edge was recorded only on the path that CREATED the service, so whether the ordering guarantee held depended on which resolution happened to come first: a scoped dependent that found its scoped dependency already in the scope recorded nothing, and the teardown ordered the two by creation stamp — which is exactly backwards when the dependency was installed after its dependent was built. Both the by-name and the by-type early returns now record the edge
- container: a scoped parent writes no edge into the container's dependency graph. The container's teardown walks that graph only over the representatives the container itself created, so a scope-keyed dependent is skipped there, while the scope's own teardown reads only the scope's graph — the entry could never be consulted from either side. Nothing pruned it either, so a live-scope registration under a request-derived name left one permanent entry per name for the life of the process
- container: a resolution with nothing to write takes the container's READ lock instead of its exclusive one. Every resolution took the exclusive lock, even one that only reads a singleton built long ago, so dependency injection had a hard ceiling that did not move with the number of cores — the http kernel alone resolves four services per request, and an application cannot route around it without giving up the container. Nothing to write means all three at once: no scope layered over the resolution, no node to record an edge for, and the instance already built; a resolution that would record an edge — including one made through a resolver a provider kept — still falls through to the exclusive path that writes the graph. The fast path answers out of the map, so it is the path that has to ask whether the map still means anything: a resolution performed after the teardown finished never reaches the creation guard that refuses a closed container, and was answered with a handle to a service every `Close` in the process had already run on, beside a nil error saying it was fine
- container: `Replacing()`'s documentation says what the code does. The sentence on the option and on `RegisterOptions.ReplacesContainerService` described a waiver that works in both directions and is inert where nothing collides. The code has neither property: the flag is read by the scoped registration paths alone — a container registration declaring it gains nothing — and declaring it where nothing collides is REMEMBERED, so a container registration of the same name arriving later is admitted without declaring anything itself. The waiver also covers the name and the registered type together, which the old sentence did not mention at all
- example: the scoped request-trail service leaves the `service.` namespace the framework reserves for its own. It was the one scoped registration in the example under a protected name, and it is now refused at boot by the rule above; the container-lifetime services of the example keep their `service.example.` spelling, which registration does admit
- exception: a foreign error whose `Context()` panics no longer takes down the recovery that is reporting it. `renderErrorText` already contained a panicking `Error()`, but the provider's context ran bare in five places — at `LogContext`'s top provider, in its cause-context walk, and in the three `FromError*` constructors that run on the same recovery paths — so the panic unwound through the recovery defer as a second panic while the first was being rendered. The context is read under a recover, and a panicking one costs the context alone, with the panic value kept in its place
- httpclient: a client configured with a base url refuses a target that leaves that origin, judged on the RESOLVED url. The headers and the authorization the client was configured with would otherwise travel to a host the target string chose — the very leak the redirect policy exists to stop, one hop earlier; a caller that talks to more than one origin builds a client without a base url
- httpclient: a `[]byte` request body is copied instead of aliased. `net/http` writes the request body on its own goroutine and `Do` returns as soon as the response headers arrive, so a server that answers without draining the body leaves the transport reading the caller's slice after the call returned — a pooled buffer reused right after a request was a data race and torn bytes on the wire
- httpclient: a nil `RequestOption` is refused instead of called. An option chosen by a condition whose other branch produced nothing is a wiring mistake, and calling it was a nil function call on the request path, outside any recovery this package owns; the refusal names the index, on the buffered and the streaming path alike
- httpclient: `NewHttpClient` refuses a nil configuration where the wiring mistake is made, instead of dying on an anonymous nil dereference inside the constructor
- httpclient: the errors of a failed exchange say which request failed. `failed to read response body` and `response body exceeded max size` carry the method, the sanitized url and the status code; `failed to create request` and the parse refusals carry the method and the sanitized url; `unsupported body type` names the type it was handed
- httpclient: a url carried into an error no longer carries the secret in it. A `*url.Error` embeds the whole request url — query string included — and net/url's parse error quotes the url it refused, userinfo included, so the most ordinary failure there is, a refused connection, wrote a `WithQuery` token or a userinfo password into the log record; the sanitized url now sits in the error context, the diagnosis survives as the cause, and the redirect-cap record sanitizes the url it names
- httpclient: `Response.Headers`/`Body` state their single-owner contract, `contract.StreamResponse` states the close obligation and the after-`Close` reading of `Body`, and `contract.HttpClient.RequestStream` says the caller closes on every path — the sentences the released majors carry and this major's contracts did not

- debug: the redaction of an error context sees the DEFINED type it is handed. `sanitizeErrorContextValue` matched only `value.(map[string]any)`, which fails for `exceptioncontract.Context` — a defined type with that exact underlying — so the redaction was a no-op on the one type the framework's own errors carry, and both `fmt` fallbacks below printed the raw `stack` and `panicStack` keys. The conversion is made through reflection on the underlying type, keeping the backing pointer so the cycle keying still identifies the same map
- debug: the error-context walk is bounded in depth and keeps its cycle guard scoped to the current path. A nested cycle passed all three guards at once: `json.Marshal` answered it with an error, which routed the value into the `fmt` fallback — and `fmt` has no cycle detection, so a service-resolution error whose context holds itself recursed until the goroutine stack was gone. A stack overflow is a fatal error no recover turns into a reported failure, so `debug:container` did not degrade its document, it killed the process. One map handed to two sibling keys is not a cycle and is not marked as one
- debug: the error-context json is truncated to the table-cell width only in the table format. Applied unconditionally, the json document carried a fragment cut at 220 bytes — unparseable, with nothing in it saying anything had been lost
- debug: `debug:container` reports WHY a service failed to build, not only that it did. The report read `getErr.Error()`, which is the message of a melody error alone, so a refused dial, a missing file or a rejected credential — exactly what an operator runs the command to learn — reached neither the table nor the json. The causes render as `caused by` lines in the table, as `errorCauseChain` in the json items and on the envelope error's cause details
- debug: the error context is read through the `ContextProvider` contract rather than through `*exception.Error` alone, so an `HttpException` or a userland context-bearing error in the resolution chain contributes its context instead of nothing
- debug: the `debug:container` list summary orders its segments `total | shown | ok | error`. Computed over the window but printed beside the global total, `50 total | 8 ok | 2 error` read as though forty services were neither — when they were simply not probed. The shown count now precedes the split, which scopes it
- debug: `NewMiddlewareCommand` refuses a nil provider by panic and `Run` returns a named refusal for a zero-value command. Each value the provider RETURNED was guarded and the provider itself was not, so `NewMiddlewareCommand(nil)` was a bare call of a nil function far from the wiring mistake that made it — the class of `RegisterCliCommand` and the module doors. A factory that panics under `--build` renders as a failure with its cause instead of killing the command
- debug: a route attribute that cannot be represented no longer takes the process down. `debug:router --format=json` handed userland's arbitrary `any` straight to `json.Marshal`, and one unserializable value emptied the whole envelope — zero bytes on stdout. A cycle-guarded walk runs first, for the same reason the error context needs one, and only a value that still cannot marshal degrades to its `%v` rendering
- debug: the `debug:router` comparator is total. Two routes may share pattern and methods and still be distinct at dispatch — differing in host, priority or requirements — and under an unstable sort a partial comparator flipped their rows between runs of the very command that exists to show which of them answers
- documentation: `DEBUG.md` said the registered command's action closes `runtimeInstance.Container()` as well as its scope. Measured at the site, `cli/command.go` closes the scope alone and the exit handler closes the container; the sentence was describing a teardown this major does not perform

- session: a session deleted while a request was still running cannot be written back. `Storage.Save` is a blind upsert, so a request that loaded the session before a logout deleted it re-created the entry when its own handler finished — with the identity intact and the cookie re-issued — and the window is as long as a request takes, which is long enough for someone holding a stolen cookie to keep a revoked session alive by repeating a slow one. The manager remembers a deleted id for `MELODY_HTTP_SESSION_TOMBSTONE_RETENTION` and refuses the write with `ErrSessionDeleted`. The check and the write are ONE critical section: testing the record and then writing outside the lock leaves the same race in miniature. The boundary is stated where the guarantee is: the record lives in the manager's own memory, per process, so a shared storage does not carry it between instances
- session: `Session.Clear` ends the session and the ending latches. `Session.Set` lifts the cleared flag, so a logout handler that cleared the session and was followed by anything writing to the same object — a middleware, an event listener leaving a farewell message — had the response path take the save branch instead of the delete branch: the values were overwritten but the pre-logout id stayed alive in the storage and was re-issued to the browser under the same cookie. `RegenerateSession` clears the session it rotated away through the same latch
- session: the response path and `Manager.SaveSession` decide a session's fate from one atomic `Snapshot` — values, modified flag and cleared flag under a single lock acquisition. Read through the individual accessors, a concurrent `Clear` landing between the reads had the cookie decision contradict the persistence branch taken above it, and the save branch write the emptied map — or the full pre-logout map — under an id the caller was just told is cleared
- session: `Manager.SaveSession` refuses a typed nil session through the interface instead of dereferencing it. A typed nil — the zero value of a `*Session` variable a caller left unassigned — is not equal to `nil` once carried in the interface, so a bare comparison let it through and the read that followed panicked; on the response path that panic lands inside the recovery defer, where a second panic escapes `ServeHttp` with no response at all
- session: `Manager.SaveSession` holds the session id to the standard the load and delete paths hold it to. An id those two refuse stored an entry `Manager.Session` can never read back and `DeleteSession` can never remove: the save reported success, the entry sat in the storage until the process ended, and the clear path then logged a delete failure the manager itself had manufactured
- session: `NewManager` refuses a negative ttl. Carried into the storages, `0 < ttl` is false for it and the entry is stored with no expiry at all — a lifetime that reads as "already lapsed" produced the immortal session instead, the exact opposite of what it asks for, and silently
- session: `NewManager` and its siblings refuse a positive ttl shorter than one second, the refusal the configuration door has always given for `MELODY_HTTP_SESSION_TTL`: below one second the value is not a short session but a broken one — the storage purges every lapsed entry on the write that stores the new one, so `SaveSession` reported success and persisted nothing
- session: `Session.All` returns a copy that reaches all the way down, the depth both storages already copy at, and `Session.Get` hands out a copy at that same depth. A copy of only the top level handed the caller the very map or slice a nested value holds, so mutating it changed the live session without passing through `Set` — `modified` stayed false, `SaveSession` skipped the write and reported success, and the mutation silently never persisted
- session: `InMemoryStorage` refuses `Load`, `Save`, `Delete` and `Clear` after `Close`, the way `FileStorage` already did. Serving a closed store is worse than the error: the cleanup goroutine is stopped by then, so an entry saved after `Close` is never reclaimed by anything but a `Load` that happens to name it, and the map grows for the rest of the process
- session: the instant a session expires counts as lapsed in `InMemoryStorage`, the boundary `FileStorage` already drew with `now >= ExpiresAt`. A session stored with a one second lifetime is gone exactly one second later in both storages, rather than living one instant longer in one of them — an application that moves between the two must not find the boundary moving with it
- session: `FileStorage.Load` does not let the failure of its own purging flush replace the answer it had already settled. The load's answer — no such session — is decided before the flush is attempted, and the flush is housekeeping no caller asked for; handed back instead, it made `Manager.Session` panic, so an expired cookie on a store that could not be written answered 500 where a client holding no cookie at all is served a fresh session
- session: the file storage's atomic save fsyncs the directory after the rename. Without it a power loss after a save could resurface the previous snapshot, silently logging out the user whose session had just been written — the temp file's own `Sync` covered its bytes, not its name
- session: `NewFileStorageFromPath` sweeps the `<name>.*.tmp` orphans a hard kill leaves between `CreateTemp` and the rename. Each one is a complete snapshot of every live session and its tokens that nothing ever opened again, so the surface only grew
- session: `NewFileStorageFromFile` refuses a handle opened with `O_APPEND`, naming it. An appending write ignores the offset, so every snapshot landed after the document it was replacing and the truncation then cut the pair to the new length: measured, a growing snapshot left the file readable and lost every save with no error on any path, and a shrinking one left a document the next boot refused to decode
- session: the manager's own refusals name the session through a one-way reference — a SHA-256 truncated to sixteen hex characters — rather than through the id itself. The refusal travels as an error a caller is free to log, and a raw session id in a log line is a credential a log reader can present as a cookie

- cache: the nil refusals of `Remember`, of `NewManager` and of the third-party boundaries read through the interface. A typed nil — a `(*Manager)(nil)` boxed into a non-nil interface — passed the plain comparison, was stored as a live collaborator and panicked on the first method call, on the request path, in place of the error the door promises
- cache: an error handed back by a callback, a backend or a serializer is normalized before it is judged, so a typed-nil error is read as the success it means. Under stampede protection that error was memoized and distributed to every waiter, and the first one to render it panicked on `Error()` with a nil receiver — one caller's misdeclared return type failing every request coalesced onto its key
- cache: a panic raised by the backend or the serializer on the leader's own reads and writes is reported as `cache remember cache access panicked` rather than being blamed on the callback, which its own wrapper recovers separately — the diagnosis used to be sent to a callback that never panicked. Both boundaries hand the panic value on as the CAUSE of the error they fabricate and capture the stack of the goroutine that raised it
- cache: `Set` and `SetMultiple` name the offending key in the context of a serialization failure, where the encoder's bare message travelled alone — asymmetric with the counter path, which named it already
- cache: `Manager.Many` leaves a corrupt entry out of the result the way an absent key is and returns the good values beside a `DeserializationError` naming the culprits in requested order. One corrupt entry used to discard the whole answer with an anonymous error whose message depended on map iteration order
- cache: a read refreshes an atomic access mark under the READ lock and moves its entry to the front of the recency list at most once per second per key. Taking the exclusive lock on every hit made each read a writer against a lock every other key shares, so a door the `RWMutex` advertises as a read had no read parallelism whatever; the eviction order is now right to within that interval rather than to the last read
- serializer: `PlainTextSerializer` copies on both directions — `Serialize([]byte)` returns a copy rather than the caller's backing array, where the string and default branches always allocated, and `Deserialize` into a `*[]byte` stores a copy rather than the payload slice itself. A caller returning a pooled buffer overwrote the bytes it believed it had snapshotted, and a target aliased to the read buffer changed under its owner on the next read
- serializer: a typed-nil deserialization target is refused with the same error the untyped nil gets, on both serializers. It passed the plain comparison, matched its case and dereferenced nil on the assignment line — a panic from a method that promises an error, while the json sibling answered the identical misuse correctly
- serializer: the two soft resolvers report their failure through `logging.LoggerFromRuntime` rather than the Must door, which panicked exactly when the runtime broken enough to lose the serializer had also lost the logger — inside the branch whose whole contract is to answer nil on failure
- clock: `ClockMustFromContainer` and `ClockMustFromResolver` refuse a nil or typed-nil argument by name. A nil used to dereference inside the container package, where the panic blamed the container instead of naming the argument this helper was handed
- clock: the `Ticker` and `Clock` contracts state their obligations in GoDoc, where they lived in a private comment implementors never see: `Stop` is mandatory and idempotent and must NOT close the channel, and the frozen clock's cadence is REAL wall time — `Advance` and `TravelTo` neither fire a tick nor suppress one. The witness is this repository's own: a test double closed the channel on `Stop`, surviving only through the order of a deferred call
- runtime: `runtime.New` and the runtime resolvers judge their arguments through the interface. A typed-nil scope was preferred over a healthy container, so `selectRuntimeResolver` bypassed the resolver that worked and turned the promised may-not-be-nil error into a panic inside the resolution, on the request path
- documentation: `CACHE.md`, `SERIALIZER.md` and `CLOCK.md` state what this major now does — the shared key grammar and the order of its refusals, the ownership of a backend, the typed miss and the self-healing read, the coalescing unit, the wait and the context that ends it, the `q=0` refusal and the strict qvalue grammar, and the nil refusals that name their argument. `CACHE.md` also stops declaring the registration helper's options as `...any`: the signature it documents takes `...containercontract.RegisterOption`, and has on every major
- documentation: `README.md` states the back-port rule the frozen majors actually follow — v1 and v2 receive any defect fix that fits a patch release plus security fixes, until v4 is released — where it limited them to security and critical correctness fixes, narrower than the rule applied

### Security

- security: the access-control listener no longer serves a request an entry point or access-denied handler refused with a typed-nil response. Both are application-supplied contracts that return the response interface, and a typed nil passed the plain `nil !=` check as a live response; `SetResponse` then normalized it to nil and the kernel read that as "no decision" and ran the handler — an unauthenticated request served, or a denied one served. Both are read through `IsNilInterface` now, the discipline the token path already keeps
- http: the rate-limit request listener no longer serves a refused request unmetered. `OnLimitExceeded` is application-supplied, and a typed-nil response from it passed the plain check the same way the access-control listener's did, so the 429 fallback never ran and the over-budget request reached the handler; it is read through `IsNilInterface` now, matching the middleware twin
- security: `NewAccessControlRegexRule` refuses `PUBLIC_ACCESS` on a pattern not anchored to the path start. An unanchored public pattern matches as a substring, so `/status` granted `/admin/status-board` — a protected route reached through a public rule, and the first-registered regex wins — the same over-open the raw-prefix constructor already refuses `PUBLIC_ACCESS` for; a start-anchored pattern cannot reach into the middle of a protected path and stays allowed
- config: `MarkSecret` no longer leaves a value assembled through a parameter's `kernel.*` alias unredacted. The post-resolve propagation matched templates by the marked name alone, so a dsn built from a secret read through the other spelling of the same kernel-aliased parameter printed in full beside the redacted key; the propagation now follows every alias the parameter answers to
- security: `NewHmacTokenSource` refuses a negative `MaxFutureExpiry`. The check that caps how far ahead an envelope's expiry may sit is gated on `0 < maxFutureExpiry`, so a negative value — reachable from a config typo — behaved like the zero "unbounded" case and reopened the memory-pinning window the cap exists to close; it is refused at construction the way `JwtConfig` refuses a negative revocation skew
- security: `totp.Config.Period` is clamped. `base = at.Unix() / int64(Period)`, so a period at or above `1<<63` converted to a non-positive int64 and froze the counter at 0, verifying one secret-derived code at every instant forever; a period past `maxPeriod` falls back to the default, mirroring the skew clamp
- example: the login doors rotate the session id before writing the authenticated identity. Both of them — the json login handler and the security `LoginHandler` the firewall drives — wrote the identity onto the session the request arrived with, so a session id an attacker chose before authentication and planted in the victim's browser stayed valid once it carried the victim's identity, which is session fixation. `melodyhttp.RegenerateRequestSession` carries the values over under a fresh id and republishes the session on the request, so the identity is written to the id the response emits; the primitive had no production caller in this major until now
- example: the seeded passwords are bcrypt. `security.Sha256Hex` — an unsalted digest, which is what a reader of a showcase would have copied — is replaced by `HashPassword`/`MustHashPassword` (`bcrypt.GenerateFromPassword`) at seeding and in the two user handlers, and by `PasswordMatches` (`bcrypt.CompareHashAndPassword`, constant-time on its own) at authentication, where the digest was compared with `!=`. `UserService.AuthenticateByUsernameAndPasswordHash` becomes `AuthenticateByUsernameAndPassword` and takes the plaintext, since under a salted scheme the caller cannot pre-hash; a login for a username that does not exist now spends a full comparison against a fixed hash (`DummyPasswordMatch`) so its response time is not an existence oracle, and both write doors refuse a plaintext over bcrypt's 72-byte ceiling with a 400 instead of surfacing a hashing failure. The migration that creates the column already described it as holding a bcrypt digest. The seeded credentials — `user`/`user`, `editor`/`editor`, `admin`/`admin` — are unchanged, but the stored bytes differ on every boot, so nothing may compare seeded hashes across calls. **Operational note**: a database seeded before this change keeps the sha256 rows — `seedIfEmpty` is count-guarded — and every login refuses against them; recreate the example tables (`db:rollback` then `db:migrate`, seeds return at first resolution) and drop the example's redis cache keys, which hold the cached user entities. The narrower remedy, in the order the operational note of the frozen majors gives — supervisor, cache, tables — is to empty the user table alone so the count guard opens, drop the `melody-example-v3:cache:example-user*` keys, and restart the process: the rows come back as `$2a$` digests of sixty bytes where the old ones were sixty-four hexadecimal characters, which is how the two are told apart at a glance

- http: `RedirectResponse` — and `RedirectFound`/`RedirectMovedPermanently` through it — refuses a location that leaves the application: a scheme (`https:`, `mailto:`, `javascript:`), a scheme-relative `//host`, or a backslash, which several browsers fold to a slash while `net/url` does not. A location built from client input is exactly how an open redirect is minted, and the refusal — a panic naming the door to use — makes the unsafe composition fail loudly at the first probe instead of shipping. `RedirectExternalResponse` is the new unguarded form, whose name is the caller's assertion that the target is trusted. **Behavioural change** for a caller that passed an absolute target to `RedirectResponse`; it moves to the external door
- http: `ConfinedFileResponse` and `ConfinedAttachmentResponse` serve a file selected by a name a client may steer, confined to a root directory: an absolute or climbing name is refused rather than folded away, the joined path is resolved through every symlink and checked to still lie under the resolved root, and only a regular file is answered. It is the door `FileResponse`'s own warning told every caller to build; built once here, it cannot be built subtly wrong at every call site. `FileResponse` and `AttachmentResponse` keep their documented-unsafe contract, and `AttachmentResponse` now carries the warning in its own GoDoc instead of relying on the reader to visit its delegate
- http: the static entity tag is a truncated sha256 digest of its derivation, and discloses none of it. Spelled out, the tag told every anonymous client the file's modification instant to the nanosecond — deploy times, per-file build ordering — and, for embedded assets, the binary's build version string, on every response with caching enabled. The digest of the same size-and-instant (or size-and-build-version) derivation keeps every cache property, because equal inputs digest equal and different inputs digest different, at sixteen hex characters; the frozen majors keep the spelled-out form with the disclosure documented
- http: the static file server serves only regular files, and asks before opening. A FIFO placed in the public directory parked the request goroutine for as long as nobody wrote the other end — `os.Open` on a FIFO blocks by itself, so the refusal has to precede the open, and one request per probe pinned one goroutine forever — while a device node or a socket answered bytes that are not a file's. The mode is refused before the open and re-checked on the opened handle, which also closes the window in which a regular file is swapped for something else between the two looks
- security: the api-key comparison runs over fixed-width digests, so its duration no longer reports the key's length. `subtle.ConstantTimeCompare` is constant-time only over inputs of equal length and answers immediately on unequal ones, so both api-key doors — `ApiKeyHeaderAuthenticator` and `ApiKeyHeaderRule` — told a caller, through timing, when a guess had the right length, shrinking the search space to strings of one size. Both now hash the expected and the presented value with sha256 and compare the thirty-two-byte digests through one shared spelling, which preserves exactly the equality being asked and gives every comparison the same duration
- http: `DeleteCookie` honours the browser's prefix contracts, so the deletion of a `__Host-` or `__Secure-` cookie actually lands. The browser rejects any Set-Cookie for a `__Secure-` name that is not marked `Secure`, and for a `__Host-` name additionally one whose path is not `/` or that carries a domain — the deleting Set-Cookie included — so the expiring counterpart this helper wrote was dropped in silence and the cookie stayed. A `__Secure-` deletion now carries `Secure`; a `__Host-` deletion carries `Secure` and the one path the browser accepts, over whatever path the caller passed, because the passed path could only make the deletion not happen
- security: a firewall prefix written with a trailing slash also claims the bare spelling of its route.
